Does 𝒫 ⁡ ( ω ) / fin \mathcal{P}(\omega)/\mathrm{fin} know its right hand from its left? Thanks: The author is supported by NSF grant DMS-2154229
Will Brian

Abstract
. Let σ \sigma denote the shift automorphism on 𝒫 ⁡ ( ω ) / fin \nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}} , defined by setting σ ⁡ ( [ A ] ) = [ A + 1 ] \sigma([A])=[A+1] for all A ⊆ ω A\subseteq\omega . We show that the Continuum Hypothesis implies the shift automorphism σ \sigma and its inverse σ − 1 \sigma^{-1} are conjugate in the automorphism group of 𝒫 ⁡ ( ω ) / fin \nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}} . Due to work of van Douwen and Shelah, it has been known since the 1980’s that it is consistent with 𝖹𝖥𝖢 \mathsf{ZFC} that σ \sigma and σ − 1 \sigma^{-1} are not conjugate. Our result shows that the question of whether σ \sigma and σ − 1 \sigma^{-1} are conjugate is independent of 𝖹𝖥𝖢 \mathsf{ZFC} . As a corollary to the main theorem, we deduce that the structures ⟨ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ω ) / fin , σ ⟩ \langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle and ⟨ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ω ) / fin , σ − 1 ⟩ \langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle are elementarily equivalent in the language of algebraic dynamical systems (Boolean algebras together with an automorphism), and their common theory is model complete. This corollary does not depend on the Continuum Hypothesis.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
03E35, 05C90, 06E25, 08A35, 37B99, 54D40

1.
Introduction

A
topological dynamical system
is a pair
\langle X,f\rangle
, where
X
is a compact Hausdorff space and
f:X\to X
is a homeomorphism. In the large and expanding literature on topological dynamical systems, sometimes more is required of
X
(such as metrizability), or less of
f
(perhaps it may be just a continuous surjection rather than a homeomorphism).
However the objects of this category are defined, the isomorphisms are defined as follows.
Two topological dynamical systems
\langle X,f\rangle
and
\langle Y,g\rangle
are
conjugate
if there is a homeomorphism
h:X\to Y
, called a
conjugacy
, such that
h\circ f=g\circ h
.

X
X
Y
Y
f
g
h
h

This is the natural notion of isomorphism in the category of topological dynamical systems.
The term “conjugate” (rather than
isomorphic
) arises from the case
X=Y
, where the above definition says
f
and
g
are conjugate elements of the autohomeomorphism group of
X
.
The main theorem of this paper can be phrased as a theorem of topological dynamics:

Main Theorem
.

The Continuum Hypothesis implies that the topological dynamical systems
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate.

In the statement of this theorem,
\omega^{*}
denotes the Stone-Čech remainder of the countable discrete space
\omega
of all finite ordinals, and
\sigma
denotes the shift homeomorphism of
\omega^{*}
, which maps an ultrafilter
u\in\omega^{*}
to the ultrafilter generated by
\left\{A+1\colon A\in u\right\}
.
Roughly speaking, this theorem states that, assuming the Continuum Hypothesis (henceforth abbreviated
\mathsf{CH}
), the maps
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
are indistinguishable from the topological point of view.

This theorem answers question 11 from Hart and van Mill’s “Problems on
\beta\mathbb{N}
”
[
18
]
.
(The question is also raised directly in
[
16
]
,
[
15
]
,
[
7
]
, and
[
9
]
.)
The roots of the question reach back to work of van Douwen and Shelah from the 1980’s. Near the end of his life, van Douwen considered the question of whether
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate. He proved that if in fact they are conjugate, then any map
h
witnessing their conjugacy cannot be a
trivial
homeomorphism (meaning a homeomorphism induced on
\omega^{*}
by a map
\omega\to\omega
, in the way that
\sigma
is induced by the successor map
n\mapsto n+1
). This work was published posthumously in
[
10
]
. Not long before van Douwen’s work on this question, Shelah proved in
[
26
]
that it is consistent with
\mathsf{ZFC}
that every homeomorphism
\omega^{*}\to\omega^{*}
is trivial. Combining these results of Shelah and van Douwen yields the following:

Theorem 1.1

(van Douwen and Shelah)
.

It is consistent with
\mathsf{ZFC}
that
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are not conjugate.

Later work of Shelah and Steprāns in
[
27
]
shows that “every autohomeomorphism of
\omega^{*}
is trivial” is not merely consistent with
\mathsf{ZFC}
, but is in fact a consequence of the
\mathsf{P}
roper
\mathsf{F}
orcing
\mathsf{A}
xiom, or
\mathsf{PFA}
. This was further improved by Veličković, who showed in
[
29
]
that “every autohomeomorphism of
\omega^{*}
is trivial” is already a consequence of
\mathsf{OCA+MA}
, a significant weakening of
\mathsf{PFA}
.
(Here
\mathsf{OCA}
denotes the
\mathsf{O}
pen
\mathsf{C}
oloring
\mathsf{A}
xiom defined by Todorčević in
[
28
]
. See
[
8
]
and
[
30
]
for further results in this direction.)
Thus
\mathsf{OCA+MA}
or
\mathsf{PFA}
implies
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\omega^{*},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are not conjugate.
There is no contradiction here with our main theorem, because
\mathsf{CH}
fails in Shelah’s model, and is contradicted by
\mathsf{PFA}
and
\mathsf{OCA+MA}
.
Combined with the work of van Douwen and Shelah, our result shows that the question of whether
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
are conjugate is independent of
\mathsf{ZFC}
.

An
algebraic dynamical system
is a pair
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
, where
\mathbb{A}
is a Boolean algebra and
\alpha
is an automorphism of
\mathbb{A}
. Algebraic dynamical systems have received less attention in the literature than their topological counterparts. However, the machinery of Stone duality reveals that the category of algebraic dynamical systems is naturally equivalent to the category of zero-dimensional topological dynamical systems.

It turns out that several aspects of the proof of our main theorem are stated more simply and proved more cleanly in the algebraic category rather than the topological one. For this reason, we do not say much about topological dynamical systems beyond this introduction, and do all of our work (or nearly all) with algebraic dynamical systems instead. Henceforth, a
dynamical system
(with no adjective) means an algebraic dynamical system.
It is likely helpful, but not strictly necessary, for the reader to be comfortable with the basics of Stone duality.

Boolean
algebras
zero-dimensional
compact
T_{2}
spaces
isomorphisms
of algebras
homeomorphisms
of spaces
embeddings
of algebras
continuous surjections
between spaces
\mathcal{P}(\omega)\text{ and }\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
\beta\omega\text{ and }\omega^{*}

The power set of
\omega
,
\mathcal{P}(\omega)
, when ordered by inclusion, is a complete Boolean algebra. The roles of “join” and “meet” in this algebra are played by the familiar set operations
\cup
and
\cap
, respectively.
The Boolean algebra
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}

is the quotient of the algebra
\mathcal{P}(\omega)
by the ideal of finite sets. For
A,B\subseteq\omega
, we write
A=^{*}B
to mean that
A
and
B
differ by finitely many elements, i.e.
\left\lvert(A\setminus B)\cup(B\setminus A)\right\rvert<\aleph_{0}
.
The members of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
are equivalence classes of the
=^{*}
relation, and we denote the equivalence class of
A\subseteq\omega
by
[A]
. The Boolean-algebraic relation
[A]\leq[B]
is denoted on the level of representatives by writing
A\subseteq^{*}B
: that is,
A\subseteq^{*}B
means that
[A]\leq[B]
, or (equivalently) that
B
contains all but finitely many members of
A
.
The
shift map
is the automorphism
\sigma
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
defined by setting

\sigma([A])=[A+1]=[\left\{a+1\colon a\in A\right\}]

for every
A\in\mathcal{P}(\omega)
. The inverse mapping is

\sigma^{-1}([A])=[A-1]=[\left\{a-1\colon a\in A\setminus\{0\}\right\}].

In other words,
\sigma
is the automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
that shifts every set one unit to the right, and
\sigma^{-1}
shifts every set one unit to the left.

Two dynamical systems
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
are
conjugate
if there is an isomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
such that
\phi\circ\alpha=\beta\circ\phi
.

\mathbb{B}
\mathbb{B}
\mathbb{A}
\mathbb{A}
\beta
\alpha
\phi
\phi

In other words, two (algebraic) dynamical systems are conjugate if and only if their Stone duals are conjugate as topological dynamical systems.
Weakening this notion, we say that
\eta
is an
embedding
of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
if
\eta
is a Boolean-algebraic embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
with the additional property that
\eta\circ\alpha=\beta\circ\eta
(i.e.,
\eta
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
onto a subsystem of
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
). In other words, an embedding of dynamical systems is like a conjugacy, but without requiring the connecting map to be surjective.
The Stone dual of this notion is called a
factor map
, or more rarely a
semi-conjugacy
or a
quotient map
, in the topological dynamics literature.

Our main theorem, stated in the algebraic category, says:

Main Theorem
.

\mathsf{CH}
implies
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate.

Several results related to van Douwen’s question and this theorem were known before the present paper:

\circ

As mentioned already, if there is a conjugacy
\phi
from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, then
\phi
is a non-trivial automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. Consequently, it is consistent that no such automorphism exists.
But also, it has been known since work of Rudin
[
25
]
that the Continuum Hypothesis implies the existence of many non-trivial automorphisms of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. In fact,
\mathsf{CH}
seems to be an optimal hypothesis for constructing non-trivial automorphisms (see
[
22
, Section 1.6]
).

\circ

If it is consistent with
\mathsf{ZFC}
that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate, then it is also consistent with
\mathsf{ZFC}+\mathsf{CH}
. This result is folklore. (A sketch of the proof: If
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate, then force with countable conditions to collapse the continuum. In the forcing extension,
\mathsf{CH}
is true, and because this notion of forcing is countably closed, the fact that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate is preserved in the extension.)

\circ

The existence of certain large cardinals implies that if “
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate” is true in some forcing extension, then it is true in every forcing extension satisfying
\mathsf{CH}
. This follows from Woodin’s celebrated
\Sigma^{2}_{1}
absoluteness theorem. Farah’s account in
[
13
]
contains a precise statement and proof of Woodin’s result.

\circ

The Continuum Hypothesis implies that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
embeds into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, and vice-versa. Like with conjugacies, the axiom
\mathsf{OCA+MA}
implies there are no embeddings from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
or vice-versa; thus the existence of such embeddings is independent of
\mathsf{ZFC}
. These results are found in
[
6
]
.

\circ

The conjugacy class of
\sigma
and the conjugacy class of
\sigma^{-1}
cannot be separated by a Borel set in
\mathcal{A}ut(\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}})
, the automorphism group of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
equipped with the topology of pointwise convergence. This is another sense in which
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
are topologically indistinguishable. This result is proved in
[
7
]
.

The first four of these five points suggest, and have suggested for some time, that if “
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate” is consistent with
\mathsf{ZFC}
, then it may be outright provable from
\mathsf{ZFC}+\mathsf{CH}
.
Indeed, this has turned out to be the case.
Further partial results concerning this problem, and a summary of its status as of 15 years ago, can be found in the survey of Geschke
[
16
]
.

The behavior of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
under
\mathsf{CH}
is usually considered to be well understood: see, e.g.,
[
22
, Section 1]
and
[
14
]
. Our main result here further reinforces this feeling, filling what was one of the few remaining gaps (as far as we are able to see) in our knowledge of how
\mathsf{CH}
affects
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
and its automorphisms.

Ultimately, our proof of the main theorem relies on a transfinite back-and-forth argument. This recursive argument needs to deal with
\mathfrak{c}
tasks in succession, but the recursive hypotheses cannot survive more than
\omega_{1}
stages. Thus the argument can only succeed if
\mathfrak{c}=\omega_{1}
, i.e., if
\mathsf{CH}
holds. This is in fact the only point in the proof where
\mathsf{CH}
is needed.

This back-and-forth argument is laid out in Section
6
.
The limit steps of the recursion are trivial – all the difficulty lies in the successor steps.
At successor steps, we wish to take a conjugacy between countable substructures of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
and extend it to a conjugacy between strictly larger substructures.
Furthermore, we must have at least some control over the growth of these substructures as the recursion progresses, in order to ensure that they cover
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
in
\omega_{1}
steps.

This problem at successor steps is isolated and distilled into a problem of infinite combinatorics that we call the
lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. What precisely constitutes an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and a solution to it, are defined in Section
3
.

We show in Section 3 that if every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
were solvable, then there would be a fairly straightforward path to proving our main theorem. However, we show in Section
5
that this is too much to hope for: the most general form of the lifting problem does not always have a solution.
To put it another way, the kind of task we face at every successor step of our recursive argument is not always doable.

In light of this, our recursive argument must be done very carefully, taking care to avoid running into unsolvable instances of the lifting problem at successor steps.
The key to this is to use elementary substructures, rather than arbitrary countable substructures, wherever it is possible to do so.

Instead of dealing with the lifting problem first, and then proving the main theorem, we present things in the reverse order.
Section
6
gives a contingent proof of the main theorem, relying on a key lemma that offers, under certain circumstances, a solution to the lifting problem (see Lemma
6.2
).
The remainder of the paper, except the last section, is devoted to proving this lemma.
Our proof of the lemma (and even its statement) uses some model-theoretic ideas, but the main part of the proof, the heart of the whole argument, is more combinatorial: a careful analysis of the finite directed graphs that, in a sense to be made precise in Section
7
, each capture some finite amount of information about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
This part of the argument is found in Sections
8
-
11
.

In the final section of the paper, Section
12
, we obtain several further applications of main theorem and the main lemma. Each of these answers a question that does not seem to appear in the literature, but has been part of the folklore surrounding dynamics in
\omega^{*}
for some time. First, we show (as a corollary to the main theorem) that the structures
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are elementarily equivalent. Unlike the main theorem, this corollary does not assume
\mathsf{CH}
.
Second, we show (as a corollary to the main lemma) that
\mathsf{CH}
implies any conjugacy between two countable elementary substructures of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
can be extended to a conjugacy from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to itself.
This implies for example that, ssuming
\mathsf{CH}
, the shift map is conjugate to a nontrivial automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and that there are nontrivial automorphisms commuting with the shift map, i.e., automorphisms
\phi
such that
\phi\circ\sigma=\sigma\circ\phi
, but
\phi\neq\sigma^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

I am especially grateful to Alan Dow, with whom I discussed several wrong ideas for proving this theorem over the years, and who patiently listened to one (finally) right idea over the course of several months in our seminar; and to Ilijas Farah, who provided invaluable feedback on an earlier draft of this paper, and to whom credit is due for several of the results in Section 12.

2.
A blueprint

This section examines the analogue of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, a problem of algebraic dynamics, in the simpler category of Boolean algebras.
This simplified version of the lifting problem was completely solved about 60 years ago by the work of Parovičenko
[
24
]
, Jónsson and Olin
[
20
]
, and Keisler
[
21
]
.
We review some of that work in this section.
We have two reasons for doing this.
First, it provides a way to introduce some preliminaries that are needed anyway later in the paper.
Second, the proof of our main theorem, in its broad strokes, is built from the blueprint laid out in this section.
However, obstacles are present in the dynamical category that do not appear in the simpler algebraic setting, and these obstacles force us to deviate from this pattern.
Before understanding our approach to the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, it is useful to understand the pattern we are building from, and to see both where and why it is necessary to abandon it.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a Boolean algebra. An
instance of the lifting problem
for
\mathbb{C}
is a
4
-tuple

(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B},\iota,\eta),

where
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
are countable Boolean algebras,
\iota
is an embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
, and
\eta
is an embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{C}
.
A
solution
to this instance of the lifting problem is an embedding
\bar{\eta}:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\bar{\eta}\circ\iota=\eta
.

\mathbb{A}
\mathbb{B}
\mathbb{C}
\iota
\eta
\bar{\eta}

In this situation we say that
\bar{\eta}
is a
lifting
of
\eta
from
\mathbb{A}
to
\mathbb{B}
.
☕

Equivalently, one may think of
\eta
as an isomorphism from
\mathbb{A}
onto some subalgebra
\mathbb{C}_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, and a lifting
\bar{\eta}
as an isomorphism from
\mathbb{B}
onto a larger subalgebra
\mathbb{C}_{1}\supseteq\mathbb{C}_{0}
such that
\bar{\eta}\circ\iota=\eta
.

\mathbb{A}
\mathbb{B}
\mathbb{C}_{1}
\mathbb{C}_{0}
\iota
\eta
\bar{\eta}

\subseteq

Theorem 2.2

(Parovičenko, 1963
[
24
]
)
.

Every instance of the lifting problem for
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
has a solution.

Let us note that
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
also has the (closely related) property of being
\aleph_{1}
-saturated. (We will say more on saturation in Section
12
below.)
This was proved by Keisler in
[
21
]
and independently, two years later, by Jónsson and Olin in
[
20
]
.
Let us consider some consequences of Theorem
2.2
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a Boolean algebra such that every instance of the lifting problem for
\mathbb{C}
has a solution.
Then every Boolean algebra of size
\leq\!\aleph_{1}
embeds in
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a Boolean algebra of size
\leq\!\aleph_{1}
, and write
\mathbb{A}
as the union of a length-
\omega_{1}
sequence
\left\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\colon\alpha<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of subalgebras, such that
\mathbb{A}_{0}=\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
, and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{A}_{\beta}
whenever
\alpha\leq\beta
, and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
for limit
\alpha<\omega_{1}
.

Using transfinite recursion, we now build a sequence
\left\langle\eta_{\alpha}\colon\alpha<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of maps such that each
\eta_{\alpha}
is an embedding
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}
, and the sequence is “coherent” in the sense that
\eta_{\alpha}=\eta_{\beta}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
whenever
\alpha\leq\beta
. The base step is trivial: there is an embedding
\eta_{0}:\mathbb{A}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
because
\mathbb{A}_{0}=\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
. At successor steps, observe that
(\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\iota,\eta_{\alpha})
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\mathbb{C}
, taking
\iota
to be the inclusion map
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}
.
By assumption, there is an embedding
\eta_{\alpha+1}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}\to\mathbb{C}
that extends
\eta_{\alpha}
.
At limit stages there is nothing to do: because
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
, we can (and must, to preserve coherence) define
\eta_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\eta_{\xi}
, which is well-defined because the
\eta_{\xi}
’s are coherent.
In the end,
\eta=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\eta_{\alpha}
is an embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{C}
.
∎

In particular, combining this with Parovičenko’s Theorem
2.2
, we see that every Boolean algebra of size
\leq\!\aleph_{1}
embeds in
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
are Boolean algebras of size
\aleph_{1}
, and every instance of the lifting problem for
\mathbb{C}
or for
\mathbb{D}
has a solution. Then
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{D}
.

Proof.

Let
\left\langle c_{\xi}\colon\xi<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
be an enumeration of
\mathbb{C}
, and let
\left\langle d_{\xi}\colon\xi<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
be an enumeration of
\mathbb{D}
.
Using transfinite recursion, we now build a sequence
\left\langle\phi_{\alpha}\colon\alpha<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of maps such that each
\phi_{\alpha}
is an isomorphism of its domain (which we denote
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}
) to its codomain (which we denote
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
); furthermore, we shall have
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\beta}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{\beta}
whenever
\alpha\leq\beta
,
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{C}_{\xi}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{D}_{\xi}
for limit
\alpha
, and the
\phi_{\alpha}
’s will be coherent, in the sense that
\phi_{\alpha}=\phi_{\beta}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}
whenever
\alpha\leq\beta
.
Given
X\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, let
\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle

denote the subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}
generated by
X
, and similarly for
\mathbb{D}
.

For the base case, let
\mathbb{C}_{0}=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{C}}\}
and
\mathbb{D}_{0}=\{\mathbf{0}_{\mathbb{D}},\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{D}}\}
, and let
\phi_{0}
denote the unique isomorphism
\mathbb{C}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
.
At limit stages there is nothing to do: take
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{C}_{\xi}
,
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{D}_{\xi}
, and
\phi_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\phi_{\xi}
, which is well-defined because the
\phi_{\xi}
are coherent.

At successor steps we obtain
\phi_{\alpha+1}
from
\phi_{\alpha}
by solving the lifting problem twice, once in the forwards direction and once in the backwards direction.
First, let
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}^{0}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\cup\{d_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
and observe that
(\mathbb{D}_{\alpha},\mathbb{D}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\iota,\phi_{\alpha}^{-1})
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\mathbb{C}
, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
.
Consequently, there is an isomorphism
\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1}
from
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
to some subalgebra
\mathbb{C}^{0}_{\alpha+1}
of
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{C}^{0}_{\alpha+1}\supseteq\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}
.
Second, let
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}^{0}\cup\{c_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
and observe that
(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}^{0},\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1},\iota,(\phi_{\alpha}^{0})^{-1})
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\mathbb{D}
, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}^{0}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}
.
Thus there is an isomorphism
\phi_{\alpha+1}
from
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}
to some subalgebra
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}
of
\mathbb{D}
with
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}\supseteq\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
.

\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}^{0}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}\cup\{d_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}
\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}
=\mathrm{Image}(\phi_{\alpha+1})
\mathrm{Image}(\phi_{\alpha+1}^{0})=
\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}^{0}\cup\{c_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle=

\subseteq

=

=

\subseteq

\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}
\phi_{\alpha+1}^{0}
(\phi_{\alpha+1}^{0})^{-1}
\phi_{\alpha+1}

This completes the recursion. Because the
\phi_{\alpha}
are coherent partial isomorphisms,
\phi=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\phi_{\alpha}
is an isomorphism from its domain
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}
to its image
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}
.
Furthermore,
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}=\mathbb{C}
, because we ensured at stage
\alpha+1
of the recursion that
c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+1}
, and
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{D}_{\alpha}=\mathbb{D}
, because we ensured at stage
\alpha+1
of the recursion that
d_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{D}_{\alpha+1}
.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

Assuming
\mathsf{CH}
,
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
is (up to isomorphism) the unique Boolean algebra of size
\aleph_{1}
for which every instance of the lifting problem has a solution.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorems
2.2
and
2.4
.
∎

Sometimes the name “Parovičenko’s theorem” refers to Corollary
2.5
, or sometimes to the following statement in the topological category:

Assuming
\mathsf{CH}
, every crowded zero-dimensional compact Hausdorff
F
-space of weight
\mathfrak{c}
in which nonempty
G_{\delta}
’s have nonempty interior is homeomorphic to
\omega^{*}
.

This is, more or less, the Stone dual of Corollary
2.5
.
Despite its topological flavor, this latter form of Parovičenko’s theorem is most naturally proved in the algebraic category (see, e.g.,
[
22
, Section 1.1]
).
A direct topological proof, including a topological analogue of Theorem
2.2
, can be found in
[
3
]
.

3.
Incompressibility

The results in the previous section suggest a natural approach to proving our main theorem, namely, by proceeding in the dynamical category via analogy with the Boolean-algebraic category:

(1)

First, show that every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
has a solution (analogous to Theorem
2.2
), once this lifting problem has been suitably defined.

(2)

Consequently, by an argument more or less identical to our proofs of Theorem
2.4
and Corollary
2.5
,
\mathsf{CH}
implies these two structures are isomorphic.

In this section, we turn our attention to the phrase “suitably defined” in
(1)
. To answer the question of how the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
should be defined, we need to look at the dynamical notion of incompressibility.

Definition 3.1
.

A dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is
compressible
if there is some
x\in\mathbb{A}\setminus\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
such that
\alpha(x)\leq x
.
Otherwise
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is
incompressible
.
☕

Theorem 3.2
.

\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are incompressible.

Proof.

This can be found in
[
5
, Theorem 5.3]
; but the proof is short and not difficult, so we reproduce it here.

Let
a\in\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
with
a\neq\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}
. In other words, let
a
be the equivalence class
[A]
of some
A\subseteq\omega
that is neither finite (since then
[A]=[\emptyset]=\mathbf{0}
) nor co-finite (since then
[A]=[\omega]=\mathbf{1}
). Because
A
is neither finite nor co-finite, the set
B=\left\{n\in A\colon n+1\notin A\right\}
is infinite. But then

\sigma([A])-[A]\,=\,[A+1]-[A]\,=\,[(A+1)\setminus A]\,=\,[B+1]\,\neq\,\mathbf{0},

which means that
\sigma(a)=\sigma([A])\not\leq[A]=a
.

A similar proof works for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
. Alternatively, one may observe that, in general,
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is compressible if and only if
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
is, because if
\alpha(x)\leq x
then
\alpha^{-1}(\mathbf{1}-x)\leq\mathbf{1}-x
.
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

If
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
embeds in an incompressible dynamical system, it is incompressible.

Proof.

Like the previous theorem, this is a known fact and can be found (in dual form) in
[
1
, Chapter 4]
or
[
5
, Section 5]
. But like the previous theorem, the proof is short and not difficult, so we reproduce it here.

Suppose
\eta
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into an incompressible dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, and let
x\in\mathbb{A}\setminus\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
. Then
\beta(\eta(x))\not\leq\eta(x)
(by the incompressibility of
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
), which implies
\eta(\alpha(x))\not\leq\eta(x)
(because
\eta\circ\alpha=\beta\circ\eta
), which implies
\alpha(x)\not\leq x
(because
\eta^{-1}
preserves inequalities). Thus
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is incompressible.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

Every dynamical system that can be embedded in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
or
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is incompressible.

For example, of the two dynamical systems on the
4
-element Boolean algebra, one of them embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and the other one does not.

\mathbf{0}
\mathbf{1}
\mathbf{0}
\mathbf{1}

In fact, the dynamical system illustrated on the left admits exactly two (essentially identical) embeddings into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
:
\mathbf{0}
must map to
[\emptyset]
and
\mathbf{1}
to
[\omega]
, and the two other elements of the algebra must map to
[\left\{2n\colon n\in\omega\right\}]
and
[\left\{2n+1\colon n\in\omega\right\}]
.

The most direct analogue of Definition
2.1
for dynamical systems is:

Definition 3.5
.

An
instance of the general lifting problem
for a dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
is a
4
-tuple

(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta),

where
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
are countable dynamical systems,
\iota
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, and
\eta
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
.

\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
\iota
\eta
\bar{\eta}

A
solution
to this instance of the general lifting problem is an embedding
\bar{\eta}
from
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
such that
\bar{\eta}\circ\iota=\eta
.
☕

However, allowing
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
to be any countable dynamical systems is too broad, because an analogue of Theorem
2.3
holds in this context:

Theorem 3.6
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
is a dynamical system such that every instance of the general lifting problem for
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
has a solution. Then every dynamical system of size
\leq\!\aleph_{1}
embeds in
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward adaptation of the proof of Theorem
2.3
.
∎

As we have already seen, not every countable dynamical system, or even every finite dynamical system, embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
; only incompressible systems do. This observation motivates our definition of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.

Definition 3.7
.

An
instance of the lifting problem
for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is a
4
-tuple

(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta),

where
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
are countable incompressible dynamical systems,
\iota
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, and
\eta
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.

\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
\iota
\eta
\bar{\eta}

A
solution
to this instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is an embedding
\bar{\eta}
from
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
such that
\bar{\eta}\circ\iota=\eta
.

An
instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is defined similarly, as are the notions of
solution
and
lifting
in that context.
☕

Once again, an analogue of Theorem
2.3
holds in this context:

Theorem 3.8
.

If every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
has a solution, then every incompressible dynamical system of size
\leq\!\aleph_{1}
embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.

Similarly, an analogue of Theorem
2.4
and Corollary
2.5
is also provable in this context. The proof is a straightforward adaptation of the proofs of Theorem
2.4
and Corollary
2.5
in the previous section.

Theorem 3.9
.

If every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
has a solution, then
\mathsf{CH}
implies
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate.

To complete step
(1)
in the strategy outlined at the beginning of this section, we should now prove that every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
has a solution. The paper’s main theorem then follows via Theorem
3.9
.

But alas, it turns out this is too much to hope for.
We prove in Section 5 below that not every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
has a solution.
This forces us to travel a more difficult road in order to reach a proof of the main theorem.

Interestingly, Theorems
3.8
and
3.9
are both vacuously true.
More precisely, both theorems assert conditional statements –
if
A
then
B
–
and in both cases,
A
is false while
B
is true nonetheless.
So while not every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
has a solution, the most important consequences of that statement hold anyway. There is a reason for this, as we shall see: all “sufficiently nice” instances of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
do have a solution, and this suffices to derive the conclusions of Theorems
3.8
and
3.9
, albeit by more nuanced arguments.

A rough outline of the next few sections is:

\lx@sectionsign
4
:

We develop some techniques for constructing embeddings of a countable dynamical system into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
or
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.

\lx@sectionsign
5
:

We prove that not every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
has a solution. In other words, we show the hypotheses of Theorems
3.8
and
3.9
do not hold.

\lx@sectionsign
6
:

We show that, nevertheless, if every “sufficiently nice” instance of the lifting problem has a solution, then
\mathsf{CH}
implies
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate, via a modification of the back-and-forth argument used for Theorem
2.4
.

In other words, the strategy outlined at the beginning of this section fails in step
(1)
, because the natural analogue of Theorem
2.2
for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is not true. But we can rescue the strategy by only considering only “sufficiently nice” instances of the lifting problem.

The fact that “sufficiently nice” instances of the lifting problem are solvable is not proved in Section 6, but in Sections 7-11. The main content of Section 6 is to define precisely what “sufficiently nice” means, and to prove that being able to solve only these instances of the lifting problem still suffices to imply the main theorem via a transfinite back-and-forth argument similar to the one in the previous section.

The conclusion of Theorem
3.8
, that every weakly incompressible dynamical system of size
\leq\!\aleph_{1}
embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, is the main result of
[
6
]
.
But this result is also a relatively straightforward consequence of some of the techniques used in this paper, and we include a proof of it at the end of Section
8
below.

4.
Embeddings of Boolean algebras and dynamical systems

Recall that a dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
embeds in another dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
if there is a Boolean-algebraic embedding
\eta:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
such that
\eta\circ\alpha=\beta\circ\eta
.
In other words, an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
onto a subsystem of
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
.

In this section, we develop some techniques for constructing embeddings of dynamical systems into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
. Ultimately, the goal is to show that every embedding of a countable dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
or
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is completely described by a particular kind of sequence in
\mathbb{A}
(see Theorem
4.10
).

In what follows, we have no need to consider infinite partitions of unity in a Boolean algebra, but will work quite a lot with finite partitions. To avoid repeating the word “finite” many times, we will use the word “partition” to always mean what is usually meant by “finite partition.”

Definition 4.1
.

A
partition of unity
, or simply a
partition
, in a Boolean algebra
\mathbb{A}
is a finite set
\mathcal{U}
of nonzero members of
\mathbb{A}
such that
\bigvee\mathcal{U}=1\hskip-4.97922pt1
and
u\wedge v=0\hskip-5.97508pt0
for all
u,v\in\mathcal{U}
.

Given a partition
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathbb{A}
and some
a\in\mathbb{A}
, we say that
a
is
below

\mathcal{U}
if
a\leq u
for some
u\in\mathcal{U}
.
If
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are two partitions of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathcal{V}

refines

\mathcal{U}
if every member of
\mathcal{V}
is below
\mathcal{U}
. The
natural map
from
\mathcal{V}
to
\mathcal{U}
sends each
v\in\mathcal{V}
to the unique
u\in\mathcal{U}
with
v\leq u
.

A sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
is
eventually small
if for every partition
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathbb{A}
, there is some
N\in\omega
such that
a_{n}
is below
\mathcal{U}
for all
n\geq N
.

A sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
is
eventually dense
if for every
x\in\mathbb{A}
, there are infinitely many
n\in\omega
such that
a_{n}\leq x
.
☕

Theorem 4.2
.

Suppose
\mathbb{A}
is a Boolean algebra, and
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an eventually small, eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{A}\setminus\{0\hskip-5.97508pt0\}
.
Then the mapping

a\,\mapsto\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]

is an embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an eventually small, eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{A}\setminus\{0\hskip-5.97508pt0\}
, and define
\eta:\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
by setting
\eta(a)=[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]
.

The definition of
\eta
readily implies
\eta(\mathbf{0})=[\emptyset]
and
\eta(\mathbf{1})=[\omega]
.
Now suppose
a,b\in\mathbb{A}
. Let
\mathcal{U}=\{a-b,b-a,a\wedge b,\mathbf{1}-(a\vee b)\}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
(the partition of
\mathbb{A}
generated by
a
and
b
).
Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually small, there is some
N\in\omega
such that if
n\geq N
then
a_{n}
is below
\mathcal{U}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\vee b\right\}=^{*}

\displaystyle\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a-b\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq b-a\right\}\cup\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\wedge b\right\}

\displaystyle=\,\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq b\right\}

which means that

\displaystyle\eta(a\vee b)

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\vee b\right\}]\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}\cup\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq b\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]\vee[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq b\right\}]\,=\,\eta(a)\vee\eta(b).

A similar computation shows
\eta(a\wedge b)=\eta(a)\wedge\eta(b)
and
\eta(\mathbf{1}-a)=\mathbf{1}-\eta(a)
.

Thus
\eta
is a homomorphism
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. To check that it is injective, suppose
a\in\mathbb{A}
and
a\neq 0\hskip-5.97508pt0
.
Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually dense,

\left\{n\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}
is infinite, which means
\eta(a)=[\left\{n\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]\neq[\emptyset]
.
In other words, the kernel of the homomorphism
\eta
is
\{0\hskip-5.97508pt0\}
, and this implies
\eta
is injective.
∎

Definition 4.3
.

Suppose
\mathbb{A}
is a Boolean algebra, and
\vec{s}=\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an eventually small, eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{A}\setminus\{0\hskip-5.97508pt0\}
.
The mapping

a\,\mapsto\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]

is called the embedding
induced
by
\vec{s}
.
If
\eta:\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
is an embedding, we say that
\eta
is
induced
if there is a sequence
\vec{s}
in
\mathbb{A}
that induces it.
☕

Observe that if
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{A}
, then taking
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon a_{n}\leq x\right\}
gives a countable family of filters whose union is
\mathbb{A}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
; hence
\mathbb{A}
is
\sigma
-centered.
Contrapositively, if
\mathbb{A}
is not
\sigma
-centered, then there is no eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{A}
, and no embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
can be induced.
For example, there are no induced mappings
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
in the sense of Definition
4.3
.
In contrast to this, the following theorem (Theorem
4.5
) shows that if
\mathbb{A}
is a countable Boolean algebra, then every embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
is induced.

Theorem
4.5
is well known. Its Stone dual states that
\omega^{*}
continuously surjects onto every compact zero-dimensional metric space
X
, and every such surjection can be continuously extended to a map
\beta\omega\to X
. We include a proof (in the algebraic category) for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 4.4
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is a countable Boolean algebra, then there is a sequence
\left\langle\mathcal{U}_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
of partitions of
\mathbb{A}
such that
\mathcal{U}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{U}_{m}
whenever
m\leq n
, and for every partition
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathcal{U}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{V}
for all sufficiently large
n
.

Proof.

Let
\left\langle a_{k}\colon k\in\omega\right\rangle
be an enumeration of
\mathbb{A}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, and for each
n\in\omega
let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
be the partition of
\mathbb{A}
generated by
\left\{a_{k}\colon k\leq n\right\}
.
∎

Theorem 4.5
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is a countable Boolean algebra, then

(1)

there is an embedding of
\mathbb{A}
into
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and

(2)

every embedding of
\mathbb{A}
into
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
is induced.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a countable Boolean algebra. Fix a sequence
\mathcal{U}_{0},\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2},\dots
of partitions of
\mathbb{A}
with the properties stated in Lemma
4.4
.

For
(1)
, let
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
be a sequence of members of
\mathbb{A}
obtained by simply listing the (finitely many) members of
\mathcal{U}_{0}
, then listing the members of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
, then
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, etc. This sequence is both eventually small and eventually dense, so it induces an embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
by Theorem
4.2
.

For
(2)
, suppose
\eta:\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
is an embedding.
For each
a\in\mathbb{A}
fix a representative
X_{a}
of the equivalence class
\eta(a)
: that is,
X_{a}\subseteq\omega
and
\eta(a)=[X_{a}]
.
For convenience, define
n_{-1}=0
, and then, for each
k
, define
n_{k}
to be some natural number large enough that:

(1)

n_{k}>n_{k-1}
.

(2)

For each
a\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
,
X_{a}\cap[n_{k-1},n_{k})\neq\emptyset
.

(3)

If
a\in\mathbb{A}
and
a=\bigvee\left\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{k}\colon u\leq a\right\}
, then

\left\{X_{u}\setminus n_{k}\colon u\in\mathcal{U}_{k+1}\text{ and }u\leq a\right\}

is a partition of
X_{a}\setminus n_{k}
.

It is clear that all sufficiently large
n_{k}
satisfy
(1)
and
(2)
. For condition
(3)
, let
A=\left\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{k+1}\colon u\leq a\right\}
, suppose
a=\bigvee A
,
and recall that, in
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
,

[X_{u}]\wedge[X_{u^{\prime}}]=\eta(u)\wedge\eta(u^{\prime})=\eta(u\wedge u^{\prime})=\eta(0\hskip-5.97508pt0)=[\emptyset]

for all
u,u^{\prime}\in A
with
u\neq u^{\prime}
. In other words, the sets in
\left\{X_{u}\colon u\in A\right\}
are “pairwise almost disjoint” in the sense that
\left\{X_{u}\setminus n\colon u\in A\right\}
are pairwise disjoint above
N
for large enough
N
. Similarly,
[X_{a}]=\bigvee_{u\in A}[X_{u}]
in
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and this implies
\bigcup\left\{X_{u}\setminus N\colon u\in A\right\}=X_{a}\setminus N
for large enough
N
.

This describes the
n_{k}
. Next, for each
n\in\omega
, define

a_{n}=a\qquad\text{ if and only if }\qquad n\in[n_{k-1},n_{k}),\,a\in\mathcal{U}_{k},\text{ and }n\in X_{a}.

We claim that this sequence is eventually small and eventually dense, and that it induces the embedding
\eta:\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.

The sequence is eventually small because for every partition
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathcal{U}_{k}
refines
\mathcal{V}
for all sufficiently large
k
, which implies (by the definition of the
a_{n}
) that
a_{n}
is below
\mathcal{V}
for all sufficiently large
n
.

To see that
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually dense, fix some
a\in\mathbb{A}\setminus\{0\hskip-5.97508pt0\}
. There is some
K\in\omega
such that
\mathcal{U}_{k}
refines
\{a,\mathbf{1}-a\}
whenever
k\geq K
. But then condition
(2)
of our choice of the
n_{k}
implies that in every interval
[n_{k-1},n_{k})
, there is some
n
such that
a_{n}\leq a
. Hence
\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}
is infinite.

Finally, we must show that
\eta
is induced by
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
and fix
a\in\mathbb{A}
. By our choice of the
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, there is some
K\in\omega
such that
\mathcal{U}_{k}
refines
\{a,\mathbf{1}-a\}
whenever
k\geq K
, which implies

a=\bigvee\left\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{k}\colon u\leq a\right\}
for all
k\geq K
.
By part
(3)
of our choice of the
n_{k}
, and by the definition of the
a_{n}
, this implies that if
n\geq n_{K}
, then
a_{n}\leq a
if and only if
n\in X_{a}
.
Thus

\eta^{\prime}(a)=[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]=[X_{a}\setminus n_{K}]=[X_{a}]=\eta(a).

As
a
was arbitrary, this shows
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a Boolean algebra. If
\mathcal{U}
is a partition of
\mathbb{A}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{A}
, we write
a\approx_{\mathcal{U}}b
to mean that
a\wedge u\neq 0\hskip-5.97508pt0
if and only if
b\wedge u\neq 0\hskip-5.97508pt0
for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
.
Define
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx_{\mathcal{U}}^{*}\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
to mean that
a_{n}\approx_{\mathcal{U}}b_{n}
for all sufficiently large
n
.
Two sequences
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
and
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
are
eventually close
if
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx_{\mathcal{U}}^{*}\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle

for every partition
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathbb{A}
.
This is denoted
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
☕

Theorem 4.7
.

Suppose
\mathbb{A}
is a Boolean algebra, and that
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
and
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
are eventually small, eventually dense sequences in
\mathbb{A}
. These sequences induce the same embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
if and only if they are eventually close.

Proof.

For the “if” direction, fix two eventually small, eventually dense sequences
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
,
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
with
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
Fix some
a\in\mathbb{A}
, and let
\mathcal{U}=\{a,1\hskip-4.97922pt1-a\}
.
Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
and
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
are eventually small,
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
are below
\mathcal{U}
for all sufficiently large
n
. But also
a_{n}\approx_{\mathcal{U}}b_{n}
for sufficiently large
n
.
Combining these two things, if
n
is sufficiently large then
either
a_{n},b_{n}\leq a
or else
a_{n},b_{n}\leq\mathbf{1}-a
.
Therefore
\left\{n\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}=^{*}\left\{n\colon b_{n}\leq a\right\}
, which means the embedding induced by
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
and the embedding induced by
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
both map
a
to the same member of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, namely
[\left\{n\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]=[\left\{n\colon b_{n}\leq a\right\}]
.
As
a
was arbitrary, these two sequences induce the same embedding.

For the “only if” direction, fix two eventually small, eventually dense sequences
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
,
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
that induce the same embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
.
Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
and
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
are eventually small,
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
are below
\mathcal{U}
for sufficiently large
n
.
In particular,
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
are each below exactly one member of
\mathcal{U}
for all sufficiently large
n
.
For any particular
u\in\mathcal{U}
,

\left\{n\colon a_{n}\leq u\right\}\,=^{*}\,\left\{n\colon b_{n}\leq u\right\}

(because the sequences induce the same embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
).
As this is true for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
are each below the same member of
\mathcal{U}
for sufficiently large
n
. Consequently,
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx_{\mathcal{U}}\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
∎

Theorem 4.8
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is a dynamical system, and
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an eventually small, eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{A}
.
Let
\eta
denote the embedding of
\mathbb{A}
into
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
that is induced by this sequence.
Then
\eta\circ\alpha=\sigma\circ\eta
if and only if
\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

In other words, this theorem states a criterion for determining precisely when a Boolean-algebraic embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by some sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is also an embedding of dynamical systems from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
: namely, this holds if and only if
\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
\eta
denote the embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
. Observe that

\sigma^{-1}\circ\eta(a)=\sigma^{-1}([\left\{n\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}])=[\left\{n-1\colon a_{n}\leq a\right\}]=[\left\{n\colon a_{n+1}\leq a\right\}]

for every
a
, which means that
\sigma^{-1}\circ\eta
is the embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by the sequence
\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
Likewise,

\eta\circ\alpha^{-1}(a)=[\left\{n\colon a_{n}\leq\alpha^{-1}(a)\right\}]=[\left\{n\colon\alpha(a_{n})\leq a\right\}]

for every
a
, which means that
\eta\circ\alpha^{-1}
is the embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by the sequence
\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

By Theorem
4.7
, this implies that
\eta
is an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
if and only if
\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
This proves the theorem, because
\eta
is an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
if and only if it is also an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. (This is because
\eta\circ\alpha^{-1}=\sigma^{-1}\circ\eta
implies
\sigma\circ\eta=\sigma\circ(\eta\circ\alpha^{-1})\circ\alpha=\sigma\circ(\sigma^{-1}\circ\eta)\circ\alpha=\eta\circ\alpha
, and similarly
\sigma\circ\eta=\eta\circ\alpha
implies
\eta\circ\alpha^{-1}=\sigma^{-1}\circ\eta
.)
∎

Of course, by suitably modifying the proof of this theorem, one can obtain a similar result for
\sigma^{-1}
:

Theorem 4.9
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is a dynamical system, and
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an eventually small, eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{A}
.
Let
\eta
denote the embedding of
\mathbb{A}
into
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
that is induced by this sequence.
Then
\eta\circ\alpha=\sigma^{-1}\circ\eta
if and only if
\left\langle a_{n-1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
(where we set
a_{-1}=\mathbf{1}
in order to make the sequence on the left well-defined).

Alternatively, one could prove this directly from Theorem
4.8
using the general fact that
\eta
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
if and only if
\eta
is also an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta^{-1}\rangle
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is a countable dynamical system and
\eta
is a Boolean-algebraic embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

The map
\eta
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.

(2)

The map
\eta
is induced by an eventually small, eventually dense sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
such that
\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

(3)

The map
\eta
is induced by an eventually small, eventually dense sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
such that
\alpha(a_{n})\wedge a_{n+1}\neq 0\hskip-5.97508pt0
for all
n\in\omega
.

Proof.

We’ve proved already that
(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)
: that
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
follows from Theorem
4.8
, and that
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
follows from Theorem
4.8
together with Theorem
4.5
(2).
It remains to prove
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
.

To see that
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
, suppose
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
satisfies condition
(3)
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
.
Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually small,
a_{n+1}
and
\alpha(a_{n})
are both below
\mathcal{U}
for sufficiently large
n
.
Because
\alpha(a_{n})\wedge a_{n+1}\neq\mathbf{0}
, this implies
a_{n+1}
and
\alpha(a_{n})
are below the same member of
\mathcal{U}
for sufficiently large
n
.
Hence
\alpha(a_{n})\approx_{\mathcal{U}}^{*}a_{n+1}
.
As
\mathcal{U}
was arbitrary, this shows that
\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

To see that
(2)\Rightarrow(3)
, suppose
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
satisfies condition
(2)
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{0},\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2},\dots
be a sequence of partitions of
\mathbb{A}
with the properties stated in Lemma
4.4
.
For every
k\in\omega
, let
\mathcal{V}_{k}
be the partition of
\mathbb{A}
generated by the (finite) set
\mathcal{U}_{k}\cup\left\{\alpha^{-1}(u)\colon u\in\mathcal{U}_{k}\right\}
.
Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually small, for every
k\in\omega
there is some
N_{k}\in\omega
such that
a_{n}
is below
\mathcal{V}_{k}
for all
n\geq N_{k}
.
By our choice of
\mathcal{V}_{k}
, this implies that both
\alpha(a_{n})
and
a_{n+1}
are below
\mathcal{U}_{k}
whenever
n\geq N_{k}
.
Furthermore, because
\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
,
\alpha(a_{n})
and
a_{n+1}
are below the same member of
\mathcal{U}_{k}
whenever
n\geq N_{k}
.

Define a new sequence
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
by setting

b_{n}
equal to the unique
u\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
with
a_{n}\leq u
whenever
n\in[N_{k},N_{k+1})
, and
b_{n}=\mathbf{1}
whenever
n<N_{0}
.
By the conclusion of the previous paragraph, this new sequence satisfies the condition in
(3)
.
∎

Just as with Theorems
4.8
and
4.9
, one can obtain a version of Theorem
4.10
for
\sigma^{-1}
. This can be done by suitably modifying the proof above, or by using Theorem
4.10
directly, together with the fact that
\eta
is an embedding
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle\to\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
if and only if
\eta
is an embedding
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle\to\langle\mathbb{B},\beta^{-1}\rangle
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is a countable algebraic dynamical system, and let
\eta:\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

The map
\eta
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.

(2)

The map
\eta
is induced by an eventually small, eventually dense sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
such that
\left\langle a_{n-1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

(3)

The map
\eta
is induced by an eventually small, eventually dense sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
such that
a_{n-1}\wedge\alpha(a_{n})\neq 0\hskip-5.97508pt0
for all
n\in\omega
.

5.
A false hope cruelly dashed

The goal of this section is to show, as promised at the end of Section
3
, that not every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
or for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
has a solution.

This counterexample motivates a good deal of what follows, such as the definition of “incompatibility” and “polarization” in Section 9. Understanding the example thoroughly may help to guide one’s intuition through the proof of the main theorem. That being said, this counterexample is not strictly necessary for understanding the proof of the main theorem, and this section can be skipped by the reader who is feeling impatient about getting to the proof of the main theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

There is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
that has no solution.

Proof.

We begin by describing a particular instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, which, we recall, consists of two countable incompressible dynamical systems
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, an embedding
\iota
from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, and an embedding
\eta
from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. We begin by describing the Stone duals of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
.

The topological space
X
(whose Stone dual gives us
\mathbb{A}
) is a
3
-point compactification of
4
copies of
\mathbb{Z}
:

X\,=\,\big(\{0,1,2,3\}\times\mathbb{Z}\big)\cup\{a,b,c\}

where points of the form
(i,n)
are discrete,
the basic neighborhoods of
a
have the form
\{a\}\cup\{0,1\}\times(-\infty,n]
,
the basic neighborhoods of
c
have the form
\{c\}\cup\{2,3\}\times[n,\infty)
,
and the basic neighborhoods of
b
have the form
\{b\}\cup\{0,1\}\times[n,\infty)\cup\{2,3\}\times(-\infty,n]
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
a
b
c
\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}
\{1\}\times\mathbb{Z}
\{2\}\times\mathbb{Z}
\{3\}\times\mathbb{Z}
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.
.

.
.
.
.
.

). This is well-defined, because homeomorphisms map clopen sets to clopen sets, and it is an automorphism of

. So
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is an algebraic dynamical system.

The topological space

(whose Stone dual gives us
\mathbb{B}
) is a

Define

by setting

. This is an automorphism of
\mathbb{B}
, so
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
is an algebraic dynamical system.

Note that
X
and
Y
have been defined so that
X\subseteq Y
, and
f=g\!\restriction\!X
. Define a projection map
\pi:Y\to X
by setting
\pi(x)=x
for all
x\in X
, and otherwise set

\pi(d)=b,\qquad\pi(4,n)=(3,-n),\qquad\pi(5,n)=(2,-n).

Roughly speaking,
\pi
takes the rightmost third of
Y
from our picture, and rotates it around the point
c
precisely one half turn, until it sits on top of the middle third. We then define
\iota(A)=\pi^{-1}(A)
for all
A\in\mathbb{A}
. This is a Boolean-algebraic embedding (the Stone dual of
\pi
). On the topological side, we have
\pi\circ g=f\circ\pi
, and it follows that
\iota\circ\alpha=\beta\circ\iota
on the algebraic side. Hence
\iota
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
.

To finish our description of the counterexample, we must define an embedding
\eta
from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
We do this by describing an eventually small, eventually dense sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
of members of
\mathbb{A}
such that
\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

The sequence is chosen so that each
a_{n}
is a clopen singleton in
X
. The pattern of the sequence is depicted below. Roughly, the idea is to follow the action of
\alpha
for increasingly long stretches along each of the
4
copies of
\mathbb{Z}
in
X
, jumping to the next stretch at some prescribed time.

More precisely, define the first
4
elements of the sequence as:

a_{0}=\{(0,0)\},\quad a_{1}=\{(2,0)\},\quad a_{2}=\{(3,0)\},\quad a_{3}=\{(1,0)\}.

Then define the next
12
elements of the sequence by taking

a_{5}=a_{0},\quad a_{8}=a_{1},\quad a_{11}=a_{2},\quad a_{14}=a_{3},

and then defining
a_{i-1}=\alpha^{-1}(a_{i})
and
a_{i+1}=\alpha(a_{i})
for
i=5,8,11,14
.
Then define the next
20
members of the sequence similarly:

a_{18}=a_{0},\quad a_{23}=a_{1},\quad a_{28}=a_{2},\quad a_{33}=a_{3},

and
a_{i+j}=\alpha^{j}(a_{i})
for
i=18,23,28,33
and
j=\pm 1,\pm 2
.
The remainder of the sequence is defined similarly.

This sequence is eventually small. In fact, every member of this sequence is an atom of
\mathbb{A}
, and so every member of this sequence is below every partition of
\mathbb{A}
.

This sequence is eventually dense. Indeed, note that for any isolated
p\in X
, there are infinitely many
n
such that
a_{n}=\{p\}
. If
A\in\mathbb{A}
, then there is some isolated
p\in X
with
\{p\}\subseteq A
, and therefore we have infinitely many
n
with
a_{n}=\{p\}\subseteq A
.

Finally,
\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
. To see this, let
\mathcal{U}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
.
For many values of
n
, we defined
a_{n+1}=\alpha(a_{n})
, and for these values of
n
we have
a_{n+1}\approx_{\mathcal{U}}\alpha(a_{n})
.
For the other values of
n
, either

\circ

a_{n}=\{(0,k)\}
and
a_{n+1}=\{(2,-k)\}
, or

\circ

a_{n}=\{(2,k)\}
and
a_{n+1}=\{(3,k)\}
, or

\circ

a_{n}=\{(3,-k)\}
and
a_{n+1}=\{(1,k)\}
, or

\circ

a_{n}=\{(1,-k)\}
and
a_{n+1}=\{(0,-k-1)\}
.

The partition
\mathcal{U}
consists of finitely many clopen subsets of
X
, including some
A\ni a
,
B\ni b
, and
C\ni c
. For large enough values of
n
, the corresponding
k
-values in the bulleted list above will also be large, large enough that
(0,k)
and
(2,-k)
are both in
B
, and
(2,k)
and
(3,k)
are both in
C
, etc. Thus for large enough
n
, we still have
a_{n+1}\approx_{\mathcal{U}}\alpha(a_{n})
even when we do not have
a_{n+1}=\alpha(a_{n})
. Because this is true for any partition
\mathcal{U}
, this shows that
\left\langle\alpha(a_{n})\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle a_{n+1}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
as claimed.

By Theorem
4.8
, the sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
induces an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. Let
\eta
denote this embedding.

This completes our description of the counterexample:
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. It remains to prove that this instance of the lifting problem has no solution.

Suppose, aiming for a contradiction, that
\bar{\eta}
is a lifting of
\eta
to
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, so that
\eta=\bar{\eta}\circ\iota
.
By Theorem
4.8
, there is some eventually small, eventually dense sequence
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{B}
that induces the embedding
\bar{\eta}
, with the property that
\beta(b_{n})\wedge b_{n+1}\neq\mathbf{0}
for all
n
.

Roughly, the problem here is that to be eventually dense, the sequence
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
must get close to
d
infinitely often. The only way to do this is to enter the region near
d
from the region near
c
, and then to exit again into the region near
c
. Using the fact that
\eta=\bar{\eta}\circ\iota
, this means that the sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
must infinitely often be going near
c
, and then near
b=\pi(d)
, and then near
c
again. But the sequence of
a_{n}
’s does not behave this way: it was chosen so that whenever it goes from near
c
to near
b
, it will, every time, continue on in the direction of
a
, not back towards
c
.

Let us make this idea more precise.
Let
\mathcal{U}=\{A,B,C\}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
into three elements, with
a\in A
and
b\in B
, and
c\in C
.
Note that
\iota(B)=\pi^{-1}(B)\in\mathbb{B}
, and
b,d\in\iota(B)
. Fix some
\bar{B},\bar{D}\in\mathbb{B}
such that
\bar{B}\cup\bar{D}=\iota(B)
, with
b\in\bar{B}
and
d\in\bar{D}
. Let
\bar{A}=\iota(A)
and
\bar{C}=\iota(C)
, and let
\mathcal{V}=\{\bar{A},\bar{B},\bar{C},\bar{D}\}
. Thus
\mathcal{V}
is a partition of
\mathbb{B}
into four elements, with
a\in\bar{A}
,
b\in\bar{B}
,
c\in\bar{C}
, and
d\in\bar{D}
.

Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
and
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
are eventually small,
a_{n}
is below
\mathcal{U}
and
b_{n}
is below
\mathcal{V}
for sufficiently large
n
.
Furthermore, because
\eta=\bar{\eta}\circ\iota
,

\displaystyle[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\leq A\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,\eta(A)\,=\,\bar{\eta}\circ\iota(A)\,=\,\bar{\eta}(\bar{A})\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon b_{n}\leq\bar{A}\right\}],

which means that
\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\in A\right\}=^{*}\left\{n\in\omega\colon b_{n}\in\bar{A}\right\}
, and so

a_{n}\leq A\quad\text{if and only if}\quad b_{n}\leq\bar{A}

for sufficiently large
n
. Because
\iota
is an embedding
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
,
a_{n}\leq A
if and only if
\iota(a_{n})\leq\iota(A)=\bar{A}
. Hence

\iota(a_{n})\leq\bar{A}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad b_{n}\leq\bar{A}

for sufficiently large
n
.
Similarly,

\iota(a_{n})\leq\bar{B}\cup\bar{D}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad b_{n}\leq\bar{B}\cup\bar{D},\text{ and}

\iota(a_{n})\leq\bar{C}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad b_{n}\leq\bar{C}

for sufficiently large
n
.
Because
a_{n}
is below
\mathcal{U}
and
b_{n}
below
\mathcal{V}
for sufficiently large
n
, this means each of
\iota(a_{n})
and
b_{n}
is
\leq
exactly one of
\bar{A}
,
\bar{B}\cup\bar{D}
, and
\bar{C}
for large enough
n
. Combined with the three displayed assertions above, this implies
\iota(a_{n})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}b_{n}
for sufficiently large
n
. Fix
N\in\omega
such that
n\geq N
implies
\iota(a_{n})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}b_{n}
.

Using the fact that
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually dense, fix some
i\geq N
such that
b_{i}\not\leq\bar{D}
and some
k>i
such that
b_{k}\leq\bar{D}
.

Let
j
denote the maximum integer
<\!k
such that
b_{j}\not\leq\bar{D}
. (Note that
N\leq i\leq j<k
.) Let
\ell
denote the minimum integer
>\!k
such that
b_{\ell}\not\leq\bar{D}
. (Some such
k
must exist because
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually dense, and so infinitely often
b_{n}\leq\mathbf{1}-\bar{D}
.)
Thus
b_{j}\not\leq\bar{D}
and
b_{\ell}\not\leq\bar{D}
, but
b_{n}\leq\bar{D}
for every
n\in(j,\ell)
.
In fact, because
b_{j}
and
b_{\ell}
are below
\mathcal{V}
, we have
b_{j}\wedge\bar{D}=b_{\ell}\wedge\bar{D}=\mathbf{0}
.

Recall that
\beta(b_{n})\wedge b_{n+1}\neq\mathbf{0}
for all
n
. Because
b_{\ell-1}\leq\bar{D}
, we must have
b_{\ell}\wedge\beta(\bar{D})\neq\mathbf{0}
. But
\beta(\bar{D})\leq\bar{D}\vee\bar{C}
and
b_{\ell}\wedge\bar{D}=\mathbf{0}
, so this implies
b_{\ell}\wedge\bar{C}\neq\mathbf{0}
. Because
b_{\ell}
is below
\mathcal{V}
, we see that
b_{\ell}\leq\bar{C}
.
Similarly, because
b_{j+1}\leq\bar{D}
we must have
b_{j}\wedge\beta^{-1}(\bar{D})\neq\mathbf{0}
. But
\beta^{-1}(\bar{D})\leq\bar{D}\vee\bar{C}
and
b_{j}\wedge\bar{D}=\mathbf{0}
, so this implies
b_{j}\wedge\bar{C}\neq\mathbf{0}
. Because
b_{j}
is below
\mathcal{V}
, we get
b_{j}\leq\bar{C}
.

Hence
b_{j},b_{\ell}\leq\bar{C}
, and
b_{n}\leq\bar{D}
for all
n\in(j,\ell)
. Because
\iota(a_{n})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}b_{n}
for all
n\geq N
, this means that
\iota(a_{j})\wedge\bar{C}\neq\mathbf{0}
,

\iota(a_{\ell})\wedge\bar{C}\neq\mathbf{0}
, and

\iota(a_{n})\wedge\bar{D}\neq\mathbf{0}
for all
n\in(j,\ell)
.
But
a_{n}
is below
\mathcal{U}
for all
n\in[j,\ell]
, so the only way for this to happen is if

a_{j}\leq C
, then
a_{n}\leq B
for all
n\in(j,\ell)
, and then
a_{\ell}\leq C
.

We have arrived at our sought-after contradiction, because the sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
never exhibits this behavior. If
a_{j}\leq C
and
a_{n}\leq B
for all
n\in(j,\ell)
, then (by our definition of the
a_{n}
’s), we must have either
a_{\ell}\leq B
or
a_{\ell}\leq A
.
∎

As one would probably guess, a “reverse” version of Theorem
5.1
is also true: not every instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
has a solution. This can of course be proved by suitably modifying the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
Alternatively, one can deduce this directly from Theorem
5.1
by showing that if
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, then
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta^{-1}\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, and

\bar{\eta}
is a solution to one of these problems if and only if it is also a solution to the other.
This idea (or one nearly identical to it) is laid out in more detail in the proof of Lemma
6.3
below.

6.
The Lifting Lemma, part 1: a back-and-forth argument

Despite the results of the previous section, we still aim to prove
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate under
\mathsf{CH}
via a back-and-forth argument like the proof of Theorem
2.4
.
The idea, as mentioned at the end of Section
3
, is to show that all “sufficiently nice” instances of the lifting problem have a solution.
We can then do our back-and-forth argument carefully, so that we never encounter any not-nice instances of the lifting problem, and the argument goes through.
What precisely “sufficiently nice” means in this context involves the model-theoretic concept of elementarity.

By the
language of dynamical systems
we mean the formal logical language that, in addition to the usual things like the logical connectives “and”, “or”, and
\Rightarrow
, the negation symbol
\neg
, the quantifiers
\forall
and
\exists
, etc., includes two special constant terms
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{1}
, three binary operations
+
,
-
, and
\cdot
, the binary relation
\leq
, and unary function symbols representing an automorphism of a Boolean algebra and its inverse.
This language can be used to write down properties of a dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
.
For example,
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is incompressible if and only if it satisfies the sentence

\forall x\in\mathbb{A}\,(\neg(x=\mathbf{0})\text{ and }\neg(x=\mathbf{1}))\Rightarrow\neg(\alpha(x)\leq x).

Following the usual practice, all the standard abbreviations are allowed; for example, the sentence above can be written “
\forall x\in\mathbb{A}\!\setminus\!\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}\ \alpha(x)\not\leq x
” with no ambiguity.
A
first-order
sentence in the language of dynamical systems is one where the quantifiers
\forall
and
\exists
range only over members of a Boolean algebra (not, for example, subsets of that algebra). For example, the sentence displayed above is first-order.

Definition 6.1
.

An
elementary embedding
of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
is a function
\eta:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
that preserves the truth value of all first-order sentences in the language of dynamical systems, in the sense that

\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle\models\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},\alpha)\ \text{ if and only if }\,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle\models\varphi(\eta(a_{1}),\dots,\eta(a_{n}),\beta)

whenever
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{A}
.
If
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{B}
, then
\langle\mathbb{A},\beta\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}\rangle
is called an
elementary substructure
of
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
if the inclusion map
