\mathbb{A}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{B}
is an elementary embedding.
☕

Roughly,
\langle\mathbb{A},\beta\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
if there is no difference between them detectable by a first-order sentence in the language of dynamical systems, even when that sentence is allowed to contain parameters from
\mathbb{A}
.
Nevertheless, a structure can be quite different from one of its elementary substructures in their second-order properties. For example, the (downward) Löwenheim-Skolem Theorem tells us that every uncountable dynamical system has countable elementary substructures. Among other things, this means uncountability is not expressible in a first-order sentence.

We are now in a position to state the paper’s primary lemma, mentioned in the introduction, which states that every “sufficiently nice” instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
has a solution.

Lemma 6.2

(Lifting Lemma)
.

Let
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
be an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
If the image of
\eta
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, then this instance of the lifting problem has a solution.

The proof of this lemma occupies most of the rest of the paper. In the remainder of this section, we show how to deduce the main theorem of the paper from the lemma.
The first step is to deduce a “backwards” version of the Lifting Lemma from the version stated above.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
be an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
If the image of
\eta
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, then this instance of the lifting problem has a solution.

Proof.

If
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, then

(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta^{-1}\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Furthermore, if the image of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
under
\eta
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, then
the image of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
under
\eta
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
. (In fact the images coincide, and this assertion really just amounts to the fact that if
\eta
transforms true sentences about
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into true sentences about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, then it must also transform true sentences about
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
into true sentences about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
. This is obvious, because these two classes of sentences are literally the same.)
So assuming Lemma
6.2
, there is an embedding
\bar{\eta}
of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
that solves the instance
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta^{-1}\rangle,\iota,\eta)
of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
But this implies that
\bar{\eta}
is also a solution to the instance
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
∎

Theorem 6.4

(The Main Theorem)
.

The Continuum Hypothesis implies
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate.

Proof.

We repeat the proof of Theorem
2.4
with a few important differences.
Given
X\subseteq\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
with
X
countable, let
\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle

denote not merely the subalgebra generated by
X
, but some countable subalgebra of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
with
X\subseteq\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
such that

\big\langle\hskip-0.56905pt\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle,\sigma\!\!\restriction\!\!\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle\hskip-0.56905pt\big\rangle

is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
(Clearly this contains the subalgebra generated by
X
, but it may be larger.)
This is well-defined by the downward Löwenheim-Skolem theorem, which asserts that if
X\subseteq\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
and
X
is countable, then there is some countable
Y\supseteq X
such that
\langle Y,\sigma\!\restriction\!Y\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. (One may even describe a canonical way of obtaining
\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
from
X
, analogous to the closure of
X
under algebraic operations to obtain the subalgebra generated by
X
. This process is called
Skolemization
, but defining it precisely is not necessary to our proof.)
In what follows, we will write

\big\langle\hskip-0.56905pt\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle,\sigma\hskip-0.56905pt\big\rangle

and
\big\langle\hskip-0.56905pt\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle,\sigma^{-1}\hskip-0.56905pt\big\rangle

instead of
\big\langle\hskip-0.56905pt\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle,\sigma\!\!\restriction\!\!\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle\hskip-0.56905pt\big\rangle
and
\big\langle\hskip-0.56905pt\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle,\sigma^{-1}\!\!\restriction\!\!\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle\hskip-0.56905pt\big\rangle
.

Generally
\left\lvert\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\right\rvert=\mathfrak{c}
, so
\left\lvert\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\right\rvert=\aleph_{1}
because we are assuming
\mathsf{CH}
.
Let
\left\langle a_{\xi}\colon\xi<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
be an enumeration of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
in order type
\omega_{1}
.

Using transfinite recursion, we now build a sequence
\left\langle\phi_{\alpha}\colon\alpha<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of maps such that each
\phi_{\alpha}
is a conjugacy from its domain (which we denote
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
) to its codomain (which we denote
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}
), or more precisely from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
Furthermore, the following will hold at every stage
\alpha
of the recursion:

\circ

\mathbb{A}_{\xi}\subseteq\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\xi}\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}
for all
\xi\leq\alpha
,

\circ

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{B}_{\xi}
for limit
\alpha
,

\circ

the
\phi_{\xi}
’s are coherent, i.e.,
\phi_{\xi}=\phi_{\zeta}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
whenever
\xi\leq\zeta\leq\alpha
,

\circ

\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.

For the base case of the recursion, let
\mathbb{A}_{0}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\emptyset\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
. (In other words,
\mathbb{A}_{0}
is some countable subalgebra of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.)
Because incompressibility is expressible by a first-order sentence and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is incompressible, elementarity implies
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle
is also incompressible.
Recall that every countable incompressible dynamical system embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
. (This was mentioned in Section
3
. It is proved, in dual form, in
[
6
]
; it is also proved below as Theorem
7.9
.) Let
\phi_{0}
be an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
. Let
\mathbb{B}_{0}
denote the image of
\mathbb{A}_{0}
under
\phi_{0}
. Thus

\phi_{0}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}_{0},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, and

\phi_{0}^{-1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{B}_{0},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle
.

At limit stages of the recursion there is nothing to do: take
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
,
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{B}_{\xi}
, and
\phi_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\phi_{\xi}
, which is well-defined because the
\phi_{\xi}
are coherent. Recall that an increasing union of elementary substructures is itself an elementary substructure, so taking unions like this preserves the hypotheses of the recursion through limit stages.

At successor steps we obtain
\phi_{\alpha+1}
from
\phi_{\alpha}
using Lemmas
6.2
and
6.3
.
First, let
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\cup\{a_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
and observe that

(\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha},\sigma^{-1}\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma^{-1}\rangle,\iota,\phi_{\alpha}^{-1})

is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
. Furthermore, the image of
\phi^{-1}_{\alpha}
, namely
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
, is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
By Lemma
6.2
, there is an embedding
\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1}
from
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Let
\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1}
denote the image of this embedding. (Note that there is no obvious reason why
\langle\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
should be an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. Instead, we now have elementarity on the other side of our mappings, with
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\cup\{a_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
.)
Because
\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
,

(\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1})^{-1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\cup\{a_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}
=\mathrm{Image}(\phi_{\alpha+1})
\mathrm{Image}(\phi_{\alpha+1}^{0})=
\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}^{0}\cup\{a_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle=

\subseteq

=

=

\subseteq

\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}
\phi_{\alpha+1}^{0}
(\phi_{\alpha+1}^{0})^{-1}
\phi_{\alpha+1}

Second, let
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}^{0}\cup\{a_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
and observe that

(\langle\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle,\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle,\iota,(\phi_{\alpha}^{0})^{-1})

is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion
\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}
. Furthermore, the image of
(\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1})^{-1}
, namely
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
By Lemma
6.2
, there is an embedding
\phi_{\alpha+1}
from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
Let
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}
denote the image of this embedding, so that

\phi_{\alpha+1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, and

(\phi_{\alpha+1})^{-1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
.

This completes the recursion. Because the
\phi_{\alpha}
are coherent conjugacies from substructures of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to substructures of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
,
\phi=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\phi_{\alpha}
is a conjugacy from
\big\langle\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\big\rangle
to
\big\langle\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{\alpha},\sigma^{-1}\big\rangle
.
But
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
and
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}=\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, because we ensured at stage
\alpha+1
of the recursion that
a_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}
and
a_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}
.
∎

7.
The Lifting Lemma, part 2: directed graphs

We now set about proving the Lifting Lemma.
This entails a close look at the “finite substructures” of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, and how they all fit together.
Following
[
4
,
17
,
7
]
, we use digraphs to encode the finitary fragments of our large dynamical systems.

Definition 7.1
.

A
directed graph
, or
digraph
, is a pair
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
, where
\mathcal{V}
is a finite set (the vertices) and
\to
is a relation on
\mathcal{V}
(possibly asymmetric, and possibly with “loops”, i.e., vertices
v\in\mathcal{V}
with
v\to v
).
☕

b
a
c

When convenient, to keep the clutter in our pictures to a minimum, we will sometimes use a two-headed arrow between
x
and
y
when
x\to y
and
y\to x
(unlike in the picture above).

Definition 7.2
.

Suppose
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
are directed graphs. An
epimorphism
from
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
is a surjection
\phi:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
such that
for any
u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
u\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}u^{\prime}
if and only if there are some
v\in\phi^{-1}(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in\phi^{-1}(u^{\prime})
such that
v\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}v^{\prime}
.

In other words, arrows in
\mathcal{V}
map to arrows in
\mathcal{U}
, and every arrow in
\mathcal{U}
is the image of some arrow in
\mathcal{V}
.

In this case, we say that
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
is a
refinement
of
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
(via
\phi
).
☕

For example, the following directed graph is a refinement of the one drawn above, and the epimorphism
\phi
witnessing this fact is indicated by where the vertices of the new graph are drawn.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Definition 7.3
.

Given a dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
, we associate to every partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{A}
a digraph
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{A}
itself is the vertex set, and we define
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}
as the “hitting relation” for
\alpha
: that is,

x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}y\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad\alpha(x)\wedge y\neq\mathbf{0}

for all
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
.
☕

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
be a dynamical system. Suppose
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are partitions of
\mathbb{A}
, and
\mathcal{B}
refines
\mathcal{A}
. Then the natural map
\pi:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Because
\mathcal{B}
is a partition refining
\mathcal{A}
, for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
there is at least one
b\in\mathcal{B}
with
b\leq a
, i.e., with
\pi(b)=a
. Thus
\pi
is a surjection
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Suppose
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
and
b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}b^{\prime}
. This means
\alpha(b)\wedge b^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
, which implies
\alpha(\pi(b))\wedge\pi(b^{\prime})\neq\mathbf{0}
(because
b\leq\pi(b)
and
b^{\prime}\leq\pi(b^{\prime})
), i.e.
\pi(b)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\pi(b^{\prime})
.

On the other hand, suppose that
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
and
a\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}a^{\prime}
. This means
\alpha(a)\wedge a^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
, or equivalently,
a\wedge\alpha^{-1}(a^{\prime})\neq\mathbf{0}
. This implies that there is some
b\in\mathcal{B}
with
b\wedge a\wedge\alpha^{-1}(a^{\prime})\neq\mathbf{0}
.
Hence
b\wedge a\neq\mathbf{0}
and
\alpha(b)\wedge a^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
.
The fact that
b\wedge a\neq\mathbf{0}
implies
\pi(b)=a
.
The fact that
\alpha(b)\wedge a^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
implies there is some
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
with
b^{\prime}\wedge\alpha(b)\wedge a^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
.
But
b^{\prime}\wedge a^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
implies
\pi(b^{\prime})=a^{\prime}
, and
b^{\prime}\wedge\alpha(b)\neq\mathbf{0}
means
b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}b^{\prime}
.
Thus there are
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
such that
\pi(b)=a
,
\pi(b^{\prime})=a^{\prime}
, and
b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}b^{\prime}
.
∎

This lemma explains our choice of terminology in the last part of Definition
7.2
:
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is a (digraph) refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
, via the natural map, whenever
\mathcal{B}
is a (partition) refinement of
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 7.5
.

A
walk
through a digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is a finite sequence
\langle a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\rangle
of members of
\mathcal{V}
such that
a_{i}\to a_{i+1}
for all
i<n
. We say that this walk has length
n
(counting the arrows, not the vertices), and that it is a walk from
a_{0}
to
a_{n}
.

A digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is
strongly connected
if for any
a,b\in\mathcal{V}
, there is a walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
from
a
to
b
.
☕

The next two theorems show roughly that the strongly connected digraphs capture the property of incompressibility in a dynamical system.

Theorem 7.6
.

A dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is incompressible if and only if
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected for every partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{A}
.

Proof.

For the forward implication, suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is an incompressible dynamical system, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathcal{A}
. Fix
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and let

C_{a}=\left\{b\in\mathcal{A}\colon\text{there is a walk in $\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle$ from $a$ to $b$}\right\}.

If
b\in C_{a}
and
b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}c
, then any walk from
a
to
b
can be extended to a walk from
a
to
c
, and it follows that
c\in C_{a}
. Thus
\alpha(b)\leq\bigvee C_{a}
for every
b\in C_{a}
.
It follows that
\alpha(\bigvee C_{a})=\bigvee\left\{\alpha(b)\colon b\in C_{a}\right\}\leq C_{a}
.
Because
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is incompressible (and
C_{a}\neq\emptyset
), this implies
\bigvee C_{a}=\mathbf{1}
. Thus
C_{a}=\mathcal{A}
, which means there is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
from
a
to any other member of
\mathcal{A}
. But
a
was arbitrary, so this means
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected.

For the reverse implication, suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is not incompressible, which means there is some
x\in\mathbb{A}\setminus\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
such that
\alpha(x)\leq x
. Let
\mathcal{A}=\{x,\mathbf{1}-x\}
.

x
\mathbf{1}-x
x
\mathbf{1}-x
or

The digraph
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is not strongly connected: because
x\leq\alpha(x)
, we do not have
x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\mathbf{1}-x
, and there is no walk from
x
to
\mathbf{1}-x
.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
be a strongly connected digraph, and let
v,w\in\mathcal{V}
.
There is a walk
\langle v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
such that
v_{0}=v
and
v_{n}=w
, and such that for any
x,y\in\mathcal{V}
with
x\to y
, there is some
i<n
such that
v_{i}=x
and
v_{i+1}=y
.
(In other words, every arrow in the digraph is represented in the walk.)

Proof.

Fix an enumeration
\left\{(a_{i},b_{i})\colon i\leq k\right\}
of (the ordered pairs in) the edge relation
\to
, such that
a_{0}=v
and
b_{k}=w
.
To get the desired walk, first let
v_{0}=a_{0}
and
v_{1}=b_{0}
. Because
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is strongly connected, there is a walk from
b_{0}
to
a_{1}
; so we may define
v_{2},v_{3},\dots,v_{\ell_{1}}
(for some
\ell_{1}
) so that
\langle b_{0},v_{2},v_{3},\dots,v_{\ell_{1}},a_{1}\rangle
is a walk from
b_{0}
to
a_{1}
. Take
v_{\ell_{1}+1}=a_{1}
and
v_{\ell_{1}+2}=b_{1}
, and then define
v_{\ell_{1}+3},v_{\ell_{1}+4},\dots,v_{\ell_{2}}
(for some
\ell_{2}\geq\ell_{1}+3
) so that
\langle b_{1},v_{\ell_{1}+3},v_{\ell_{1}+4},\dots,v_{\ell_{2}},a_{2}\rangle
is a walk from
b_{1}
to
a_{2}
. Take
v_{\ell_{2}+1}=a_{2}
and
v_{\ell_{2}+2}=b_{2}
, and then choose the next few
v_{i}
to form a walk to
a_{3}
. Continuing in this way, we find a walk from
a_{0}=v
to
b_{k}=w
such that every arrow in the digraph is represented in the walk.
∎

Theorem 7.8
.

Given a digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is strongly connected.

(2)

There is an incompressible dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and a partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{A}
such that
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle\cong\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.

(3)

There is a partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle\cong\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.

Proof.

(3)\Rightarrow(2)
because
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is incompressible (by Theorem
3.2
).

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
by Theorem
7.6
.

To prove
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
,
fix some
w\in\mathcal{V}
and then, applying the previous lemma, fix a walk
\left\langle v_{i}\colon i\leq n\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
such that
v_{0}=v_{n}=w
and such that every arrow in the digraph is represented in the walk.
For each
v\in\mathcal{V}
, define

A_{v}=\left\{nk+i\colon k\geq 0\text{ and }v_{i}=v\right\}

and let
\mathcal{A}=\left\{[A_{v}]\colon v\in\mathcal{V}\right\}
. We claim that
\mathcal{A}=\left\{[A_{v}]\colon v\in\mathcal{V}\right\}
is a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
and
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle\cong\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
.

Because
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is strongly connected, every
v\in\mathcal{V}
has in-degree and out-degree
>\!0
, and this implies (as every arrow is represented in our path) that
v=v_{i}
for at least one
i\leq n
. Thus
A_{v}
is infinite.
Furthermore, it is clear that
A_{u}\cap A_{v}=\emptyset
whenever
u\neq v
, and that
\bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{V}}A_{v}=\omega
.
Hence
\mathcal{A}=\left\{[A_{v}]\colon v\in\mathcal{V}\right\}
is a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.

To finish the proof, we show that the map
v\mapsto[A_{v}]
is an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.
This map is clearly bijective, so we must show that it preserves the edge relation. If
u,v\in\mathcal{V}
and
u\to v
, then there is some
i<n
such that
v_{i}=u
and
v_{i+1}=v
. So
(A_{u}+1)\cap A_{v}\supseteq\left\{nk+i+1\colon k\geq 0\right\},

and it follows that
\sigma([A_{u}])\wedge[A_{v}]=[(A_{u}+1)\cap A_{v}]\geq[\left\{nk+i+1\colon k\geq 0\right\}]\neq\mathbf{0}
, which means
[A_{u}]\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}[A_{v}]
.
Conversely, if
u,v\in\mathcal{V}
and
[A_{u}]\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}[A_{v}]
, then we have
j+1\in A_{v}
for infinitely many
j\in A_{u}
. By our definition of
A_{u}
and
A_{v}
, this means that
u=v_{i}
and
v=v_{i+1}
for some
i<n
. Because
\left\langle v_{j}\colon j\leq n\right\rangle
is a walk, this implies
u\to v
.
∎

We end this section with the following important observation, which was used in the proof of Theorem
6.4
above. This theorem, in its topological dual form, first appeared in
[
6
]
, but we include a proof here for the sake of completeness.

Theorem 7.9
.

A countable dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
if and only if it is incompressible.

Proof.

The forward direction was proved already: every dynamical system that embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is incompressible by Theorem
3.3
.

For the reverse direction, fix a sequence
\left\langle\mathcal{A}_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
of partitions of
\mathbb{A}
satisfying the conclusion of Lemma
4.4
: that is,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{A}_{m}
whenever
m\leq n
, and for every partition
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{V}
for all sufficiently large
n
.
Because
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is incompressible, Theorem
7.6
tells us that
\langle\mathcal{A}_{n},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected for every
n
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an ultrafilter on
\mathbb{A}
, and for each
n\in\omega
let
a^{n}_{0}
be the (unique) member of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
in
\mathcal{U}
. (This implies
a^{0}_{0}\geq a^{1}_{0}\geq a^{2}_{0}\geq\dots
.)
Applying Lemma
7.7
, for each
n
let
\left\langle a^{n}_{i}\colon i\leq\ell_{n}\right\rangle
be a walk through
\langle\mathcal{A}_{n},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
such that, for every
n
,
a^{n}_{0}=a^{n}_{\ell_{n}}=a^{n}_{0}
and every arrow in
\langle\mathcal{A}_{n},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is represented in the walk.

Let
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
be the infinite sequence of members of
\mathbb{A}
obtained by concatenating the walks
\left\langle a^{0}_{i}\colon i<\ell_{0}\right\rangle
,
\left\langle a^{1}_{i}\colon i<\ell_{1}\right\rangle
,
\left\langle a^{2}_{i}\colon i<\ell_{2}\right\rangle
, etc., in that order. In other words,
b_{i}=a^{n}_{j}
if and only if
i=\ell_{0}+\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{n-1}+j
for some
j<\ell_{n}
.

If
\mathcal{V}
is a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, then
\mathcal{A}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{V}
for sufficiently large
n
, which means
b_{i}
is below
\mathcal{V}
for sufficiently large
i
. Hence
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually small.
If
a\in\mathbb{A}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
then for all sufficiently large
n
there is some
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
with
a^{\prime}\leq a
, which implies there is some
j<\ell_{n}
with
a^{n}_{j}=a^{\prime}\leq a
. Hence there are infinitely many
i
with
b_{i}\leq a
, and as
a
was arbitrary,
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually dense.

Finally, fix a partition
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathbb{A}
, and suppose
n
is large enough that
\mathcal{A}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{V}
.
If
b_{i}=a^{n}_{j}
and
b_{i+1}=a^{n}_{j+1}
, then
b_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}b_{i+1}
because
\langle a^{n}_{j}:\,j\leq\ell_{n}\rangle
is a walk.
Because
\mathcal{A}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{V}
, this implies
\alpha(b_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}b_{i+1}
.
If instead
b_{i}=a^{n}_{\ell_{n}-1}
and
b_{i+1}=a^{n+1}_{0}
, then
\alpha(b_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}b_{i+1}
because

b_{i}\,=\,a^{n}_{\ell_{n}-1}\,\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\,a^{n}_{\ell_{n}}\,=\,a^{n}_{0}\,\geq\,a^{n+1}_{0}\,=\,b_{i+1}.

Because
\mathcal{A}_{n}
refines
\mathcal{V}
, we have
\alpha(b_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}b_{i+1}
in this case as well.
In either case,
b_{i}\approx_{\mathcal{V}}b_{i+1}
for all sufficiently large
n
.
As
\mathcal{V}
was an arbitrary partition of
\mathbb{A}
, this means
\left\langle\alpha(b_{i})\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle b_{i+1}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.
By Theorem
4.10
,
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
induces an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
∎

8.
The Lifting Lemma, part 3: a diagonal argument

In Theorem
8.8
below, we give a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence a solution to a given instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Roughly, the condition is that we can solve an instance of the lifting problem if and only if we can solve arbitrarily large “finitary fragments” of the problem (where these fragments are encoded via digraphs as described in the previous section).
The proof of this theorem is essentially just a diagonalization argument, albeit a somewhat intricate one.
First we need several definitions and lemmas.

Definition 8.1
.

An
infinite walk
through a digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is an infinite sequence
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathcal{V}
such that

v_{n}\to v_{n+1}
for all
n\in\omega
.

A
diligent walk
through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is an infinite walk with the property that every arrow is traversed infinitely often, or more precisely:

\circ

for each
v,w\in\mathcal{V}
with
v\to w
, there are infinitely many
n\in\omega
such that
v_{n}=v
and
v_{n+1}=w
.

Given an infinite walk
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
(diligent or not) through a digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
, for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
define
A_{v}=[\left\{n\in\omega\colon v_{n}=v\right\}]
.
It is not difficult to see that
\left\{A_{v}\colon v\in\mathcal{V}\right\}\setminus\{[\emptyset]\}
is a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
; we call this the
partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
associated to the walk
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
☕

Lemma 8.2
.

A digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is strongly connected if and only if it has no isolated points and there is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
.

Proof.

If
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is strongly connected, then clearly it has no isolated points. Furthermore, by Lemma
7.7
there is a walk
\langle v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
such that
v_{k}=v_{0}
, and for each
v,w\in\mathcal{V}
with
v\to w
, there is some
i<k
such that
v_{i}=v
and
v_{i+1}=w
.
For each
n\in\omega
, let
v_{n}=v_{i}
if and only if
i\equiv n
modulo
k
. Then
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
.

Conversely, suppose
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
has no isolated points, and
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
.
Let
u,v\in\mathcal{V}
.
Because
u
and
v
are not isolated in
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
, the definition of “diligent” implies
u
and
v
both appear infinitely often in
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
Consequently, there is some
m\in\omega
with
v_{m}=u
and some
n>m
with
v_{n}=v
. But then
\langle v_{m},v_{m+1},\dots,v_{n}\rangle
is a walk from
u
to
v
in
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
. As
u
and
v
were arbitrary,
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is strongly connected.
∎

The next two lemmas express the most important aspect of diligent walks: a diligent walk through a digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is equivalent to an identification of
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
with a digraph of the form
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{A}
is a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
be a strongly connected digraph and let
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
be a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
.
Let
\mathcal{A}=\left\{A_{v}\colon v\in\mathcal{V}\right\}
be the partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
associated to this walk. Then the map
v\mapsto A_{v}
is an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
be a strongly connected digraph, let
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
, and let
\mathcal{A}=\left\{A_{v}\colon v\in\mathcal{V}\right\}
be the partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
associated to this walk.
The map
v\mapsto A_{v}
is clearly a bijection.
If
v\to w
, then (by the definition of a diligent walk) there are infinitely many
n
such that
v_{n}=v
and
v_{n+1}=w
, and this implies
A_{v}\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}A_{w}
.
Conversely, if
A_{v}\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}A_{w}
, then this means that

\displaystyle[\left\{n\colon v_{n}=v\text{ and }v_{n+1}=w\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\colon v_{n}=v\right\}]\wedge[\left\{n\colon v_{n+1}=w\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\colon v_{n}=v\right\}]\wedge[\left\{n-1\colon v_{n}=w\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,A_{v}\wedge\sigma^{-1}(A_{w})\,\neq\,[\emptyset].

In other words, there are infinitely many
n
such that
v_{n}=v
and
v_{n+1}=w
, which implies
v\to w
.
Hence
v\to w
if and only if
A_{v}\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}A_{w}
.
∎

Definition 8.4
.

Suppose that
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is a strongly connected digraph, and
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
. The map
v\mapsto A_{v}
is called the
natural isomorphism
from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
or, more precisely, the natural isomorphism
associated to the walk
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
☕

Lemma 8.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and suppose that there is a digraph
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
and an isomorphism
\phi
from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
. Then there is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
such that
\phi
is the natural isomorphism associated to this walk.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, let
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
be a digraph, and let
\phi
be an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
. Note that this implies
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is strongly connected by Theorem
7.8
.

For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
fix some
X_{a}\subseteq\omega
such that
a=[X_{a}]
.
If
a\neq b
then
a\wedge b=[\emptyset]
, which means that
X_{a}\cap X_{b}
is finite.
Also, because
\bigvee\mathcal{A}=[\omega]
,
\left\{n\in\omega\colon n\notin\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}X_{a}\right\}
is finite.
Similarly, if it is not the case that
a\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}b
then
\left\{n\colon n\in X_{a}\text{ and }n+1\in X_{b}\right\}
is finite, but if it is the case that
a\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}b
then
\left\{n\colon n\in X_{a}\text{ and }n+1\in X_{b}\right\}
is infinite.

Consequently, there is some sufficiently large value of
N\in\omega
such that

\circ

If
n\geq N
then there is exactly one
a\in\mathcal{A}
such that
n\in X_{a}
, and

\circ

The following are equivalent for any
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
:

\cdot

n\in X_{a}
and
n+1\in X_{b}
for some
n\geq N

\cdot

n\in X_{a}
and
n+1\in X_{b}
for infinitely many
n\geq N

\cdot

a\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}b
.

By modifying each of the
X_{a}
below
N
(which does not change the equality
[X_{a}]=a
), we may (and do) assume that these two properties hold with
N=0
.
(More precisely, to see how these modifications can be accomplished, fix a length-
N
walk
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\leq N\right\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
with
a_{N}=[X_{a_{N}}]
.
Some such walk exists because
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, by Theorem
7.8
.
Then put
i\in X_{a_{i}}
for all
i\leq N
.)

For each
k\in\omega
, let
a_{k}
denote the unique
a\in\mathcal{A}
such that
k\in X_{a}
.
For each
n\in\omega
, let

v_{n}=\phi^{-1}(a_{n})
.
We claim that
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
, and that
\phi
is the natural isomorphism associated to this walk.

To see that this is a diligent walk, first note that
a_{n}\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}a_{n+1}
for all
n
(because
n\in X_{a}
and
n+1\in X_{b}
is equivalent to
a\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}b
).
Because
\phi
is an isomorphism, this implies
\phi^{-1}(a_{n})\to\phi^{-1}(a_{n+1})
for all
n
.
Thus
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an infinite walk.
Furthermore, if
v,w\in\mathcal{V}
with
v\to w
, then
\phi(v)\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\phi(w)
, which means there are infinitely many
n
with
n\in X_{\phi(v)}
and
n+1\in X_{\phi(w)}
. Thus there are infinitely many
n
such that
v_{n}=v
and
v_{n+1}=w
.

Finally, we claim that
\phi
is the natural isomorphism associated to this diligent walk.
Our definition of the walk implies that for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
,

A_{v}\,=\,[\left\{n\colon v_{n}=v\right\}]\,=\,[\left\{n\colon a_{n}=\phi(v)\right\}]\,=\,[X_{\phi(v)}]\,=\,\phi(v).

Thus
A_{v}=\phi(v)
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
.
∎

Recall that
[X]^{2}
denotes the set of all unordered pairs of elements of
X
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Suppose
\chi:[\omega]^{2}\to\omega
, i.e.,
\chi
is an
\omega
-coloring of the complete graph
K_{\omega}
, and suppose that all monochromatic cliques for
\chi
are finite. Then
there is an increasing sequence
\left\langle n_{k}\colon k\in\omega\right\rangle
of natural numbers such that
\chi(n_{k},n_{k+1})>k
for all
k
.

Proof.

Fix
\chi:[\omega]^{2}\to\omega
, an
\omega
-coloring of
K_{\omega}
, and assume that all monochromatic cliques are finite.
Now suppose
X\subseteq\omega
is infinite.
If only finitely many colors are taken by
\chi
in the graph
[X]^{2}
, then Ramsey’s Theorem (the infinitary version) implies that there is an infinite monochromatic clique for
\chi
in
[X]^{2}
, contradicting our assumption about
\chi
.
Thus if
X\subseteq\omega
is infinite, then the image of
[X]^{2}
under
\chi
is infinite.

We now construct an
\omega
-sequence of infinite subsets of
\omega
by recursion.
Let
X_{0}=\omega
.
Given
X_{n}
, define a
2
-coloring
\chi_{n}
of
[X_{n}]^{2}
by setting

\chi_{n}(a,b)\,=\,\begin{cases}0\ \text{ if }\chi(a,b)<n+1,\\
1\ \text{ if }\chi(a,b)\geq n+1.\end{cases}

By the previous paragraph,
[X_{n}]^{2}
does not have any infinite monochromatic cliques with color
0
. But by Ramsey’s Theorem,
[X_{n}]^{2}
does have an infinite monochromatic clique. Let
X_{n+1}
be any infinite subset of
X_{n}
such that
\chi_{n}(a,b)\geq n+1
for all
a,b\in X_{n+1}
. This completes the recursion.

For each
k\in\omega
, let
n_{k}\in X_{k}\setminus\max\{n_{0},n_{1},\dots,n_{k-1}\}
.
Then
n_{k},n_{k+1}\in X_{k}
, which implies
\chi(n_{k},n_{k+1})>k
, for all
k
.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
be dynamical systems, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
.
If
\eta
is an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, then
\eta
is an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\eta[\mathcal{A}],\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Clearly
\eta
is a bijection
\mathcal{A}\to\eta[\mathcal{A}]
.
Furthermore, because
\eta
is an embedding, given any
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
a\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}b
if and only if
\eta(a)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\eta(b)
.
It follows that
\eta
is a digraph isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\eta[\mathcal{A}],\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
.
∎

Before stating the next theorem, which is the main result of this section, let us establish a new (abuse of) notation.
Suppose
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{C}
are Boolean algebras,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are partitions of
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{C}
, respectively, and
\eta
is a bijection
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
. Suppose also that
a
is in the (finite) subalgebra of
\mathbb{B}
generated by
\mathcal{B}
, i.e.,
a=\bigvee\left\{b\in\mathcal{B}\colon b\leq a\right\}
. In this case, we define
\eta(a)=\bigvee\left\{\eta(b)\colon b\in\mathcal{B}\text{ and }b\leq a\right\}
. In other words, a bijection
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
extends naturally to an isomorphism from the finite subalgebra of
\mathbb{B}
generated by
\mathcal{B}
to the finite subalgebra of
\mathbb{C}
generated by
\mathcal{C}
, and we abuse notation by denoting this extension also by
\eta
. This abuse occurs in the statement of
(\dagger)
in the following theorem when speaking of the map
\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota
. In general we may not have
\iota(a)\in\mathcal{B}
(although we do have
\iota(a)=\bigvee\left\{b\in\mathcal{B}\colon b\leq\iota(a)\right\}
), so
\tilde{\eta}(\iota(a))
is only well-defined via this notational abuse.

Theorem 8.8
.

Suppose
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
This instance of the lifting problem has a solution if and only if the following condition holds:

(\dagger)

For every partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{A}
and every partition
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathbb{B}
that refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
, there is a partition
\mathcal{C}
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
refining
\eta[\mathcal{A}]
, and an isomorphism
\tilde{\eta}
from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
such that
\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota=\eta

\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
\iota
\eta
\tilde{\eta}

Roughly, this theorem asserts that
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
has a solution if and only if every finitary fragment
(\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle,\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle,\iota,\eta)
of it has a solution.

Proof.

The “only if” direction of the theorem is relatively easy. Suppose that
\bar{\eta}
is a solution to
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
.
To show that
(\dagger)
holds, fix a partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{A}
and a partition
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathbb{B}
that refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
.
Let
\mathcal{C}=\bar{\eta}[\mathcal{B}]
. Taking
\tilde{\eta}=\bar{\eta}
gives an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
by Lemma
8.7
, and because
\bar{\eta}
is a lifting of
\eta
, we have
\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota=\bar{\eta}\circ\iota=\eta
.

For the “if” direction, suppose
(\dagger)
holds. We aim to find a solution
\bar{\eta}
to
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
. The plan is to obtain
\bar{\eta}
by finding an eventually small, eventually dense sequence in
\mathbb{B}
and then applying Theorem
4.10
.

Let
\left\langle\mathcal{A}_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
be a sequence of partitions of
\mathbb{A}
satisfying the conclusion of Lemma
4.4
, and likewise let
\left\langle\mathcal{B}_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
be a sequence of partitions of
\mathbb{B}
satisfying the conclusion of Lemma
4.4
.
Furthermore, by thinning out the sequence
\left\langle\mathcal{B}_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
if necessary, we may (and do) assume that
\mathcal{B}_{n}
refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}_{n}]
for all
n
.

For each
n
, property
(\dagger)
gives us a partition
\mathcal{C}_{n}
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, with
\mathcal{C}_{n}\supseteq\eta[\mathcal{A}_{n}]
, and an isomorphism
\tilde{\eta}_{n}
from
\langle\mathcal{B}_{n},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{C}_{n},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
such that
\tilde{\eta}_{n}\circ\iota=\eta
.
Applying Lemma
8.5
, for each
n\in\omega
there is a diligent walk
\left\langle b^{n}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{B}_{n},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
such that
\tilde{\eta}_{n}
is the natural isomorphism associated with the walk
\left\langle b^{n}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.

The plan of the proof is to diagonalize across the sequences
\left\langle b^{n}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
in order to obtain a single sequence
\left\langle d_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{B}
. This diagonal sequence will be eventually small and eventually dense, so that it induces an embedding
\mathbb{B}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and the embedding it induces will be the desired solution
\bar{\eta}
to the lifting problem.

Not all the sequences
\left\langle b^{n}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
are used in our diagonalization, but only a selection of them.
In order to decide which sequences to use, we employ Lemma
8.6
.
Define a function
\chi:[\omega]^{2}\to\omega
as follows.
For every
k,\ell\in\omega
with
k<\ell
, define

\displaystyle\chi(k,\ell)=\sup\left\{j\leq k\colon\beta(b^{k}_{n})\approx_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}b^{\ell}_{n+1}\text{ for infinitely many }n\right\}.

Roughly, the idea motivating the definition of
\chi
is as follows.
In our diagonal sequence
\left\langle d_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
that we are planning to construct, we will have longs stretches of indices
i
where
d_{i}=b^{k}_{i}
, i.e., where the diagonal sequence just copies
\left\langle b^{k}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
, for some
k
.
Then at some point we stop copying
\left\langle b^{k}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
and “jump” to copying another sequence
\left\langle b^{\ell}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
with
\ell>k
.
In particular, for some
i\in\omega
(the jump time) our diagonal sequence will have
d_{i}=b^{k}_{i}
and then
d_{i+1}=b^{\ell}_{i+1}
.
In the end, Theorem
4.10
requires that we have
\left\langle\beta(d_{i})\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle d_{i+1}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.
Thus we need
\beta(d_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}d_{i+1}
for larger and larger
j
as we go along.
In some sense, the
j
in this expression is measuring the degree of error in the jump: the bigger the
j
, the less the error between
\beta(d_{i})
and
d_{i+1}
.
Because we cannot control these jump times very well in the diagonalization, we would like to have infinitely many opportunities to jump from one sequence to another with small error (large
j
).
The number
\chi(k,\ell)
tells us what error we can expect to see when jumping from
\left\langle b^{k}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
to
\left\langle b^{\ell}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.

In the parlance of Ramsey theory, this function
\chi
is a coloring
[\omega]^{2}\to\omega
.
We claim that all the monochromatic cliques for this coloring are finite.
To see this, fix
j\in\omega
, and suppose
C\subseteq\omega
is a clique with color
j
: i.e.,
\chi(k,\ell)=j
for all
k,\ell\in C
.
Let
j^{\prime}
be large enough that
\mathcal{B}_{j^{\prime}}
refines the partition generated by
\mathcal{B}_{j}\cup\left\{\beta(b)\colon b\in\mathcal{B}_{j}\right\}
.
We claim that
|C|\leq|\mathcal{B}_{j^{\prime}}|+1
.
Aiming for a contradiction, let
J=|\mathcal{B}_{j^{\prime}}|
and suppose that
|C|>J+1
.
For every
b\in\mathbb{B}
that is below
\mathcal{B}_{j^{\prime}}
, let
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b)
denote the (unique) member of
\mathcal{B}_{j^{\prime}}
with
b\leq\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b)
.
Because
k\geq{j^{\prime}}-1
for all
k\in C
(by our definition of
\chi
), this means there are distinct points
k_{0},k_{1},\dots,k_{J}\in C
such that
k_{i}\geq{j^{\prime}}
for all
i\leq J
.
For any given
k\geq{j^{\prime}}
and any
n
, there are only
|\mathcal{B}_{j^{\prime}}|=J
possibilities for
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k}_{n})
.
In particular, by the pigeonhole principle, at least two of the
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i}}_{n})
(where
0\leq i\leq J
) must be the same for every
n
.
Consequently, there are some
i,i^{\prime}\leq J
such that
\pi_{j}(b^{k_{i}}_{n})
and
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n})
are the same infinitely often.
But (assuming without loss of generality that
i<i^{\prime}
), this implies
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i}}_{n})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n+1})
for infinitely many
n
, because
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n+1})
for any
n
and
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i}}_{n+1})=\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n+1})
.
By our choice of
j^{\prime}
,
\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i}}_{n})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\pi_{j^{\prime}}(b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n+1})
implies
\beta(b^{k_{i}}_{n})\approx_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n+1}

Thus
\beta(b^{k_{i}}_{n})\approx_{\mathcal{B}_{j}}b^{k_{i^{\prime}}}_{n+1}
for infinitely many
n
, which means
\chi(k_{i},k_{i^{\prime}})>j
, a contradiction.

As all the monochromatic cliques for
\chi
are finite, Lemma
8.6
gives us an increasing sequence
\left\langle n_{k}\colon k\in\omega\right\rangle
such that
\chi(n_{k},n_{k+1})>k
for all
k
.

Next we define a sequence
J_{0},J_{1},J_{2},\dots
of integers (our jump times) by recursion.
Let
J_{0}=0
, and given some
J_{k}
,
k\in\omega
, choose
J_{k+1}
such that

\circ

J_{k+1}>J_{k}
,

\circ

for every
b\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{k}}
, there is some
i\in[J_{k},J_{k+1})
such that
b^{n_{k}}_{i}=b
,

\circ

\beta\big(b^{n_{k}}_{J_{k}-1}\big)\approx_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}b^{n_{k+1}}_{J_{k}}
.

To see that some such
J_{k+1}
exists: the first condition is satisfied by all sufficiently large choices of
J_{k+1}
; the second condition is as well, because the sequence
\left\langle b^{n_{k}}_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk and therefore contains each member of
\mathcal{B}_{n_{k}}
infinitely many times; the third condition is satisfied infinitely often, because
\chi(n_{k},n_{k+1})>k
.

Define the diagonal sequence
\left\langle d_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{B}
by setting

d_{i}\,=\,b^{n_{k}}_{i}\quad\text{ whenever $J_{k}\leq i<J_{k+1}$}.

The sequence
\left\langle d_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is eventually small in
\mathbb{B}
by our choice of the
\mathcal{B}_{n}
’s, and because the
n_{k}
are increasing and each
b^{n_{k}}_{i}
is in
\mathcal{B}_{n_{k}}
.
The sequence is eventually dense by the condition that for every
b\in\mathcal{B}_{n_{k}}
there is some
i\in[J_{k},J_{k+1})
such that
b^{n_{k}}_{i}=b
. By our choice of the
\mathcal{B}_{n}
’s, this implies that for any given
b\in\mathcal{B}
, if
k
is sufficiently large then there is some
d_{i}\leq b
with
J_{k}\leq i<J_{k+1}
.

Finally, we claim
\left\langle\beta(d_{i})\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle d_{i+1}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be a partition of
\mathbb{B}
, and fix
K
large enough that
\mathcal{B}_{K}
refines the partition generated by
\mathcal{V}\cup\left\{\beta(b)\colon b\in\mathcal{V}\right\}
.
If
i\neq J_{\ell}-1
for any
\ell
, then there are some
k,j
such that
d_{i}=b^{k}_{j}
and
d_{i+1}=b^{k}_{j+1}
.
But
\langle b^{k}_{j}:\,j\in\omega\rangle
is is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{B}_{k},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
, and so has
b^{k}_{j}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}b^{k}_{j+1}
for all
j
.
Thus
d_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}b_{i+1}
. If
k\geq K
, then this implies
\beta(d_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}d_{i+1}
. Thus
\beta(d_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}d_{i+1}
for sufficiently large
i
, provided that
i\neq J_{\ell}-1
for any
\ell
.
On the other hand, suppose
i=J_{\ell}-1
for some
\ell\geq K
. Then
\beta(d_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{B}_{\ell}}d_{i+1}
by the third condition listed above in our definition of the
J_{k}
. Because
\ell\geq K
,
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}
refines
\mathcal{V}
, and this implies
\beta(d_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}d_{i+1}
. Thus, in either case,
\beta(d_{i})\approx_{\mathcal{V}}d_{i+1}
for all sufficiently large
i
.
Because
\mathcal{V}
was an arbitrary partition of
\mathbb{B}
, this shows
\left\langle\beta(d_{i})\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle\approx\left\langle d_{i+1}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
as claimed.

Applying Theorem
4.10
,
\left\langle d_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
induces an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. Call this embedding
\bar{\eta}
. We claim that
\bar{\eta}
is a lifting of
\eta
.

Fix
a\in\mathbb{A}
. There is some
k\in\omega
such that
\mathcal{A}_{k}
refines
\{a,\mathbf{1}-a\}
, and this implies
a=\bigvee\left\{c\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\colon c\leq a\right\}
. In fact
a=\bigvee\left\{c\in\mathcal{A}_{\ell}\colon c\leq a\right\}
for all
\ell\geq k
because
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
refines
\mathcal{A}_{k}
.
If
\ell\geq k
, then
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}
refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}_{\ell}]
, and thus if
d\in\mathcal{B}_{\ell}
then
\iota(a)=\bigvee\left\{d\in\mathcal{B}_{\ell}\colon d\leq\iota(a)\right\}
.
Consequently, for all
\ell\geq k
,

\eta(a)\,=\,\tilde{\eta}_{\ell}\circ\iota(a)\,=\,\tilde{\eta}_{\ell}(\iota(a))\,=\,[\{i:\,b^{\ell}_{i}\leq\iota(a)\}].

But if
i\geq J_{k}
, then
d_{i}=\pi_{\ell}(b^{n_{\ell}}_{i})
for some
\ell\geq k
, and it follows that
\left\{i\geq J_{k}\colon\pi_{\ell}(b^{n_{\ell}}_{i})\leq\iota(a)\right\}=\left\{i\geq J_{k}\colon b^{n_{\ell}}_{i}\leq\iota(a)\right\}
. Therefore

\eta(a)\,=\,[\{i:\,b^{\ell}_{i}\leq\iota(a)\}]\,=\,[\left\{i\colon d_{i}\leq\iota(a)\right\}]\,=\,\bar{\eta}(\iota(a)).

(This last equality applies the abuse of notation mentioned before the statement of the theorem.)
As
a
was arbitrary, this shows
\eta=\bar{\eta}\circ\iota
as claimed.
∎

Of course, one could prove an analogous result concerning the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
However, we have no need to do so. The purpose of Theorem
8.8
is that it brings us one step closer to proving the Lifting Lemma. We have already proved that an “inverse” version of the Lifting Lemma (Lemma
6.3
) follows from the original version, so there is no need for an inverse version of Theorem
8.8
.

The rest of this section is not part of the proof of Lemma
6.2
, and the reader who wishes to may skip it without losing the thread of the argument.

The main theorem of
[
6
]
, translated from the topological to the algebraic category, states that every incompressible dynamical system of size
\leq\!\aleph_{1}
embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
(the conclusion of the vacuously true Theorem
3.8
.)
To end this section, we derive this theorem as a relatively straightforward consequence of Theorem
8.8
.

The proof shows how elementarity can be used, in combination with Theorem
8.8
, to solve certain instances of the lifting problem, just as in Lemma
6.2
.
The difference is that the following lemma requires
\iota
to be elementary, whereas Lemma
6.2
places the burden on
\eta
instead.

Lemma 8.9
.

Suppose
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
If
\iota
is an elementary embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, then condition
(\dagger)
from Theorem
8.8
is satisfied, and this instance of the lifting problem has a solution.

Proof.

Suppose
\iota
is an elementary embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
and let
\mathcal{B}
be a partition of
\mathbb{B}
that refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
.
We aim to show that condition
(\dagger)
from Theorem
8.8
holds.

Let
\varphi
be the first-order sentence about
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
obtained as the conjunction of all true assertions of the following form:

\circ

\bigvee\mathcal{B}=\mathbf{1}
and
b\wedge b^{\prime}=\mathbf{0}
whenever
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
and
b\neq b^{\prime}
,

\circ

\bigvee\iota[\mathcal{A}]=\mathbf{1}
and
b\wedge b^{\prime}=\mathbf{0}
whenever
b,b^{\prime}\in\iota[\mathcal{A}]
and
b\neq b^{\prime}
,

\circ

b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}b^{\prime}
for some
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
or some
b,b^{\prime}\in\iota[\mathcal{A}]
,

\circ

\neg(b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}b^{\prime})
for some
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
or some
b,b^{\prime}\in\iota[\mathcal{A}]
,

\circ

b\leq b^{\prime}
for some
b\in\mathcal{B}
and
b^{\prime}\in\iota[\mathcal{A}]
.

In other words,
\varphi
asserts that
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
and
\mathcal{B}
are partitions of
\mathbb{B}
, it describes precisely the digraphs
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\iota[\mathcal{A}],\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
, and it records which members of
\mathcal{B}
are below which members of
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
.

Fix an enumeration
\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{m}\}
of
\mathcal{A}
and an enumeration
\mathcal{B}=\{b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{B}
.
Let
\psi(\iota(a_{1}),\dots,\iota(a_{m}),x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\beta)
be the formula about
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
obtained from
\varphi
by replacing every instance of
b_{i}
with the variable
x_{i}
, and let
\bar{\psi}
be the formula

\exists x_{1}\exists x_{2}\dots\exists x_{n}\psi(\iota(a_{1}),\dots,\iota(a_{m}),x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\beta).

This is a true assertion about
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, because
\psi(\iota(a_{1}),\dots,\iota(a_{m}),b_{1},\dots,b_{n},\beta)
is a true assertion about
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
.

Now consider the formula

\exists x_{1}\exists x_{2}\dots\exists x_{n}\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\alpha)

about
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
obtained from
\bar{\psi}
by replacing every instance of
\iota(a_{i})
with
a_{i}
and every instance of
\beta
with
\alpha
.
Because
\iota
is an elementary emedding, this formula must be true in
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
, as the corresponding formula (the original
\bar{\psi}
) is true in
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
.
Hence there exist some
\tilde{b}_{1},\tilde{b}_{2},\dots,\tilde{b}_{n}\in\mathbb{A}
such that

\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle\models\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},\tilde{b}_{1},\dots,\tilde{b}_{n},\alpha).

Define
\tilde{\eta}:\mathcal{B}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
by setting
\tilde{\eta}(b_{i})=\eta(\tilde{b}_{i})
for all
i\leq n
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=\{\tilde{b}_{i}:\,i\leq n\}
and let
\mathcal{C}=\tilde{\eta}[\tilde{\mathcal{B}}]
.
Observe that
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
is a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, because

\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},\tilde{b}_{1},\dots,\tilde{b}_{n},\alpha)
asserts that it is.
Similarly,
\langle\tilde{\mathcal{B}},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is isomorphic to
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
refines
\mathcal{A}
, and the natural map
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{A}
matches the natural map
\mathcal{B}\to\iota[\mathcal{A}]
(in the sense that the former sends
\tilde{b}_{i}
to
a_{j}
if and only if the latter sends
b_{i}
to
\iota(a_{j})
).
Because
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
is a partition of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathcal{C}=\eta[\tilde{\mathcal{B}}]
is a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and
\eta
is an isomorphism from
\langle\tilde{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\eta[\tilde{\mathcal{B}}],\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
by Lemma
8.7
. It follows that
\tilde{\eta}
is an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\eta[\tilde{\mathcal{B}}],\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.
Furthermore, because the natural map
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{A}
matches the natural map
\mathcal{B}\to\iota[\mathcal{A}]
, we have
\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota(a)=\eta(a)
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

In other words, this map
\tilde{\eta}
witnesses that
(\dagger)
is satisfied for
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
As
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
were arbitrary partitions,
(\dagger)
holds.
∎

Theorem 8.10
.

Every incompressible dynamical system of size
\aleph_{1}
embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.

Proof.

We prove the theorem first for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Every countable incompressible dynamical system embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
by Theorem
7.9
, so it suffices to consider systems of size
\aleph_{1}
.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
be an incompressible dynamical system with
|\mathbb{A}|=\aleph_{1}
.
Applying the downward Löwenheim-Skolem theorem
\omega_{1}
times, there is an increasing sequence
\left\langle\mathbb{A}_{\beta}\colon\beta<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of countable subalgebras of
\mathbb{A}
such that

\circ

\langle\mathbb{A}_{\beta},\alpha\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
for all
\beta<\omega_{1}
,

\circ

\mathbb{A}_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\xi<\beta}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
whenever
\beta
is a limit ordinal, and

\circ

\bigcup_{\beta<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{A}_{\beta}=\mathbb{A}
.

We now construct, via transfinite recursion, a sequence
\left\langle\eta_{\beta}\colon\beta<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of maps such that each
\eta_{\beta}
is an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\beta},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and the
\eta_{\beta}
are coherent in the sense that
\eta_{\beta}=\eta_{\gamma}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}_{\beta}
whenever
\beta<\gamma
.

To begin, fix an embedding
\eta_{0}
from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. (Some such embedding exists by Theorem
7.9
, and because elementarity implies
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\alpha\rangle
is incompressible.) At a limit stage
\beta
of the recursion, let
\eta_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\xi<\beta}\eta_{\xi}
. This is an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\beta},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, because the
\eta_{\xi}
are coherent embeddings and
\mathbb{A}_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\xi<\beta}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
.

For the successor step, suppose
\gamma=\beta+1
and suppose that we already have an embedding
\eta_{\beta}
from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\beta},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. Let
\iota
denote the inclusion map
\mathbb{A}_{\beta}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{A}_{\gamma}
.
Then
(\langle\mathbb{A}_{\beta},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{A}_{\gamma},\alpha\rangle,\iota,\eta_{\beta})
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and furthermore
\iota
is an elementary embedding. By Lemma
8.9
, there is an embedding
\eta_{\gamma}
from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\gamma},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
such that
\eta_{\gamma}\circ\iota=\eta_{\beta}
. Because
\iota
is the inclusion map
\mathbb{A}_{\beta}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{A}_{\gamma}
, this last condition just means that
\eta_{\beta}=\eta_{\gamma}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}_{\beta}
, i.e., the coherence of the maps is preserved.

In the end, let
\eta=\bigcup_{\beta<\omega_{1}}\eta_{\beta}
. Because the
\eta_{\beta}
are coherent embeddings and
\mathbb{A}=\bigcup_{\beta<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{A}_{\beta}
,
\eta
is an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.

It remains to show that we can also obtain an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
Of course one could repeat the above proof,
mutatis mutandis
, but this is not necessary.
Recall that
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is incompressible if and only if
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
is. Thus, by the preceding argument, there is an embedding
\eta^{\prime}
from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha^{-1}\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
But this implies that
\eta^{\prime}
is also an embedding from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
∎

Corollary 8.11
.

Assuming the Continuum Hypothesis,
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
embeds in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
and vice versa.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
8.10
, because
\mathsf{CH}
implies
\left\lvert\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\right\rvert=\aleph_{1}
, and both
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are incompressible.
∎

As noted in the introduction, the conclusion of this corollary is independent of
\mathsf{ZFC}
: assuming
\mathsf{OCA+MA}
, there is no embedding from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
or from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. This was proved in
[
6
]
as a relatively straightforward corollary to the extensive work of Farah in
[
12
]
on how
\mathsf{OCA+MA}
restricts the continuous self-maps of
\omega^{*}
.

9.
The Lifting Lemma, part 4: polarization

The Lifting Lemma, also known as Lemma
6.2
, guarantees that an instance
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
of the lifting problem has a solution, assuming the image of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
under
\eta
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. This is similar to Lemma
8.9
above, except that the Lifting Lemma puts no conditions whatsoever on the
\iota
part of the lifting problem: all the work is done by the map
\eta
instead.

In the previous section we formulated a condition
(\dagger)
that is necessary and sufficient for the existence of a solution to an instance of the lifting problem.
In this section we use
(\dagger)
to provide a sufficient condition for a solution.
Unlike
(\dagger)
, this condition is a property of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
only (called “polarization”), and in particular it makes no mention of
\iota
.

After this section, our plan for completing the proof of Lemma
6.2
is as follows. In Section 10 we prove a combinatorial statement more or less equivalent to the fact that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is polarized. Then in Section 11, we show that polarization passes to elementary substructures, because it is expressible in a sufficiently first-order way.
This means that the hypotheses of the Lifting Lemma imply
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is polarized, which (by the results proved in this section) suffices to get a solution to the lifting problem.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
be an incompressible dynamical system and let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
. A
virtual refinement
of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
is a pair
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
, where
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
is a strongly connected digraph and
\phi
is an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.

A partition
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{A}

realizes

(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
refines
\mathcal{A}
and there is an isomorphism
\psi
from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
such that
\pi\circ\psi=\phi
, where
\pi
is the natural map from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{A}
.
☕

The following lemma says virtual refinements generalize actual refinements.
This lemma has Lemma
7.4
as a special case (by taking
\iota=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{A}}
).

Lemma 9.2
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
are dynamical systems, and
\iota
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
. Given a partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{A}
and any partition
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathbb{B}
that refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
, define the map

\phi(b)\,=\,\text{the unique member of $\mathcal{A}$ with }b\leq\iota(a).

Then
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle)
is a virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Note that
\phi
is well-defined, because
\mathcal{B}
refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
, and is clearly a surjection
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
.
If
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
and
a\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}a^{\prime}
, then
\iota(a)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\iota(a^{\prime})
(because
\iota
is an embedding), and this implies that
b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}b^{\prime}
for some
b\leq\iota(a)
and
b^{\prime}\leq\iota(a^{\prime})
. In other words, there are
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
such that
\phi(b)=a
,
\phi(b^{\prime})=a^{\prime}
, and
b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}b^{\prime}
.
On the other hand, it is clear that
b\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}b^{\prime}
implies
\phi(b)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\phi(b^{\prime})
.
∎

Henceforth, if the conditions of this lemma hold then the function

\phi(b)\,=\,\text{the unique member of $\mathcal{A}$ with }b\leq\iota(a)

is called the
natural epimorphism
from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.

Definition 9.3
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
,
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
, and

\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and suppose that

\phi
and
\psi
are epimorphisms from
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and from
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, respectively.
These two epimorphisms are
compatible
if there is a strongly connected digraph

\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
, and there are epimorphisms
\bar{\phi}
and
\bar{\psi}
from

\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
onto

\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
, respectively, such that
\phi\circ\bar{\phi}=\psi\circ\bar{\psi}
.

\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
\psi
\phi
\bar{\psi}
\bar{\phi}

Otherwise
\phi
and
\psi
are
incompatible
.
By extension, if
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is a dynamical system and
\mathcal{A}
is a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, then two virtual refinements
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle)
and
(\psi,\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle)
of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
are (in)compatible if the epimorphisms
\phi
and
\psi
are (in)compatible.
☕

Note that this definition requires
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
to be strongly connected.
In fact, without the requirement that
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected, any two digraph epimorphisms would be compatible.

To see this, let
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
and
\psi
be epimorphisms from
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
, respectively, onto some
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
Define
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
to be the so-called “fibered product”, also known as the “pullback”, of
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
, with
\bar{\phi}
and
\bar{\psi}
being the standard projections.
(The fibered product of
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
is defined to have vertex set
\left\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}\colon\phi(u)=\psi(v)\right\}
. The edge relation is defined by setting
(u,v)\to(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
if and only if
u\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}u^{\prime}
and
v\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}v^{\prime}
. It is a good exercise to check that the standard projection maps for this fibered product really are epimorphisms onto its factors. For further details, see, e.g., in
[
2
]
or
[
23
]
.)
However, the fibered product of two strongly connected digraphs need not be strongly connected.
In particular, the fibered product of
\langle\mathcal{U},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{U}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{V},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{V}}\,$}}\rangle
may not be eligible to witness their compatibility in the sense of Definition
9.3
.

As we shall soon see, the polarization property, which is at the center of the proof of the Lifting Lemma, depends on the incompatibility of certain digraph epimorphisms. In light of the previous paragraph, we should expect that any proof of the incompatibility of two epimorphisms makes essential use of the strong connectedness requirement for
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
are incompressible dynamical systems, and
\iota
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, and let
\mathcal{B}
be a partition of
\mathbb{B}
refining
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
.
Then for any partition
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{A}
refining
\mathcal{A}
, the natural map from
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle

and the natural epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle

are compatible.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be any partition of
\mathbb{B}
refining both
\mathcal{B}
and
\iota[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]
.
We claim that the digraph
\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
, along with the appropriate natural epimorphisms, witnesses the
p
-compatibility of the two virtual refinements in question.
In short, the proof works because the composition of two natural maps is again a natural map (with the appropriate domain and co-domain).

More precisely, let
\phi
denote the natural map from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{A}
, and
let
\psi
denote the natural epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.
Let
\bar{\phi}
denote the natural epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
and
let
\bar{\psi}
denote the natural map from
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{B}
.
The definitions of these various maps give us that both
\phi\circ\bar{\phi}
and
\psi\circ\bar{\psi}
are equal to the function
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
given by

b\,\mapsto\,\text{the unique member of $\mathcal{A}$ with }b\leq\iota(a),

in other words, the natural epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.
∎
