In other words, this lemma states that a virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
that comes from an embedding (in the way that
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
does in the statement of the lemma) is always compatible with actual refinements of
\mathcal{A}
.

Similarly, when two virtual refinements come from embeddings that form a commutative triangle, in the sense of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and Lemma
6.2
, then the two corresponding virtual refinements are compatible:

Observation 9.5
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
,
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
, and
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
are dynamical systems, and
\iota
and
\eta
are embeddings of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
, respectively.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, let
\mathcal{B}
be a partition of
\mathbb{B}
refining
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a partition of
\mathbb{C}
refining
\eta[\mathcal{A}]
.
If there is an embedding
\bar{\eta}
from
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{C},\gamma\rangle
with
\bar{\eta}\circ\iota=\eta
,
then the natural epimorphisms from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
and from

\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\gamma}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
are compatible.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be a partition of
\mathbb{C}
that refines all three of
\mathcal{C}
,
\bar{\eta}[\mathcal{B}]
, and
\eta[\mathcal{A}^{p}]
.
Let
\bar{\phi}
be the natural epimorphism (induced by
\bar{\eta}
) from
\langle\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\gamma}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
. Let
\bar{\psi}
be the natural projection
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
.

Just as in the proof of Lemma
9.4
,
\langle\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\gamma}\,$}}\rangle
, along with these various maps, witnesses compatibility. As before, this works essentially because the composition of natural maps, when defined, is another natural map.
∎

This observation suggests a reason that an instance
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
might fail to have a solution: the reason could be that there are partitions
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
, and
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that (together with the natural maps corresponding to
\iota
and
\eta
) the digraphs
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
are incompatible virtual refinements of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.

Indeed, this is precisely what happens in the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
denote the dynamical systems constructed there.
There is a partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbb{A}
such that the digraph
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
looks like this:

U
V
W

There is a partition of
\mathbb{B}
that refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
(separating the points
a
,
b
,
c
, and
d
into four disjoint clopen sets) with the following picture:

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Finally, consider the embedding
\eta
of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
described in the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
This embedding is described via an eventually small, eventually dense sequence
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
.
Consider the following partition of
\omega
(where
U
,
V
, and
W
refer to the members of
\mathcal{A}
):

A\,=\,\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\in U\right\},\ \quad B\,=\,\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\in W\right\},

C\,=\,\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\in V\text{ and if $m>n$ is minimal with $a_{m}\notin V$, }a_{m}\in W\right\},

D\,=\,\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}\in V\text{ and if $m>n$ is minimal with $a_{m}\notin V$, }a_{m}\in U\right\}.

This partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
refines
\eta[\mathcal{A}]
and is pictured as follows:

A
B
C
D
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Thus we have two different virtual refinements of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
, arising from these partitions
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathbb{B}
and
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, respectively, and their associated natural epimorphisms:

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

But these two refinements are incompatible.
(To see this, let
m
denote the higher of the two vertices in the middle of
\mathcal{B}
, and let
r
denote the vertex on the right of
\mathcal{B}
.
Suppose, aiming for a contradiction, that
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
,
\bar{\phi}
, and
\bar{\psi}
witness the compatibility of
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.
Because
\bar{\phi}
is surjective,
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(m)\neq\emptyset
and
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(r)\neq\emptyset
.
Because
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, there is a walk
\langle w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
from
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(r)
to
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(m)
and then back to
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(r)
.
By truncating this walk at the front and back if necessary, we may assume, without loss of generality, this walk begins in
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(r)
, then moves to
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(m)
, and then returns to
\bar{\phi}^{-1}(r)
, without visiting any other regions of
\mathcal{W}
.
Because
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism,
\langle\bar{\psi}(w_{0}),\bar{\psi}(w_{1}),\dots,\bar{\psi}(w_{n})\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.
Similarly,
\langle\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{0}),\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{1}),\dots,\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{n})\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.
By our assumptions about the “shape” of the walk in
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
, this walk in
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
begins at the rightmost vertex, then moves to the middle, and then moves back to the right.
Because
\bar{\phi}\circ\phi=\bar{\psi}\circ\psi
, this implies that the walk
\langle\bar{\psi}(w_{0}),\bar{\psi}(w_{1}),\dots,\bar{\psi}(w_{n})\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
begins at the rightmost vertex of
\mathcal{C}
, moves to one/both of the middle vertices for a while, and then returns to the right.
But there are no walks like this in
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
. Contradiction!)

Thus Observation
9.5
gives us another way of seeing what was proved differently in Theorem
5.1
:
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
has no solution.

Our definition of “polarized” is motivated by this observation.
It says roughly that every virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
either is realized by a further refinement of
\mathbb{A}
, or is incompatible with a further refinement of
\mathbb{A}
.

Definition 9.6
.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
be an incompressible dynamical system, let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, and let
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
be a virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.
This virtual refinement
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
is
polarized
if either

(1)

there is a partition
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{A}
that realizes
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
, or

(2)

there is a partition
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{A}
refining
\mathcal{A}
such that
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
and
(\pi,\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle)
are incompatible, where
\pi
denotes the natural projection from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
onto
\mathcal{A}
.

By extension, the dynamical system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is
polarized
if every virtual refinement of every partition of
\mathbb{A}
polarized.
☕

For example, finite dynamical systems are never polarized. To see this, suppose
\mathbb{A}
is finite and
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is an incompressible dynamical system. Then
\mathbb{A}
has a maximal partition
\mathcal{A}
(the set of atoms of
\mathbb{A}
). If
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
is any virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
with
|\mathcal{V}|>|\mathcal{A}|
, then both options from Definition
9.6
are impossible, because
\mathcal{A}
has no proper refinements.

This simple example suggests a useful intuition for polarization, which is that every partition of
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
must have many refinements. Observe that polarization is an “existence of witnesses” property: whenever a virtual refinement
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
of some
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
cannot be realized, there is a refinement
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}
that witnesses this in a strong way, namely by being incompatible with that virtual refinement.

For a less trivial example of a non-polarized dynamical system, consider the system
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
from the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
The fact that
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is not polarized follows from Theorem
9.7
below.

We end this section by verifying what was promised at its beginning: if the
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
in an instance of the lifting problem is polarized, then that instance of the lifting problem has a solution.

Theorem 9.7
.

Suppose
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
If
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is polarized, then this instance of the lifting problem has a solution.

Proof.

In order to prove that
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
has a solution, it suffices to prove that condition
(\dagger)
from Theorem
8.8
holds.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, and let
\mathcal{B}
be a partition of
\mathcal{B}
that refines
\iota[\mathcal{A}]
.

Let
\phi
denote the natural epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
. By Lemma
9.2
,

(\phi,\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle)
is a virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
be any partition of
\mathbb{A}
refining
\mathcal{A}
, and let
\tilde{\pi}
denote the natural map
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}
. Then
(\tilde{\pi},\langle\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle)
is a virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
by Lemma
7.4
, and this virtual refinement is compatible with
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle)
, by Lemma
9.4
.
In other words, case
(2)
of Definition
9.6
is not true of
\mathcal{A}
and
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle)
.
Because
\mathcal{A}
is a polarized partition, this means that case
(1)
must be true: there is a partition
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{A}
that realizes
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle)
.
Letting
\pi
denote the natural map from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
onto
\mathcal{A}
, this means that there is an isomorphism
\phi^{\prime}
from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
such that
\pi\circ\phi^{\prime}=\phi
.

The remainder of the argument imitates the proof of Lemma
8.9
, but with the members of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
playing the part that the
\tilde{b}_{i}
played in that proof.

Define
\tilde{\eta}(b)=\eta\circ\phi^{\prime}(b)
for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
, and let
\mathcal{C}=\tilde{\eta}[\mathcal{B}]
.
Because
\phi^{\prime}
is a digraph isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
and
\eta
is a digraph isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.9}{$\,{}_{\alpha}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
(by Lemma
8.7
), we see that
\tilde{\eta}
is an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\,{}_{\beta}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.
Also,
\mathcal{C}=\tilde{\eta}[\mathcal{B}]=\eta[\phi^{\prime}[\mathcal{B}]]=\eta[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]
; because
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
refines
\mathcal{A}
, this implies that
\mathcal{C}
refines
\eta[\mathcal{A}]
.
It remains to check that
\eta=\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Recall that
\iota(a)
may not be in
\mathcal{B}
(the domain of
\tilde{\eta}
), but extending (or abusing) our notation in the natural way, we define

\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota(a)=\bigvee\left\{\tilde{\eta}(b)\colon b\in\mathcal{B}\text{ and }b\leq\iota(a)\right\}
.
Similarly, (with the same notational abuse) we define
\phi^{\prime}\circ\iota(a)=\bigvee\left\{\phi^{\prime}(b)\colon b\in\mathcal{B}\text{ and }b\leq\iota(a)\right\}
.
Because
\pi\circ\phi^{\prime}=\phi
and
\pi(\phi^{\prime}(b))=a
if and only if
\phi^{\prime}(b)\leq a
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}\circ\iota(a)

\displaystyle\,=\,\textstyle\bigvee\left\{\phi^{\prime}(b)\colon b\in\mathcal{B}\text{ and }b\leq\iota(a)\right\}

\displaystyle\textstyle\,=\,\bigvee\left\{\phi^{\prime}(b)\colon b\in\mathcal{B}\text{ and }\phi^{\prime}(b)\leq a\right\}

\displaystyle\textstyle\,=\,\bigvee\left\{c\colon c\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\text{ and }c\leq a\right\}\,=\,a.

Consequently,
\eta(a)=\eta\circ\phi^{\prime}\circ\iota(a)=\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota(a).

As
a
was an arbitrary member of
\mathcal{A}
, this shows that
\eta=\tilde{\eta}\circ\iota
as required.
∎

10.
The Lifting Lemma, part 5: the heart of the lemma

In this section, we prove a graph-theoretic result that is the combinatorial heart and soul of the Lifting Lemma, and indeed, of the entire paper.

Definition 10.1
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
be a function
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
(for instance, an epimorphism).

A length-
k
walk
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle

projects
onto a length-
k
walk
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
(via
\phi
) if
\phi(b_{i})=a_{i}
for all
i\leq k
.
In the same way, an infinite walk
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle

projects
(via
\phi
) onto an infinite walk
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
if
\phi(b_{n})=a_{n}
for all
n
.

Likewise,
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle

almost projects
(via
\phi
) onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
if
\phi(b_{n})=a_{n}
for all sufficiently large values of
n
.
☕

The main theorem of this section states:

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
If
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, then either

(1)

there is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
, or

(2)

there is a strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
, and an epimorphism
\psi
from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, and a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
, such that
\psi
and
\phi
are incompatible.

These two alternatives are mutually exclusive (though not obviously), and they correspond roughly to the two alternatives in Definition
9.6
.

The proof of Theorem
10.2
naturally divides itself into two major stages. In the first stage, we prove a weaker version of the theorem, where the “diligent walk” from alternative
(1)
is replaced by the weaker “infinite walk” instead.
This is stated as Theorem
10.8
below.
The second stage of the proof extends this weaker version of the theorem to the full version.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, and suppose
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
There is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
if and only if for every
k\in\omega
there is a length-
k
walk
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
.

Proof.

The “only if” direction is obvious: if
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
, then
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle
projects onto
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
for every
k\in\omega
.

The “if” direction is a consequence of Kőnig’s Tree Lemma. To see this, consider the set
\mathcal{T}
of all finite walks
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that project onto the initial segment
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
of
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
with the same length.
When ordered in the natural way, by end-extension, this set
\mathcal{T}
is a tree. Because
\mathcal{B}
is finite, this tree is finitely branching (specifically, each member of
\mathcal{T}
has at most
|\mathcal{B}|
immediate successors).

Now suppose that for every
k\in\omega
there is a length-
k
walk
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
.
This implies
\mathcal{T}
is infinite.
Consequently, Kőnig’s Tree Lemma implies that
\mathcal{T}
has an infinite branch

\big\langle\emptyset,\langle b_{0}\rangle,\langle b_{0},b_{1}\rangle,\langle b_{0},b_{1},b_{2}\rangle,\dots\big\rangle.

The union of this branch is an infinite sequence
\langle b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\dots\rangle
in
\mathcal{B}
, and this sequence is an infinite walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
∎

Definition 10.4
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
The
state space
of
\phi
is a digraph
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\phi},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\rangle
, where

\mathcal{S}^{\phi}\,=\,\left\{\mathrm{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\colon\mathrm{S}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(a)\text{ for some }a\in\mathcal{A}\right\},

and given
\mathrm{S}_{0},\mathrm{S}_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
, we define
\mathrm{S}_{0}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\mathrm{S}_{1}
if and only if there is some edge
(a_{0},a_{1})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
such that
\mathrm{S}_{0}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(a_{0})
and
\mathrm{S}_{1}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(a_{1})
, and

\mathrm{S}_{1}\,=\,\left\{y\in\phi^{-1}(a_{1})\colon\exists x\in\mathrm{S}_{0}\text{ such that }x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}y\right\}.

In this case we say that the edge
(a_{0},a_{1})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}

induces
the edge
(\mathrm{S}_{0},\mathrm{S}_{1})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}
.
If
\mathrm{S}\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
, there is some
a\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\phi[\mathrm{S}]=\{a\}
.
The
natural map

\Pi_{\phi}:\mathcal{S}^{\phi}\setminus\{\emptyset\}\to\mathcal{A}
sends each
\mathrm{S}\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
to the unique member of
\phi[\mathrm{S}]
.
☕

Drawn below are two examples of the state space
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\phi},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\rangle
associated to some epimorphism
\phi
from some
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
onto some
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
To clarify the construction, we have labelled the members of
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
, and labelled each member of
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}
with the member of
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
that induces it (except for the arrow
\emptyset\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\emptyset
, which is induced by every edge in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
).

d

\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
\phi\,,\,\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\phi},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\rangle

d

The natural map
\Pi_{\phi}:\mathcal{S}^{\phi}\setminus\{\emptyset\}\to\mathcal{A}
is indicated by the positions of the vertices of
\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
in the picture (although we have omitted the dotted circles as in the drawings of
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
).

Observe that
\Pi_{\phi}
is an epimorphism from the digraph
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\phi}\setminus\{\emptyset\},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
Observe also that
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\phi},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\rangle
is not strongly connected, because there is no walk from the empty state
\emptyset
to any other vertex of
\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
.
But even the sub-digraph
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\phi}\setminus\{\emptyset\},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\rangle
need not be strongly connected: for instance, in the second example illustrated above, there is no path from a size-
1
member of
\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
to a size-
2
member.

Definition 10.5
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
If
\bar{a}=\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, then we associate to
\bar{a}
an infinite sequence
\left\langle\mathrm{S}_{n}^{\bar{a}}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
, defined by taking

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k}\,=\,\Big\{b\in\mathcal{B}:\ \text{$b$ is the last member }

of a length-
k
walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle

\displaystyle\text{ that projects onto }\langle a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\rangle\Big\}

for all
k\in\omega
.
Each
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k}
is well-defined (i.e.,
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k}\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
) because
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(a_{k})
.
This sequence is the
state space sequence associated to
\bar{a}
.
☕

To motivate Definition
10.5
, suppose we have an epimorphism
\phi
from some strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
onto some other strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
. We wish to understand the following

Question
: Given an infinite walk
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, is there an

infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto it?

By Lemma
10.3
, this question is equivalent to asking whether there are arbitrarily long finite walks through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that project onto initial segments of
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
. The state space sequence is simply a convenient way of keeping track of what such walks might look like:
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k}
tells us the possibilities for
b_{k}
in such an infinite walk
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
, or at least, it tells us the possibilities not yet ruled out by looking at
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
. (Some possibilities may be ruled out later, if there is no extension to a longer walk that projects correctly.)

According to Lemma
10.3
, there is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
projecting onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
if and only if the state space sequence associated to
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
never reaches the empty state
\emptyset
.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
If
\bar{a}=\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, then the state space sequence associated to
\bar{a}
satisfies the following recursion:

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{0}

\displaystyle\,=\,\phi^{-1}(a_{0}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle\,=\,\left\{y\in\phi^{-1}(a_{k+1})\colon\exists x\in\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k}\text{ such that }x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}y\right\}.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{0}=\phi^{-1}(a_{0})
, because
\langle b\rangle
is a length-
0
walk projecting onto
\langle a_{0}\rangle
if and only if
\phi(b)=a_{0}
.
For the general step of the recursion, observe that
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k},b_{k+1}\rangle
is a length-
(k+1)
walk projecting onto
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a_{k+1}\rangle
if and only if
(1)

\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle
is a length-
k
walk projecting onto
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
,
(2)

b_{k}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}b_{k+1}
, and
(3)

\phi(b_{k+1})=a_{k+1}
. Thus
b\in\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k+1}
if and only if
\phi(b)=a_{k+1}
and there is some length-
k
walk
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle
projecting onto
\langle a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
such that
b_{k}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}b
.
∎

One immediate consequence of this lemma is that

\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{k+1}
for every
k
, and this edge in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}
is induced by the edge
(a_{k},a_{k+1})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
.
Consequently,
\left\langle\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}}_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
either is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\phi},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}\rangle
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Suppose that
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
is a strongly connected digraph, and let
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
be a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
Suppose that
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is a digraph with no isolated points, and let
\psi
be a function
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}
.
If there is a diligent walk
\left\langle c_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects via
\psi
onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
, then
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected and
\psi
is an epimorphism.

Proof.

Suppose
\left\langle c_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
. The existence of a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
implies
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, by Lemma
8.2
. To prove the lemma, we must show
\psi
is an epimorphism.
Fix some
N\in\omega
sufficiently large that if
n\geq N
then
\psi(c_{n})=a_{n}
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is diligent, there are infinitely many
n\in\omega
such that
a_{n}=a
; in particular,
a_{n}=a
for some
n\geq N
. Hence
\psi(c_{n})=a_{n}=a
, and as
a
was arbitrary, it follows that
\psi
is surjective.

Fix
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
c\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}c^{\prime}
. Because
\left\langle c_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is diligent, there is some
n\geq N
such that
c=c_{n}
and
c^{\prime}=c_{n+1}
. Because
n\geq N
, this implies
\psi(c)=a_{n}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}a_{n+1}=\psi(c^{\prime})
.

Lastly, fix
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
a\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}a^{\prime}
. Because
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is diligent, there is some
n\geq N
such that
a=a_{n}
and
a^{\prime}=a_{n+1}
. Because
n\geq N
, this implies
\psi(c_{n})=a
and
c_{n+1}=a^{\prime}
. But
c_{n}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}c_{n+1}
, so this shows there are
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\psi(c)=a
,
\psi(c^{\prime})=a^{\prime}
, and
c\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}c^{\prime}
.
∎

The following theorem is a weakening of Theorem
10.2
, which has “infinite walk” instead of “diligent walk” instead in alternative
(1)
. The proof of this theorem completes the first major stage of our proof of Theorem
10.2
.

Theorem 10.8
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
If
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, then either

(1)

there is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
, or

(2)

there is a strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
and an epimorphism
\psi
from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
such that
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
, and there is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, and let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
Fix an infinite walk
\bar{a}=\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
For each
m\in\omega
, let
\bar{a}_{m}=\left\langle a_{n+m}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
(i.e.,
\bar{a}_{m}
is the sequence
\bar{a}
with the first
m
entries removed).

To begin, suppose there is some
m\in\omega
such that
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k}\neq\emptyset
for all
k
.
Applying Lemma
10.3
, this implies there is an infinite walk
\left\langle b_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\bar{a}_{m}
.
Using the fact that
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, fix some length-
m
walk
\langle b^{\prime}_{0},b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime}_{m}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
such that
b^{\prime}_{m}=b_{0}
. Define a new walk
\langle\tilde{b}_{0},\tilde{b}_{1},\tilde{b}_{2},\dots\rangle
by concatenating these two walks:

\tilde{b}_{i}\,=\,\begin{cases}b_{i}^{\prime}&\text{ if }i\leq m,\\
b_{i-m}&\text{ if }i\geq m.\end{cases}

But then
\langle\tilde{b}_{0},\tilde{b}_{1},\tilde{b}_{2},\dots\rangle
is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\bar{a}
via
\phi
, because
\phi(b_{n})=a_{n}
whenever
n\geq m
.

This shows that if there is some
m\in\omega
such that
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k}\neq\emptyset
for all
k
, then alternative
(1)
from the statement of the theorem holds.
Thus, to prove the theorem, it suffices to show that
if this is not the case, then alternative
(2)
holds instead.

Let us suppose, then, that for every
m\in\omega
there is some
k>0
such that
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k}=\emptyset
.
For each
m
, let
k_{m}
denote the least number with this property, so that
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{i}\neq\emptyset
for all
i<k_{m}
, but
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k_{m}}=\emptyset
.

Because
\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
and
\mathcal{A}
are both finite, there are some particular
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\ \xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{S}^{\phi}}\,$}}
, and
\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
, and
a_{\text{in}},a_{\text{end}},a_{\text{out}}\in\mathcal{A}
, such that

\circ

\left\{\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{i}\colon i\leq k_{m}\right\}\,=\,\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

\circ

\left\{(\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{i},\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{i+1})\colon i<k_{m}\right\}\,=\,\mathcal{E}_{0}
,

\circ

\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{0}\,=\,\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}
,

\circ

a_{m-1}\,=\,a_{\text{in}}
,

\circ

a_{k_{m}}\,=\,a_{\text{end}}
,

\circ

a_{k_{m}+1}\,=\,a_{\text{out}}

for infinitely many
m>0
. (We exclude
m=0
from consideration so that
a_{m-1}
is well-defined.)
Let
D
denote the set of all
m>0
satisfying the six properties listed above.

We are now ready to describe the digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
and the epimorphism
\psi
that witness alternative
(2)
from the statement of the theorem.
First define
\mathcal{C}\,=\,\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{C}_{0}
. (We are assuming, without loss of generality, that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{C}_{0}=\emptyset
. This is true automatically unless the members of
\mathcal{A}
are labeled in an unreasonable way.)
Define the edge relation on
\mathcal{C}
as follows:

\displaystyle x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}y\quad\ \Longleftrightarrow\quad\

\displaystyle x,y\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}y\text{, or}

\displaystyle x,y\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\text{ and }(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}_{0}\text{, or}

\displaystyle x=a_{\text{in}}\text{ and }y=\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}\text{, or}

\displaystyle x=\emptyset\text{ and }y=a_{\mathrm{out}}.

\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
\bullet

a_{\text{in}}

\bullet

a_{\text{out}}

\langle\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}\rangle
\bullet

\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}

\bullet

\emptyset

Define
\psi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}
as follows:

\psi(x)=\begin{cases}x&\text{ if }x\in\mathcal{A},\\
\Pi_{\phi}(\mathrm{S})&\text{ if }\mathrm{S}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\setminus\{\emptyset\},\\
a_{\text{end}}&\text{ if }x=\emptyset.\end{cases}

This completes the definition of
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\psi
. To finish the proof, we need to show that
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, that
\psi
is an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, that there is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
projecting onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
via
\psi
, and that
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
.

We can verify the first three of these four things all at once by applying Lemma
10.7
. To do this, we must define a diligent walk
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
via
\psi
. We define this walk via recursion, obtaining at each stage not a single
c_{i}
, but a long piece of the walk.

To begin, recall that for every edge
(a,a^{\prime})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
, there are infinitely many
i\in\omega
such that
(a_{i},a_{i+1})=(a,a^{\prime})
. Fix some
m_{0}\in D
large enough that for every
(a,a^{\prime})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
, there is at least one
i+1<m_{0}
such that
(a_{i},a_{i+1})=(a,a^{\prime})
. Then define

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle\,=\,a_{i}\qquad\quad\ \ \,\text{for all }i<m_{0}\text{, and}

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{0}}}_{i-m_{0}}\qquad\text{whenever }m_{0}\leq i\leq m_{0}+k_{m_{0}}.

This completes the base step of the recursion.

Later stages of the recursion are similar. At stage
n
, suppose that there is some
m_{n-1}\in A
such that
c_{i}
is already defined for all
i\leq m_{n-1}+k_{m_{n-1}}
.
Fix some
m_{n}\in D
large enough that for every
(a,a^{\prime})\in\ \xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
there is some
i
with
m_{n-1}+k_{m_{n-1}}\leq i<m_{n}-1
such that
(a_{i},a_{i+1})=(a,a^{\prime})
. Then define

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle\,=\,a_{i}\qquad\quad\ \ \,\text{whenever }m_{n-1}+k_{m_{n-1}}\leq i<m_{n}\text{, and}

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{i-m_{n}}\qquad\text{whenever }m_{n}\leq i\leq m_{n}+k_{m_{n}}.

This completes the recursion.

Next, we check that
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
via
\psi
.

Every
i\in\omega
fits into one of the following four cases:

\circ

c_{i}=a_{i}
and
c_{i+1}=a_{i+1}
,

\circ

c_{i}=a_{m_{n}-1}=a_{\text{in}}
and
c_{i+1}=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{0}=\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}
for some
m_{n}\in D
,

\circ

c_{i}=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{j}
and
c_{i+1}=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{j+1}
for some
m_{n}\in D
and some
j<m_{n}
, or

\circ

c_{i}=\emptyset
and
c_{i+1}=a_{m_{n}+k_{m_{n}}+1}=a_{\text{out}}
for some
m_{n}\in D
.

In any of these four cases, it follows immediately from the definition of
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}
that
c_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}c_{i+1}
.
Hence
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
.

Furthermore, at stage
n
of the recursion we chose the new
c_{i}
’s (that is, those
c_{i}
for which
m_{n-1}+k_{m_{n-1}}\leq i\leq m_{n}+k_{m_{n}}
) so that:

\circ

for every
(a,a^{\prime})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}
there is some new
c_{i}
with
(c_{i},c_{i+1})=(a,a^{\prime})
,

\circ

for every
(\mathrm{S},\mathrm{S}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{E}_{0}
there is some new
c_{i}
with
(c_{i},c_{i+1})=(\mathrm{S},\mathrm{S}^{\prime})
,

\circ

there is some new
c_{i}
with
(c_{i},c_{i+1})=(a_{\text{in}},\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}})
, and

\circ

there is some new
c_{i}
with
(c_{i},c_{i+1})=(\emptyset,a_{\text{out}})
.

In other words, for every
(c,c^{\prime})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}
there is some new
c_{i}
with
(c_{i},c_{i+1})=(c,c^{\prime})
.
It follows that
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
.

For each
i\in\omega
, either
c_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
or
c_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
. If
c_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
then
\psi(c_{i})=a_{i}
by construction.
If
c_{i}=\mathrm{S}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
then either

\circ

c_{i}=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{i-m_{n}}
for some
n
with
m_{n}\leq i<m_{n}+k_{m_{n}}
, in which case we have
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{i-m_{n}}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{i-m_{n}}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(a_{i-m_{n}})
, which implies that
\psi(c_{i})=\Pi_{\phi}(\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{i-m_{n}})=a_{i}
; or

\circ

c_{i}=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m_{n}}}_{k_{m_{n}}}=\emptyset
for some
m_{n}\in D
, in which case
\psi(c_{i})=a_{\text{end}}=a_{m_{n}+k_{m_{n}}}=a_{i}
.

In either case,
\psi(c_{i})=a_{i}
. This holds for all
i\in\omega
.

By Lemma
10.7
,
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected,
\psi
is an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, and
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects via
\psi
onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.

It remains to show that
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
. Suppose, aiming for a contradiction, that
\psi
is compatible with
\phi
. That is, suppose that there is a strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
and epimorphisms
\bar{\phi}
and
\bar{\psi}
from
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
, respectively, such that
\phi\circ\bar{\phi}=\psi\circ\bar{\psi}
.

\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
\psi
\phi
\bar{\psi}
\bar{\phi}

Because
\bar{\psi}
is a surjection
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{C}
, the fibers
\bar{\psi}^{-1}(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}})
and
\bar{\psi}^{-1}(\emptyset)
are both nonempty. Using the fact that
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, fix a walk
\langle w_{0},w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
from some
w_{0}\in\bar{\psi}^{-1}(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}})
to some
w_{n}\in\bar{\psi}^{-1}(\emptyset)
.

Because
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism,
\langle\bar{\psi}(w_{0}),\bar{\psi}(w_{1}),\bar{\psi}(w_{2}),\dots,\bar{\psi}(w_{n})\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
from
\bar{\psi}(w_{0})=\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}
to
\bar{\psi}(w_{n})=\emptyset
. Without loss of generality, we may (and do) assume that
\bar{\psi}(w_{k})\neq\emptyset
for any
k<n
. (If we did have
\bar{\psi}(w_{k})=\emptyset
for some
k<n
, then we could just consider the shorter walk
\langle w_{0},w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{k}\rangle
instead of the original walk
\langle w_{0},w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n}\rangle
.) But observe that every walk in
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
from
\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}
to a vertex in
\mathcal{A}
must include the vertex
\emptyset
, because
(\emptyset,a_{\text{out}})
is the only member of
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}
going from a member of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
to a member of
\mathcal{A}
. Because we are assuming
\bar{\psi}(w_{k})\neq\emptyset
for all
k<n
, this implies
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
for all
i\leq n
.

Because
\bar{\phi}
is an epimorphism,
\langle\bar{\phi}(w_{0}),\bar{\phi}(w_{1}),\bar{\phi}(w_{2}),\dots,\bar{\phi}(w_{n})\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
. We now prove, by induction on
i
, that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\bar{\psi}(w_{i})
for all
i\leq n
.
(By the previous paragraph,
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
whenever
i\leq n
. Therefore the assertion
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\bar{\psi}(w_{i})
at least makes sense: we have
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{B}
and
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{B}
whenever
i\leq n
.)

For the base case
i=0
, observe that

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{0})\,=\,\psi(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}})\,=\,\Pi_{\phi}(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}).

Consequently,
\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\in\phi^{-1}(\Pi_{\phi}(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}))
.
Now recall that
\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{0}=\phi^{-1}(a_{m})
forlany)
m\in D
.
Thus
\Pi_{\phi}(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}})=a_{m}
, and
\phi^{-1}(\Pi_{\phi}(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}))=\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}
. Hence
\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\in\phi^{-1}(\Pi_{\phi}(\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}))=\mathrm{S}_{\text{in}}=\bar{\psi}(w_{0})
.

For the successor step, fix
i<n
and suppose (the inductive hypothesis) that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\bar{\psi}(w_{i})
; we wish to show
\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\in\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})
.
Because
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism and
w_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{i+1}
,
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})
.
Because
\bar{\psi}(w_{i}),\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
(rather than
\mathcal{A}
), this means
(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}),\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1}))\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, which is to say that for some (any)
m\in D
there is some
k<k_{m}
such that
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k}
and
\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k+1}
.
But then

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\,=\,\psi(\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k})\,=\,a_{m+k}\text{, and}

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})\,=\,\psi(\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k+1})\,=\,a_{m+k+1}.

In particular,
\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\in\phi^{-1}(a_{m+k+1})
. Furthermore,
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})
(because
w_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{i+1}
and
\bar{\phi}
is an epimorphism), and
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\bar{\psi}(w_{i})=\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k}
(by the inductive hypothesis).
But by Lemma
10.6
,

\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k+1}\,=\,\left\{b\in\phi^{-1}(a_{m+k+1})\colon\exists x\in\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k}\text{ such that }x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}y\right\},

so this means that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\in\mathrm{S}^{\bar{a}_{m}}_{k+1}=\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})
.
This completes the induction.

This shows that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\bar{\psi}(w_{i})
for all
i\leq n
. In particular,
\bar{\phi}(w_{n})\in\bar{\psi}(w_{n})
. But
\bar{\psi}(w_{n})=\emptyset
. Contradiction!
∎

This completes the first stage of our proof of Theorem
10.2
.
The second stage is similar in its broad strokes: either some condition holds that (in a relatively easy way) implies alternative
(1)
of the theorem, or else we must construct a digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
witnessing alternative
(2)
.

Proof of Theorem
10.2
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
be strongly connected digraphs, let
\phi
be an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, and
let
\bar{a}=\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
be a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.

By Theorem
10.8
, if there is no infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\bar{a}
, then
alternative
(2)
from the statement of Theorem
10.8
holds.
But alternative
(2)
from the statement of Theorem
10.8
implies alternative
(2)
from the statement of Theorem
10.2
.
Therefore, if there is no infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\bar{a}
, then alternative
(2)
holds and we are done.
Thus, for the remainder of the proof, we may (and do) assume there is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

For each
\ell,m\in\omega
with
\ell<m
, define a relation
\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
as follows:

\displaystyle(v,w)\in\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad

there is a walk
\langle b_{\ell},b_{\ell+1},\dots,b_{m}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle

from
v
to
w
that projects onto
\langle a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
.

This relation expresses the “walkability” between the members of
\mathcal{B}
, using correctly projecting walks beginning at time
\ell
and ending at time
m
.

Because
\mathcal{B}
is finite, the mapping
\chi:[\omega]^{2}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B})
defined by

\chi(\ell,m)\,=\,\mathcal{R}_{\min\{\ell,m\},\max\{\ell,m\}}

is a finite coloring of
[\omega]^{2}
.
By the infinite version of Ramsey’s Theorem, there is some particular
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
and an infinite
D\subseteq\omega
such that if
\ell,m\in D
and
\ell<m
, then
\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}=\mathcal{R}
.
We may (and do) assume
D
does not contain two consecutive integers.
Henceforth, let
m_{k}
denote the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
member of
D
. Before moving on, let us make some observations concerning this set
D
and this relation
\mathcal{R}
, both of which play an important part in the remainder of the proof.

Observation:

\mathcal{R}
is not the empty relation.

Proof of Observation:
By the assumption at the beginning of the proof, there is an infinite walk
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
. From our definition of
\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}
, it follows that
(b_{\ell},b_{m})\in\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}
for all sufficiently large
\ell,m\in\omega
with
\ell<m
. In particular, because
D
is infinite this is true for some
\ell,m\in D
, and consequently
\mathcal{R}\neq\emptyset
.

\dashv

In what follows, let
\mathrm{T}
denote the (nonempty!) domain of this relation
\mathcal{R}
, i.e.,

\mathrm{T}=\left\{v\in\mathcal{V}\colon(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}\text{ for some }v^{\prime}\right\}
.

Observation:

\mathcal{R}
is a transitive relation.

Proof of Observation:
If
k,\ell,m\in D
with
k<\ell<m
, then

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\mathcal{R}\,=\,\mathcal{R}_{k,m}\,=\,\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}\circ\mathcal{R}_{k,\ell}\,=\,\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{R}.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\dashv

Observation:
There is some
v\in\mathcal{B}
such that
(v,v)\in\mathcal{R}
.

Proof of Observation:

By assumption, there is an infinite walk
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.
There is some
v\in\mathcal{B}
such that
b_{m}=v
for infinitely many
m\in D
.
But clearly
(b_{\ell},b_{m})\in\mathcal{R}
for sufficiently large
\ell<m
with
\ell,m\in D
, so this implies
(v,v)\in\mathcal{R}
.

\dashv

Observation:
If
\ell,m\in D
, then
a_{\ell}=a_{m}
.

Proof of Observation:
Fix some
v\in\mathcal{B}
such that
(v,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, and let
\ell,m\in D
with
\ell<m
. Because
(v,v)\in\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}
, the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{\ell,m}
implies there is a walk
\langle b_{\ell},\dots,b_{m}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
v=b_{\ell}
to
v=b_{m}
that projects onto
\langle a_{\ell},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
. But (by the definition of “projects”) this means
a_{\ell}=\phi(b_{\ell})=\phi(v)
and
a_{m}=\phi(b_{m})=\phi(v)
.

\dashv

Let us write
a_{D}
to denote the one member of
\left\{a_{m}\colon m\in D\right\}
.

Next, we associate to each time
i\in\omega
two relations
\mathcal{P}^{i}
and
\mathcal{F}^{i}
(for
Past
and
Future
) that generalize the walkability relation
\mathcal{R}
.

If
i\in D
, let
\mathcal{P}^{i}=\mathcal{F}^{i}=\mathcal{R}
.
If
i\notin D
and
i>m_{0}=\min D
, then define:

\displaystyle(v,b)\in\mathcal{P}^{i}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad

v\in\mathcal{B}
and for some
m\in D
with
m<i
there is

a walk
\langle b_{m},b_{m+1},\dots,b_{i}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
v=b_{m}

to
b=b_{i}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m},a_{m+1},\dots,a_{i}\rangle
.

\displaystyle\vphantom{f^{f^{f^{f^{f}}}}}(b,v)\in\mathcal{F}^{i}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad

v\in\mathcal{B}
and for some
m\in D
with
m>i
there is

a walk
\langle b_{i},b_{i+1},\dots,b_{m}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b=b_{i}

to
v=b_{m}
that projects onto
\langle a_{i},a_{i+1},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
.

This defines
\mathcal{P}^{i}
and
\mathcal{F}^{i}
for
i\geq m_{0}
. It is not necessary to define
\mathcal{P}^{i}
and
\mathcal{F}^{i}
for
i<m_{0}
, or if desired, one may define them there arbitrarily.

Observation:

\mathcal{F}^{i}=\mathcal{F}^{i}\circ\mathcal{R}
for any
i\geq m_{0}
.

Proof of Observation:
If
i\in D
then this follows from our observation that
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{R}
, so fix
i\notin D
. Suppose
(b,v)\in\mathcal{F}^{i}
and
(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
. The former means that, for some
m\in D
with
m>i
, there is a walk
\langle b_{i},\dots,b_{m}\rangle
from
b=b_{i}
to
v=b_{m}
that projects onto
\langle a_{i},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
; the latter means that, for some
m^{\prime}\in D
with
m^{\prime}>m
, there is a walk
\langle b_{m},\dots,b_{m^{\prime}}\rangle
from
v=b_{m}
to
v^{\prime}=b_{m^{\prime}}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m},\dots,a_{m^{\prime}}\rangle
. Concatenating these two walks gives us a walk
\langle b_{i},\dots,b_{m^{\prime}}\rangle
from
b=b_{i}
to
v^{\prime}=b_{m^{\prime}}
that projects onto
\langle a_{i},\dots,a_{m^{\prime}}\rangle
. But this means
(b,v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{i}
.

\dashv

Observation:
If
i\geq m_{2}
, then
\mathcal{P}^{i}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}^{i}
.

Proof of Observation:
If
i\in D
then this follows from our observation that
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{R}
, so fix
i\notin D
with
i>m_{2}
. Suppose
(v,b)\in\mathcal{F}^{i}
and
(v^{\prime},v)\in\mathcal{R}
.

Because
(v,b)\in\mathcal{F}^{i}
, for some
m\in D
with
m<i
there is a walk
\langle b_{m},\dots,b_{i}\rangle
from
v=b_{m}
to
b=b_{i}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m},\dots,a_{i}\rangle
. We claim that, without loss of generality, we may take
m>m_{0}
. To see this, suppose instead that
m=m_{0}
. Recalling that
i>m_{2}
, the two truncated walks
\langle b_{m_{0}},\dots,b_{m_{1}}\rangle
and
\langle b_{m_{1}},\dots,b_{m_{2}}\rangle
witness, respectively, that
(b_{m_{0}},b_{m_{1}})\in\mathcal{R}
and
(b_{m_{1}},b_{m_{2}})\in\mathcal{R}
. Because
\mathcal{R}
is transitive,
(b_{m_{0}},b_{m_{2}})\in\mathcal{R}
. But this means there is a walk
\langle b^{m_{1}},\dots,b^{m_{2}}\rangle
from
b_{m_{0}}=b^{m_{1}}
to
b_{m_{2}}=b^{m_{2}}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m_{1}},\dots,a_{m_{2}}\rangle
.
Consequently,
\langle b^{m_{1}},\dots,b^{m_{2}}=b_{m_{2}},b_{m_{2}+1},\dots,b_{i}\rangle
is a walk from
b_{m_{0}}=b^{m_{1}}
to
v=b_{i}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m_{1}},\dots,a_{i}\rangle
.

Thus for some
m\in D\setminus\{m_{0}\}
with
m<i
there is a walk
\langle b_{m},\dots,b_{i}\rangle
from
v=b_{m}
to
b=b_{i}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m},\dots,a_{i}\rangle
.
Furthermore, because
(v^{\prime},v)\in\mathcal{R}
and
m>m_{0}
, there is a walk
\langle b_{m_{0}},\dots,b_{m}\rangle
from
v^{\prime}=b_{m_{0}}
to
v=b_{m}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m_{0}},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
.
Concatenating these two walks gives us a walk
\langle b_{m_{0}},\dots,b_{i}\rangle
from
v^{\prime}=b_{m_{0}}
to
b=b_{i}
that projects onto
\langle a_{m_{0}},\dots,a_{i}\rangle
. Thus
(v^{\prime},b)\in\mathcal{P}^{i}
.

\dashv

For notational convenience in what follows, if
v\in\mathcal{B}
then define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{i}_{v}

\displaystyle\,=\,\left\{b\in\mathcal{B}\colon(v,b)\in\mathcal{P}^{i}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}

\displaystyle\,=\,\left\{b\in\mathcal{B}\colon(b,v)\in\mathcal{F}^{i}\right\}.

Thus we may write
b\in\mathcal{P}^{i}_{v}
rather than
(v,b)\in\mathcal{P}^{i}
(this can be read as “
b
has
v
in its past at time
i
”); and we may write
b\in\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}
rather than
(b,v)\in\mathcal{F}^{i}
(which can be read as “
b
has
v
in its future at time
i
”).
By the previous observation, if
b\in\mathcal{P}^{i}_{v}
and
(v^{\prime},v)\in\mathcal{R}
, then
b\in\mathcal{P}^{i}_{v^{\prime}}
(provided
i\geq m_{1}
) and if
b\in\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}
and
(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
, then
b\in\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v^{\prime}}

Observation:
If
i,i^{\prime}\in\omega
and
\mathcal{P}^{i}=\mathcal{P}^{i^{\prime}}
, then
a_{i}=a_{i^{\prime}}
. Similarly, if
i,i^{\prime}\in\omega
and
\mathcal{F}^{i}=\mathcal{F}^{i^{\prime}}
, then
a_{i}=a_{i^{\prime}}
.

Proof of Observation:
By assumption, there is an infinite walk
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
. This implies
\mathcal{P}^{i}
and
\mathcal{P}^{i^{\prime}}
are nonempty. If
(v,b)\in\mathcal{P}^{i}=\mathcal{P}^{i^{\prime}}
, then the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{i}
implies
\phi(b)=a_{i}
, and likewise the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{i^{\prime}}
implies
\phi(b)=a_{i^{\prime}}
.

\dashv

If
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{i}
for some
i\in\omega
, define
\Pi(\mathcal{P})=a_{i}
. Similarly, if
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{i}
for some
i\in\omega
, define
\Pi(\mathcal{F})=a_{i}
.

With these preliminaries in place, we now move on to the main line of argument. Consider the following statement:

(\ddagger)

There is some
v\in\mathrm{T}
with
(v,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, such that for every edge
e
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}
,
there are infinitely many
\ell\in D
such that for some
m\in D
with
m>\ell

there is a walk
\langle b_{\ell},b_{\ell+1},b_{\ell+2},\dots,b_{m}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b_{\ell}=v
to
b_{m}=v
such that this walk projects onto
\langle a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1},a_{\ell+2},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
, and this walk traverses
e
, in the sense that

(b_{i},b_{i+1})=e
for some
i
with
\ell\leq i<m
.

As we shall see in what follows, this statement
(\ddagger)
is a sharp dividing line between alternaties
(1)
and
(2)
in the statement of the theorem: if
(\ddagger)
is true then
(1)
holds, and if not then
(2)
holds.

To see that
(\ddagger)\Rightarrow(1)
, we construct a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
by recursion.
At step
n
of the recursion, we add not just one point to our walk, but a string of points with indices between some
m_{k_{n-1}}
and
m_{k_{n}}
.

Fix an enumeration of the edges of
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
, say
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\ =\{e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{M-1}\}
, and fix some
b\in\mathrm{T}
witnessing
(\ddagger)
.
For the base step of the recursion, let
\ell_{0}=0
and let
\langle b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{m_{0}}\rangle
be any walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
ending at
v=b_{m_{0}}=b_{m_{k_{0}}}
. Some such walk exists because
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected.

At step
n>0
of the recursion, we begin with a walk
\langle b_{0},b_{1},\dots,b_{m_{k_{n-1}}}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
such that
b_{m_{k_{n-1}}}=v
.
Let
j
denote the unique member of
\{0,1,2,\dots,M-1\}
such that
k\equiv j
(mod
M
).

Next, because
b_{m_{k_{n-1}}}=v
,
(\ddagger)
implies
there is some
m_{k_{n}}>m_{m_{k_{n-1}}}
and a walk

\langle b_{m_{k_{n-1}}},b_{m_{k_{n-1}}+1},b_{m_{k_{n-1}}+2},\dots,b_{m_{k_{n}}}\rangle

in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
v=b_{m_{k_{n-1}}}
to
v=b_{m_{k_{n}}}
that projects onto

\langle a_{m_{k_{n-1}}},a_{m_{k_{n-1}}+1},a_{m_{k_{n-1}}+2},\dots,a_{m_{k_{n}}}\rangle
, and such that

e_{j}=(b_{i},b_{i+1})
for some
i
with
m_{k_{n-1}}\leq i<m_{k_{n}}
.
We take this walk to define
b_{i}
when
m_{k_{n-1}}<i\leq m_{k_{n}}
.

We claim that the sequence
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
produced by this recursion is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.

It is clear that
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
.
Furthermore, this walk is diligent because for any given
e_{j}
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}
, if
n\equiv j
(mod
M
) then
(b_{i},b_{i+1})=e_{j}
for some
i
with
m_{\ell_{n-1}}\leq i<m_{k_{n}}
, so that every member of
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}
is equal to
(b_{i},b_{i+1})
for infinitely many
i\in\omega
.
Finally, the construction of this walk guarantees that
\phi(b_{i})=a_{i}
for all
i\geq m_{0}
, and so
\left\langle b_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.

This completes the proof that
(\ddagger)
implies alternative
(1)
from the statement of the theorem.

It remains to show that if
(\ddagger)
fails then alternative
(2)
from the statement of the theorem holds. As in the proof of Theorem
10.8
, this involves the construction of a digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
.

For each
i\in\omega
, let
k(i)
denote the (unique) member of
\omega
with the property that
m_{k(i)}\leq i<m_{k(i)+1}
.
For each
k>0
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{k}

\displaystyle\,=\,\left\{(\mathcal{P}^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i})\colon m_{k}<i<m_{k+1}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k}

\displaystyle\,=\,\left\{\big((\mathcal{P}^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i}),(\mathcal{P}^{i+1},\mathcal{F}^{i+1})\big)\colon m_{k}<i<m_{k+1}-1\right\},

\displaystyle c^{k}_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle\,=\,\big(\mathcal{P}^{m_{k}+1},\mathcal{F}^{m_{k}+1}\big),

\displaystyle c^{k}_{\text{out}}

\displaystyle\,=\,\big(\mathcal{P}^{m_{k+1}-1},\mathcal{F}^{m_{k+1}-1}\big).

In other words,
\langle\mathcal{L}^{k},\mathcal{E}^{k}\rangle
is a digraph recording the “past” and “future” relations between time
m_{k}
and time
m_{k+1}
.

A
4
-tuple
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{E},c_{\text{in}},c_{\text{out}})
is
used infinitely often
if there are infinitely many
k>0
with
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{E},c_{\text{in}},c_{\text{out}})=(\mathcal{L}^{k},\mathcal{E}^{k},c^{k}_{\text{in}},c^{k}_{\text{out}}).

Because there are only finitely many possibilities for the
4
-tuple
(\mathcal{L}^{k},\mathcal{E}^{k},c^{k}_{\text{in}},c^{k}_{\text{out}})
, there is some
K>0
such that
(\mathcal{L}^{k},\mathcal{E}^{k},c^{k}_{\text{in}},c^{k}_{\text{out}})
is used infinitely often for every
k\geq K
.

Let
N
denote the number of
4
-tuples that are used infinitely often, and fix indices

k_{0},k_{1},\dots,k_{N}
such that

(\mathcal{L}^{k_{0}},\mathcal{E}^{k_{0}},c^{k_{0}}_{\text{in}}c^{k_{0}}_{\text{out}}),\dots,(\mathcal{L}^{k_{N}},\mathcal{E}^{k_{N}},c^{k_{N}}_{\text{in}},c^{k_{N}}_{\text{out}})

is a complete list of all the
4
-tuples used infinitely often.
For each
j\leq N
let

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{j}

\displaystyle\,=\,\{j\}\times\mathcal{L}^{k_{j}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{j}

\displaystyle\,=\,\left\{((j,t),(j,t^{\prime}))\colon(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}^{k_{j}}\right\},

\displaystyle c^{j}_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle\,=\,\big(j,c^{k_{j}}_{\text{in}}\big),

\displaystyle c^{j}_{\text{out}}

\displaystyle\,=\,\big(j,c^{k_{j}}_{\text{out}}\big).

In other words, these are the digraphs described above, only we have modified the vertex sets of the
\mathcal{L}^{k_{j}}
so that all the different
\mathcal{C}_{j}
’s are disjoint.
We assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathrm{T}\notin\bigcup_{j\leq N}\mathcal{C}_{j}
. (This is true automatically if the vertices of
\mathcal{B}
are labelled in a sensible manner.)

We are now ready to define the digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that will witness alternative
(2)
from the statement of the theorem. First, let

\mathcal{C}\,=\,\{\mathrm{T}\}\cup\mathcal{C}_{0}\cup\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathcal{C}_{N}.

Then define the edge relation on
\mathcal{C}
as follows:

\displaystyle x\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}y\quad\ \Longleftrightarrow\quad\

\displaystyle x,y\in\mathcal{C}_{j}\text{ and }(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}_{j}\text{ for some $j\leq N$, or}

\displaystyle x=\mathrm{T}\text{ and }y=c^{j}_{\text{in}}\text{ for some $j\leq N$, or}

\displaystyle x=c^{j}_{\mathrm{out}}\text{ and }y=\mathrm{T}\text{ for some $j\leq N$}.

\bullet

\mathrm{T}

\bullet
\bullet

\langle\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}\rangle

\bullet
\bullet

\langle\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{1}\rangle

\bullet
\bullet

\langle\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{2}\rangle

\bullet
\bullet

\langle\mathcal{C}_{3},\mathcal{E}_{3}\rangle

\dots

Define
\psi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}
by setting
\psi(\mathrm{T})=a_{D}
, and
\psi(j,(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{F}))=\Pi(\mathcal{P})=\Pi(\mathcal{F})
. (To see that this last equality is true, observe that if
(j,(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{F}))\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
, then
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{F})=(\mathcal{P}^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i})
for some
i
, and this implies
\Pi(\mathcal{P}_{i})=\Pi(\mathcal{F}_{i})=a_{i}
.)

This completes the definition of
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\psi
.
To finish the proof, we need to show that
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, that
\psi
is an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, that there is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
projecting onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
via
\psi
, and that
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
.

Like in the proof of Theorem
10.8
, we can verify the first three of these four things all at once via Lemma
10.7
. To do this, we must define a diligent walk
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
via
\psi
.
Recall that
K
was chosen sufficiently large so that if
k\geq K
then
(\mathcal{L}^{k},\mathcal{E}^{k},c^{k}_{\text{in}},c^{k}_{\text{out}})
is used infinitely often.

Given
i\geq m_{0}
, let
k(i)
be the (unique) index with
m_{k(i)}\leq n<m_{k(i)+1}
. Furthermore, if
i\geq m_{K}
then define
j(i)
to be the (unique) index
\leq\!N
with

\big(\mathcal{L}^{k(i)},\mathcal{E}^{k(i)},c^{k(i)}_{\text{in}},c^{k(i)}_{\text{out}}\big)\,=\,\big(\mathcal{L}^{k_{j(i)}},\mathcal{E}^{k_{j(i)}},c^{k_{j(i)}}_{\text{in}},c^{k_{j(i)}}_{\text{out}}\big).

For all
i\geq m_{K}
, define

c_{i}\,=\,\begin{cases}\mathrm{T}&\text{ if }i\in D,\\
\big(j(i),(\mathcal{P}^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i})\big)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Let us check that this sequence is a walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
.
For the first case, suppose
i,i+1\notin D
.
Then
j(i)=j(i+1)
, and we have
c_{i},c_{i+1}\in\mathcal{C}_{j(i)}
and

(c_{i},c_{i+1})\in\mathcal{E}_{j(i)}
by the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{j(i)}
. Hence
c_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}c_{i+1}
in this case.
For the second case, suppose
i\in D
but
i+1\notin D
.
Then
i=m_{k(i)}=m_{k(i+1)}
and
i+1=m_{k(i)}+1
, which means

c_{i+1}\,=\,\big(j(i+1),(\mathcal{P}^{m_{k(i)}+1},\mathcal{F}^{m_{k(i)}+1})\big)\,=\,c^{j(i+1)}_{\text{in}},

while
c_{n}=\mathrm{T}
.
Thus
c_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}c_{i+1}
in this case also.
For the third case, suppose
i\notin D
but
i+1\in D
.
Then
i+1=m_{k(i+1)}
and
k(i)=k(i+1)-1
, which implies (like in the previous case) that

c_{i}=c^{j(i)}_{\text{out}},

while
c_{i+1}=\mathrm{T}
.
Thus
c_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}c_{i+1}
in this case also.
These three cases are exhaustive, by our assumption near the beginning of the proof that
D
does not contain two consecutive integers.
Hence
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\geq m_{K}\right\rangle
is a walk.

To see that this walk is diligent, fix some edge
(c,c^{\prime})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}
. There is some
j\leq N
such that either
(1)

(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}_{j}
, or
(2)

c=\mathrm{T}
and
c^{\prime}=c^{j}_{\text{in}}
, or
(3)

c=c^{j}_{\text{out}}
and
c^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}
.
Because

\big(\mathcal{L}^{k_{j}},\mathcal{E}^{k_{j}},c^{k_{j}}_{\text{in}}c^{k_{j}}_{\text{out}}\big)

is used infinitely often, there are arbitrarily large values of
k\in\omega
such that
j(m_{k})=j
.
But then there is some
i
with
k(i)=k
and
m_{k(i)}\leq i\leq m_{k(i)+1}
such that
(c_{i},c_{i+1})=(c,c^{\prime})
. Thus
(c,c^{\prime})=(c_{i},c_{i+1})
for arbitrarily large values of
i
, and this shows that
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\geq m_{K}\right\rangle
is a diligent walk.

Furthermore, if
i\in D
, then
a_{i}=a_{D}=\psi(c_{i})=a_{D}
; and if
i\notin D
, then

\Pi_{\phi}(c_{i})=\psi\big(j(i),(\mathcal{P}^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i})\big)=\Pi(\mathcal{P}^{i})=\Pi(\mathcal{F}^{i})=a_{i}.

Therefore this walk projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\geq m_{K}\right\rangle
.
Because
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
, the tail sequence
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\geq m_{K}\right\rangle
is also a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.
We have just showed that
\left\langle c_{i}\colon i\geq m_{K}\right\rangle
is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto this diligent walk.
By Lemma
10.7
,
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, and
\psi
is an epimorphism from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
.

Now that we know
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, we can use this fact to find a length-
m_{K}
walk
\langle c^{0},c^{1},\dots,c^{m_{K}}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
such that
c^{m_{K}}=c_{m_{K}}=\mathrm{T}
.
And then

\langle c^{0},c^{1},\dots,c^{m_{K}}=c_{m_{K}},c_{m_{K}+1},c_{m_{K}+2},\dots\rangle

is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects onto
\left\langle a_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\rangle
.

It remains to show that the failure of
(\ddagger)
implies
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
.
We prove this by proving the contrapositive: if
\psi
and
\phi
are compatible, then
(\ddagger)
holds.

To this end, let us suppose that
\psi
is compatible with
\phi
. That is, suppose there is a strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
and epimorphisms
\bar{\phi}
and
\bar{\psi}
from
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
, respectively, such that
\phi\circ\bar{\phi}=\psi\circ\bar{\psi}
.

\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
\psi
\phi
\bar{\psi}
\bar{\phi}

For each
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
v\in\mathcal{B}
, define

\mathcal{P}_{v}(c)\,=\,\begin{cases}\left\{b\in\mathcal{B}\colon(v,b)\in\mathcal{R}\right\}&\text{ if }c=\mathrm{T},\\
\,\mathcal{P}^{i}_{v}&\text{ if $c=(j(i),(\mathcal{P}^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i}))$ for some $i\notin D$.}\end{cases}

\mathcal{F}_{v}(c)\,=\,\begin{cases}\left\{b\in\mathcal{B}\colon(b,v)\in\mathcal{R}\right\}&\text{ if }c=\mathrm{T},\\
\,\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}&\text{ if $c=(j(i),(\mathcal{P}^{i},\mathcal{F}^{i}))$ for some $i\notin D$.}\end{cases}

Claim:
Suppose
\langle w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell}\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
with
\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell})=\mathrm{T}
, and let
v=\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})
. Then
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))
for all
i<\ell
.

Proof of Claim:

This claim is proved by a backwards inductive argument: we take
i=\ell-1
as the base case, and prove for the inductive step that if
0<i<\ell
and
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))
, then
\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1}))
.

For the base case
i=\ell-1
, recall that we are assuming
\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell})=\mathrm{T}
. Thus

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell})\,=\,\psi(\mathrm{T})\,=\,a_{D},

and therefore
\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})\in\phi^{-1}(a_{D})
. Because
w_{\ell-1}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{\ell}
and
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism,
\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell-1})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell})
.
Because
\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell})=\mathrm{T}
, this means
\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell-1})=(j,c^{j}_{\text{out}})
for some
j\leq N
, which means
\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell-1})=(j,(\mathcal{P}^{m_{k}-1},\mathcal{F}^{m_{k}-1})
for some
k\geq K
.
Furthermore,
\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell-1})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})
, because
w_{\ell-1}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{\ell}
and
\bar{\phi}
is an epimorphism.
Thus
\langle\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell-1}),\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell-1})
to
\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})=v
, and this walk projects onto
\langle a_{m_{k}-1},a_{m_{k}}\rangle
because

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell})\,=\,\psi(\mathrm{T})\,=\,a_{D}\,=\,a_{m_{k}}\text{ and}\,
