\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell-1})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell-1})\,=\,\Pi(\mathcal{F}^{m_{k}-1})\,=\,a_{m_{k}-1}.

Consequently, by the definition of
\mathcal{F}
,
\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}^{m_{k}-1}=\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell-1}))
.

For the inductive step, fix
i
with
0<i<\ell
and suppose, as an inductive hypothesis, that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))
.
We consider two cases.
For the first case, suppose
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})=\mathrm{T}
. In this case, the inductive hypothesis states that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\mathrm{T})
. This means that
(\bar{\phi}(w_{i}),v)\in\mathcal{R}
, which implies in particular that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathrm{T}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(a_{D})
.
Once this is known, we may reason exactly as in the previous paragraph (replacing each instance of
\ell
with an
i
) and deduce that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\in\mathcal{F}_{\bar{\phi}(w_{i})}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1}))
.
Because
(\bar{\phi}(w_{i}),v)\in\mathcal{R}
, this implies (via the second observation following the definition of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{F}
and the comments that follow) that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1}))
.

For the second case, suppose
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\neq\mathrm{T}
.
Because
w_{i-1}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{i}
and
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism,

\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\bar{\psi}(w_{i})
.
By our definition of
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
, this means there is some
j\leq N
and some
p
with
m_{k_{j}}<p<m_{k_{j}+1}
such that

\mathcal{F}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))=\mathcal{F}^{p}
and
\mathcal{F}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1}))=\mathcal{F}^{p-1}
.
(The fact that
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\neq\mathrm{T}
rules out the possibility that
p\in D
.)
By definition,
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))=\mathcal{F}_{v}^{p}
means there is a walk
\langle b_{p},b_{p+1},\dots,\dots,b_{m^{\prime}}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
, for some
m^{\prime}\in D
with
m^{\prime}\geq m_{k_{j+1}}
, that goes from
b_{p}=\bar{\phi}(w_{i})
to
v=b_{m^{\prime}}
and projects onto
\langle a_{p},a_{p+1},\dots,a_{m^{\prime}}\rangle
.
Furthermore,

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\,=\,\Pi(\mathcal{F}^{p})\,=\,a_{p}\text{, and}\,

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1})\,=\,\Pi(\mathcal{F}^{p-1})\,=\,a_{p-1}.

In particular,
\phi\big(\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\big)=a_{p-1}
, and of course
\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\bar{\phi}(w_{i})
(because
w_{i-1}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{i}
and
\bar{\phi}
is an epimorphism). But then this shows that

\langle\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1}),\bar{\phi}(w_{i})=b_{p},b_{p+1},\dots,b_{m^{\prime}}\rangle

is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})
to
b_{m^{\prime}}=v
, and this walk projects onto
\langle a_{p-1},a_{p},a_{p+1},\dots,a_{m^{\prime}}\rangle
.
Hence
\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}^{p-1}=\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1}))
.

\dashv

Claim:
If
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
\bar{\psi}(w)=\mathrm{T}
, then
\bar{\phi}(w)\in\mathrm{T}
and
(\bar{\phi}(w),\bar{\phi}(w))\in\mathcal{R}
.

Proof of Claim:

Fix
w\in\mathcal{W}
with
\bar{\psi}(w)=\mathrm{T}
. Because
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, there is a walk
\langle w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell}\rangle
with length
\ell>0
from
w=w_{0}
to
w=w_{\ell}
.
Letting
v=\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})
, the previous claim gives
\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{0}))
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{0}))
, this means
(\bar{\phi}(w_{0}),v)\in\mathcal{R}
.
But
\bar{\phi}(w_{0})=\bar{\phi}(w)
and
v=\bar{\phi}(w_{\ell})=\bar{\phi}(w)
, so
(\bar{\phi}(w),\bar{\phi}(w))\in\mathcal{R}
.

\dashv

Claim:
Suppose
\langle w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell}\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
with
\bar{\psi}(w_{0})=\mathrm{T}
, and let
v=\bar{\phi}(w_{0})
. Then
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{P}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))
for all
i>0
.

Proof of Claim:

This claim is the mirror image of the one above concerning
\mathcal{F}
. It is proved in almost the same way, but by a regular induction (starting at
1
, not
0
) rather than a backward induction.

For the base case
i=1
, recall that we are assuming
\bar{\psi}(w_{0})=\mathrm{T}
. Thus

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{0})\,=\,\psi(\mathrm{T})\,=\,a_{D},

and therefore
\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\in\phi^{-1}(a_{D})
.
Because
w_{0}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{1}
and
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism,
\bar{\psi}(w_{0})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\bar{\psi}(w_{1})
.
Because
\bar{\psi}(w_{0})=\mathrm{T}
, this means
\bar{\psi}(w_{1})=(j,c^{j}_{\text{in}})
for some
j\leq N
, which means
\bar{\psi}(w_{1})=(j,(\mathcal{P}^{m_{k}+1},\mathcal{F}^{m_{k}+1})
for some
k\geq K
.
Furthermore,
\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\bar{\phi}(w_{1})
, because
w_{0}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{1}
and
\bar{\phi}
is an epimorphism.
Thus
\langle\bar{\phi}(w_{0}),\bar{\phi}(w_{1})\rangle
is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
v=\bar{\phi}(w_{0})
to
\bar{\phi}(w_{1})
, and this walk projects onto
\langle a_{m_{k}},a_{m_{k}+1}\rangle
because

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{0})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{0})\,=\,\psi(\mathrm{T})\,=\,a_{D}\,=\,a_{m_{k}}\text{ and}\,

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{1})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{1})\,=\,\Pi(\mathcal{F}^{m_{k}+1})\,=\,a_{m_{k}+1}.

Consequently, by the definition of
\mathcal{P}
,
\bar{\phi}(w_{1})\in\mathcal{P}_{v}^{m_{k}+1}=\mathcal{P}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{\ell+1}))
.

For the inductive step, fix
i
with
0<i<\ell
and suppose, as an inductive hypothesis, that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))
.
We consider two cases.
For the first case, suppose
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})=\mathrm{T}
. In this case, the inductive hypothesis states that
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{P}_{v}(\mathrm{T})
. This means
(v,\bar{\phi}(w_{i}))\in\mathcal{R}
, which implies
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathrm{T}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(a_{D})
.
Once this is known, we may reason as in the previous paragraph (replacing each
\ell
with an
i
) and deduce
\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\phi}(w_{i})}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1}))
.
Because
(v,\bar{\phi}(w_{i}))\in\mathcal{R}
, this implies
\bar{\phi}(w_{i-1})\in\mathcal{P}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1}))
.

For the second case, suppose
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\neq\mathrm{T}
.
Because
w_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{i+1}
and
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism,

\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})
.
By our definition of
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
, this means there is some
j\leq N
and some
p
with
m_{k_{j}}<p<m_{k_{j}+1}
such that

\mathcal{P}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))=\mathcal{P}^{p}
and
\mathcal{P}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i-1}))=\mathcal{P}^{p-1}
.
(The fact that
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\neq\mathrm{T}
rules out the possibility that
p\in D
.)
By definition,
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{P}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))=\mathcal{P}_{v}^{p}
means there is a walk
\langle b_{m^{\prime}},b_{m^{\prime}+1},\dots,b_{p}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
, for some
m^{\prime}\in D
with
m^{\prime}\leq m_{k_{j}}
, that goes from
v=b_{m^{\prime}}
to
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})=b_{p}
and projects onto
\langle a_{m^{\prime}},a_{m^{\prime}+1},\dots,a_{p}\rangle
.
Furthermore,

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\,=\,\Pi(\mathcal{P}^{p})\,=\,a_{p}\text{, and}\,

\phi\circ\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\,=\,\psi\circ\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})\,=\,\Pi(\mathcal{P}^{p-1})\,=\,a_{p+1}.

In particular,
\phi\big(\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\big)=a_{p+1}
, and of course
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})
(because
w_{i}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w_{i+1}
and
\bar{\phi}
is an epimorphism). But then this shows that

\langle b_{m^{\prime}},b_{m^{\prime}+1},\dots,b_{p}=\bar{\phi}(w_{i}),\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\rangle

is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b_{m^{\prime}}=v
to
\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})
, and this walk projects onto
\langle a_{m^{\prime}},\dots,a_{p},a_{p+1}\rangle
.
Hence
\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\in\mathcal{P}_{v}^{p+1}=\mathcal{P}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1}))
.

\dashv

With these claims established, we are ready to finish the proof.
Because
\bar{\psi}
is an epimorphism, there is some
w\in\mathcal{W}
such that
\bar{\psi}(w)=\mathrm{T}
.
We claim that
v=\bar{\phi}(w)
witnesses
(\ddagger)
.

From the second of the three claims above,
v\in\mathrm{T}
and
(v,v)\in\mathcal{R}
.

Fix an edge
e=(b,b^{\prime})
in
\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}
.
Because
\bar{\phi}
is an epimorphism, there exist some
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
such that
w\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}w^{\prime}
and
(b,b^{\prime})=(\bar{\phi}(w),\bar{\phi}(w^{\prime}))
.
Using the fact that
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
is strongly connected, there is a walk
\langle w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{W},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{W}}\,$}}\rangle
with
w_{\ell}=w=w_{0}
such that
(w,w^{\prime})=(w_{i},w_{i+1})
for some
i<\ell
.

Using the first and third of our three claims above,
\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{P}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i}))
and
\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\in\mathcal{F}_{v}(\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1}))
.

There is some
j\leq N
such that either
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
or
\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
(or both).
Fix any
k\geq K
with
j(m_{k})=j
.
Because
\bar{\psi}(w_{i})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})
, our definition of
\mathcal{E}_{j}
implies there is some
p
with
m_{k}\leq p<m_{k+1}
such that

\bar{\psi}(w_{i})=(j,(\mathcal{P}^{p},\mathcal{F}^{p}))
and

\bar{\psi}(w_{i+1})=(j,(\mathcal{P}^{p+1},\mathcal{F}^{p+1}))
.
Hence

b\,=\,\bar{\phi}(w_{i})\,\in\,\mathcal{P}^{p}_{v}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad b^{\prime}\,=\,\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})\,\in\,\mathcal{P}^{p+1}_{v}.

By definition, this means

\circ

for some
\ell\in D
with
\ell\leq m_{k}
, there is a walk
\langle b_{\ell},b_{\ell+1},\dots,b_{p}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b_{\ell}=v
to
b_{p}=\bar{\phi}(w_{i})=v
that projects onto
\langle a_{m_{k}},a_{m_{k}+1},\dots,a_{p}\rangle
; and

\circ

for some
m\in D
with
m\geq m_{k+1}
, there is a walk
\langle b_{p+1},b_{p+2},\dots,b_{m}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b_{p+1}=\bar{\phi}(w_{i+1})=v^{\prime}
to
b_{m}=b
that projects onto
\langle a_{p+1},a_{p+2},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
.

But then, concatenating these two walks,

\langle b_{\ell},b_{\ell+1},\dots,b_{p},b_{p+1},b_{p+2},\dots,b_{m}\rangle

is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b
to
b
that projects onto
\langle a_{\ell},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
and has
(b_{p},b_{p+1})=(v,v^{\prime})=e
for some
p
with
\ell\leq p<m
.

This nearly proves that
v
witnesses
(\ddagger)
for
e
, except that we must show it is possible for the parameter
\ell
to be arbitrarily large in such walks.

In the above argument, we took
k\geq K
subject only to the condition that
j(m_{k})=j
. There are infinitely many such
k
, because
(\mathcal{L}^{k_{j}},\mathcal{E}^{k_{j}},c^{k_{j}}_{\text{in}}c^{k_{j}}_{\text{out}})
is used infinitely often. Consequently, the value of
k
in the preceding argument may be taken arbitrarily large. To finish the proof, we show that the value of
\ell
in the preceding argument may be taken to be either
m_{k}
or
m_{k-1}
.

If
\ell=m_{k}
we are done, so suppose
\ell<m_{k}
. Then

\langle b_{\ell},b_{\ell+1},\dots,b_{m_{k}}\rangle
(the truncation of the walk constructed above) is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
that projects onto

\langle a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1},\dots,a_{m_{k}}\rangle
.
Consequently,
(b_{\ell},b_{m_{k}})=(b,b_{m_{k}})\in\mathcal{R}
.
But by our choice of
\mathcal{R}
, and our Ramsey-theoretic preparation at the beginning of the proof, it follows that there is a walk

\langle b^{m_{k-1}},b^{m_{k-1}+1},\dots,b^{m_{k}}\rangle
in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b=b^{m_{k-1}}
to
b_{m_{k}}=b^{m_{k}}
that projects onto

\langle a_{m_{k-1}},\dots,a_{m_{k}}\rangle
. Thus

\langle b^{m_{k-1}},b^{m_{k-1}+1},\dots,b^{m_{k}}=b_{m_{k}},\dots,b_{p},b_{p+1},b_{p+2},\dots,b_{m}\rangle

is a walk in
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle
from
b
to
b
that projects onto
\langle a_{m_{k-1}},\dots,a_{m}\rangle
and has
(b_{p},b_{p+1})=(v,v^{\prime})=e
for some
p
with
m_{k-1}\leq p<m
.
Therefore
b
witnesses
(\ddagger)
for
e
. Because
e
was arbitrary,
b
witnesses
(\ddagger)
, as claimed.
∎

11.
The Lifting Lemma, part 6: completing the proof

In this section we piece together the results from the preceding few sections to obtain a proof of the Lifting Lemma.

Lemma 11.1
.

For every partition
\mathcal{A}
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, there exists a partition
\left\{X_{a}\colon a\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
of
\omega
into infinite sets such that
[X_{a}]=a
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and for every
n\in\omega
, if
n\in X_{a}
and
n+1\in X_{a^{\prime}}
then
a\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}a^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Consider the special case of Lemma
8.5
with
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle=\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
and with the isomorphism
\phi
taken to be the identity map. The lemma asserts there is an infinite walk
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
such that the natural map associated to this walk is the identity: that is,
a=[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}=a\right\}]
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
Thus taking
X_{a}=\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}=a\right\}
works.
∎

Theorem 11.2
.

\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is polarized.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and let
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
be a virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.

Applying the previous lemma, there is a partition
\left\{X_{a}\colon a\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
of
\omega
into infinite sets such that
[X_{a}]=a
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and for every
n\in\omega
, if
n\in X_{a}
and
n+1\in X_{a^{\prime}}
then
a\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}a^{\prime}
. As in the proof of the previous lemma, there is an infinite walk through
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
associated to this partition: for every
n\in\omega
, let
X(n)
denote the unique member of
\left\{X_{a}\colon a\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
containing
n
, and then let
a_{n}=[X(n)]
.

Applying Theorem
10.2
with
\langle\mathcal{B},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{B}}\,$}}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
, either

(1)

there is a diligent walk through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
that almost projects (via
\phi
) onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
, or

(2)

there is a strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
and an epimorphism
\psi
from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
such that
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
, and there is a diligent walk
\left\langle c_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects (via
\psi
) onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.

First, suppose case
(1)
holds. Fix some infinite walk
\left\langle v_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
such that
\phi(v_{n})=a_{n}
for all but finitely many
n\in\omega
. As in Definition
8.1
, let
A_{v}=[\left\{n\in\omega\colon v_{n}=v\right\}]
for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
. Let

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\left\{A_{v}\colon v\in\mathcal{V}\right\}.

We claim that this partition of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
realizes
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
.
First, note that because
\phi(v_{n})=a_{n}
for all but finitely many
n\in\omega
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigvee\left\{A_{v}\colon\phi(v)=a\right\}

\displaystyle\textstyle\,=\,[\bigcup_{v\in\phi^{-1}(a)}\left\{n\in\omega\colon v_{n}=v\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon\phi(v_{n})=a\right\}]\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}=a\right\}]=a

for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
refines
\mathcal{A}
. For each
v\in\mathcal{V}
, let
\phi^{\prime}(v)=A_{v}
. By Lemma
8.3
,
\phi^{\prime}
is an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
(the natural isomorphism). If
v\in\mathcal{V}
and
\phi(v)=a
, then

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(v)\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon v_{n}=v\right\}]

\displaystyle\,\leq\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon\phi(v_{n})=a\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}=a\right\}]=a.

Recall that the natural map
\pi:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
sends
\phi(v)
to
a
whenever
\phi(v)\leq a
. The foregoing computation shows
\pi\circ\phi^{\prime}(v)=\phi(v)
. As
v\in\mathcal{V}
was arbitrary,
\pi\circ\phi^{\prime}=\phi
. Thus
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
realizes the virtual extension
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
, and so alternative
(1)
of Definition
9.6
holds.

Next, suppose case
(2)
holds in the Theorem
10.2
dichotomy.
Fix a strongly connected digraph
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
and an epimorphism
\psi
from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
onto
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
such that
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
, and there is an infinite walk
\left\langle c_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
through
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
that almost projects (via
\psi
) onto
\left\langle a_{n}\colon n\in\omega\right\rangle
.
Like in the previous paragraph, let
A_{c}=[\left\{n\in\omega\colon c_{n}=c\right\}]
for each
c\in\mathcal{C}
, and let

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\left\{A_{c}\colon c\in\mathcal{C}\right\}.

Because
\psi(c_{n})=a_{n}
for all sufficiently large
n\in\omega
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigvee\left\{A_{c}\colon\psi(c)=a\right\}

\displaystyle\textstyle\,=\,[\bigcup_{c\in\psi^{-1}(a)}\left\{n\in\omega\colon c_{n}=c\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon\psi(c_{n})=a\right\}]\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}=a\right\}]=a

for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
refines
\mathcal{A}
. For each
c\in\mathcal{C}
, let
\psi^{\prime}(c)=A_{c}
, and note that
\psi^{\prime}
is an isomorphism from
\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle
to
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
by Lemma
8.3
. If
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
\phi(c)=a
, then (as above)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(c)\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon c_{n}=c\right\}]

\displaystyle\,\leq\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon\phi(c_{n})=a\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n\in\omega\colon a_{n}=a\right\}]=a.

Thus the natural map
\pi:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
sends
\psi^{\prime}(c)
to
a
for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
Hence
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
realizes the virtual extension
(\psi,\langle\mathcal{C},\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.75}{$\,{}_{\mathcal{C}}\,$}}\rangle)
.
Because
\psi
is incompatible with
\phi
, this implies alternative
(2)
of Definition
9.6
holds.

Thus the two alternatives provided by Theorem
10.2
correspond exactly to the two cases in Definition
9.6
. Consequently,
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
is polarized.
But
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
was an arbitrary virtual partition of an arbitrary partition of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, so this shows that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is polarized.
∎

Theorem 11.3
.

If
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, then
\langle\mathcal{A},\sigma\rangle
is polarized.

Proof.

The proof strategy can be summarized in a sentence: This follows from the previous theorem, because polarization descends to elementary substructures. However, it is not necessarily obvious that this property descends to elementary substructures, because polarization does not seem to be expressible in a first-order sentence. It suffices, though, to express the existence of witnesses to either of the two alternatives in Definition
9.6
, so this is what we do.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
be an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\mathbb{A}
, and let
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
be a virtual refinement of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
.
By the previous theorem,
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
is polarized in the larger dynamical system
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. Thus one of two things happens:

(1)

there is a partition
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{A}
that realizes
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
, or

(2)

there is a partition
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{A}
refining
\mathcal{A}
such that
(\pi,\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
is incompatible with
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
, where
\pi
denotes the natural map from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
onto
\mathcal{A}
.

First suppose case
(1)
holds.
Fix an enumeration
\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{m}\}
of
\mathcal{A}
and an enumeration
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\{a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Like in the proof of Lemma
8.9
, there is a first-order sentence
\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime},\sigma)
about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
that records precisely the structure of the digraphs
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
, and also records which members of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
are below which members of
\mathcal{A}
(i.e., it records the action of the natural map
\pi:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
).
Let
\bar{\varphi}
be the formula

\exists x_{1}\exists x_{2}\dots\exists x_{n}\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\sigma).

This is a true assertion about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, witnessed by setting
x_{i}=a^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i\leq n
.
By elementarity,
\bar{\varphi}
must also be a true assertion about
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
. That is,
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle\models\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{n},\sigma)
for some
\bar{a}_{1},\bar{a}_{2},\dots,\bar{a}_{n}\in\mathbb{A}
.
Because of our choice of
\varphi
, this means that
\bar{\mathcal{A}}=\{\bar{a}_{1},\bar{a}_{2},\dots,\bar{a}_{n}\}
is a partition of
\mathbb{A}
refining
\mathcal{A}
and
(\pi,\langle\bar{\mathcal{A}},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
is identical to
(\pi,\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
, where in each case
\pi
denotes the natural map.
Consequently,
(\pi,\langle\bar{\mathcal{A}},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
realizes
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
, because
(\pi,\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
does.
Thus the first case of Definition
9.6
holds for
\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle
.

Next suppose case
(2)
holds.
As in case
(1)
, fix an enumeration
\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{m}\}
of
\mathcal{A}
and an enumeration
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\{a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, and fix a first-order sentence
\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime},\sigma)
about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
that records precisely the structure of
\langle\mathcal{A},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle
, and the action of the natural map
\pi:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
.
Let
\bar{\varphi}
be the formula

\exists x_{1}\exists x_{2}\dots\exists x_{n}\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\sigma).

This is a true assertion about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, so by elementarity,
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle\models\varphi(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{n},\sigma)
for some
\bar{a}_{1},\bar{a}_{2},\dots,\bar{a}_{n}\in\mathbb{A}
.
By our choice of
\varphi
, this means that
\bar{\mathcal{A}}=\{\bar{a}_{1},\bar{a}_{2},\dots,\bar{a}_{n}\}
is a partition of
\mathbb{A}
refining
\mathcal{A}
and
(\pi,\langle\bar{\mathcal{A}},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
is identical to
(\pi,\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
, where in each case
\pi
denotes the natural map.
Consequently,
(\pi,\langle\bar{\mathcal{A}},\xrightarrow{\text{\tiny$\,\sigma\,$}}\rangle)
is incompatible with
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
, and the second case of Definition
9.6
holds for
(\phi,\langle\mathcal{V},\to\rangle)
.

Thus the two cases listed at the beginning of the proof correspond exactly to the two cases in Definition
9.6
, so
\mathcal{A}
is polarized in
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
.
∎

At this point the Lifting Lemma is essentially proved: we need only to piece together some of the things we have done.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Let
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
be an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and suppose that the image of
\eta
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
In other words,
\eta
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
onto
\langle\eta[A],\sigma\rangle
.
The previous theorem implies that
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
is polarized, because this property is preserved by conjugacies.
Consequently,
(\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle,\iota,\eta)
has a solution by Theorem
9.7
.
∎

Recall that, in Section 6, we showed already that the main theorem of the paper, Theorem
6.4
, follows from the Lifting Lemma. Thus this proof of the Lifting Lemma concludes the proof of the paper’s main result.

12.
A few corollaries

In this final section we deduce a few further results about
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Some of these results are direct consequences of the main theorem, while some others are deduced from the Lifting Lemma instead.

Two dynamical systems
\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle
are
elementarily equivalent
if they satisfy the same sentences of first-order logic in the language of dynamical systems: i.e., if
\varphi(\tau)
is a parameter-free sentence in the language of dynamical systems, then

\langle\mathbb{A},\alpha\rangle\models\varphi(\alpha)
if and only if
\langle\mathbb{B},\beta\rangle\models\varphi(\beta)
.

Theorem 12.1
.

The structures
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are elementarily equivalent.

Proof.

We provide two proofs of this theorem. The first proof uses the technique of forcing, and is in some sense “slicker” because it gives the present theorem as a nearly immediate immediate consequence of Theorem
6.4
. The second proof is forcing-free, and more direct. It gives the theorem not as a consequence of Theorem
6.4
, but rather as a consequence of the technique used in its proof.

For the first proof, let
\mathbb{P}
denote the poset of countable partial functions
\omega_{1}\times\omega\to\omega
, ordered by extension, i.e., the usual poset for forcing
\mathsf{CH}
with countable conditions. Let
G
be a
V
-generic filter on
\mathbb{P}
.
Because
\mathbb{P}
is a countably closed notion of forcing, no new subsets of
\omega
are added by
\mathbb{P}
, which means that
V\cap\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}=V[G]\cap\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. Furthermore, because
\sigma
is definable by the simple formula
\sigma([A])=[A+1]
, the action of
\sigma
is the same in
V
and in
V[G]
. Thus the structures
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle^{V}
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle^{V[G]}
are one in the same.
Thus (by the “absoluteness of the satisfaction relation”) the theory of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is the same in
V
and in
V[G]
.
The same argument applies to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, so the theory of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is also the same in
V
and in
V[G]
.
Because
V[G]\models\mathsf{CH}
, Theorem
6.4
implies
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate in
V[G]
.
In particular,
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are elementarily equivalent in
V[G]
.
To summarize: the theory of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is the same in
V
as in
V[G]
, the theory of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is the same in
V
as in
V[G]
, and in
V[G]
these two structures have the same theory. Consequently, they have the same theory in
V
.

For the second proof, let us take a closer look at the proof of Theorem
6.4
earlier in the paper.
In that proof, we construct a coherent sequence

\left\langle\phi_{\alpha}\colon\alpha<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of maps, where each
\phi_{\alpha}
is a conjugacy from

\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, where

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}
are subalgebras of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.
In fact, each
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
had the property that

\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
is a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and
each
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}
had the property that there is some subalgebra
\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1}
with
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1}\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}
such that

\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.

Note that the existence of these
\phi_{\alpha}
’s and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
’s,
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}
’s and
\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1}
’s does not depend on
\mathsf{CH}
: the construction of the sequence can be carried out in
\mathsf{ZFC}
. The assumption of
\mathsf{CH}
was used only to arrange that
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\beta<\omega_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}=\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.

Without
\mathsf{CH}
, we still get a map
\phi_{\omega}:\mathbb{A}_{\omega}\to\mathbb{B}_{\omega}
.
This map is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\omega},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\omega},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
.
Now recall that an increasing union of elementary substructures is also an elementary substructure (by the Tarski-Vaught criterion). In particular,

\langle\mathbb{A}_{\omega},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\omega},\sigma^{-1}\rangle

are elementary substructures of

\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
, respectively, because
\mathbb{A}_{\omega}=\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathbb{A}_{n}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\omega}=\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathbb{B}_{n+1}^{0}
.
Because
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
have elementary substructures that are conjugate to one another, they are elementarily equivalent.
∎

In fact, one may strengthen the conclusion of this theorem, without much altering the proof, by considering infinitary logic.

Theorem 12.2
.

The structures
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
share the same
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{1}}
-theory in the language of dynamical systems.

As with Theorem
12.1
, this can be proved in two ways. The first, using forcing, works exactly as above, noting that the
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{1}}
-theory of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is unchanged by a countably closed forcing. The second is like the second method of proof for Theorem
12.1
above, but the recursion must be run for
\omega_{1}
steps instead of just
\omega
. Note that this recursion can run for
\omega_{1}
steps without assuming
\mathsf{CH}
; the only difference is that without
\mathsf{CH}
, we cannot have
\mathbb{A}_{\omega_{1}}=\mathbb{B}_{\omega_{1}}=\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
after only
\omega_{1}
steps.

The following theorem stems from the observation that in our proof of Theorem
6.4
, the embedding at the base step of the recursion can be arbitrary. This gives us some freedom to construct not just one conjugacy from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
or from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, but many.

Theorem 12.3
.

Assume
\mathsf{CH}
. Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
be a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and let
\eta
be an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. Then there is a conjugacy
\phi
from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to itself such that
\phi\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}=\eta
.

Proof.

We repeat the proof of Theorem
6.4
with a few minor differences.
As in that proof, if
X\subseteq\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
and
|X|\leq\aleph_{0}
, then
\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle

denotes some countable subalgebra of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
with
X\subseteq\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
such that

\big\langle\hskip-0.56905pt\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle,\sigma\!\!\restriction\!\!\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle X\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle\hskip-0.56905pt\big\rangle

is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Also as in that proof, we write

\langle\mathcal{A},\sigma\rangle
to abbreviate
\langle\mathcal{A},\sigma\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}\rangle
.

Let
\left\langle a_{\xi}\colon\xi<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
be an enumeration of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
in order type
\omega_{1}
.
Using transfinite recursion, we now build a coherent sequence
\left\langle\phi_{\alpha}\colon\alpha<\omega_{1}\right\rangle
of maps such that each
\phi_{\alpha}
is a conjugacy from its domain
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
to its co-domain
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
; furthermore, we shall have
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{A}_{\beta}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{\beta}
whenever
\alpha\leq\beta
, and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{B}_{\xi}
for limit
\alpha
.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
be a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and let
\eta
be an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
For the base case of the recursion, let
\mathbb{A}_{0}=\mathbb{A}
, let
\phi_{0}=\eta
, and let
\mathbb{B}_{0}=\phi_{0}[\mathbb{A}_{0}]
.

The remaining stages of the recursion proceed as in the proof of Theorem
6.4
.
At limit stages there is nothing to do: simply take
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{A}_{\xi}
,
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\mathbb{B}_{\xi}
, and
\phi_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi<\alpha}\phi_{\xi}
.
At successor steps, to obtain
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}
and
\phi_{\alpha+1}
, first let
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\cup\{a_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
and observe that

(\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle,\langle\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle,\iota,\phi_{\alpha}^{-1})

is an instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0}
. Furthermore, the image of
\phi^{-1}_{\alpha}
, namely
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha},\sigma\rangle
, is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
By Lemma
6.2
, there is an embedding
\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1}
from
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Let
\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1}
denote the image of this embedding, so that

\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
, and

(\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1})^{-1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
.

Second, let
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}=\langle\hskip-2.84526pt\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}^{0}\cup\{a_{\alpha}\}\rangle\hskip-2.84526pt\rangle
and observe that

(\langle\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle,\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle,\iota,(\phi_{\alpha}^{0})^{-1})

is another instance of the lifting problem for
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion
\mathbb{A}^{0}_{\alpha+1}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1}
. Furthermore, the image of
(\phi^{0}_{\alpha+1})^{-1}
, namely
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}^{0},\sigma\rangle
, is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
By Lemma
6.2
, there is an embedding
\phi_{\alpha+1}
from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Let
\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1}
denote the image of this embedding, so that

\phi_{\alpha+1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
, and

(\phi_{\alpha+1})^{-1}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\mathbb{B}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{A}_{\alpha+1},\sigma\rangle
.

This completes the recursion. In the end,
\phi=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}\phi_{\alpha}
is a conjugacy from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and clearly
\phi\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}=\phi\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}_{0}=\phi_{0}=\eta
.
∎

We now deduce several consequences of Theorem
12.3
. We begin with two model-theoretic consequences. These two results were obtained in collaboration with Ilijas Farah, and are reproduced here with his permission.

Theorem 12.4
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
is a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. Then every embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary embedding.

Proof.

First let us show that this is true assuming
\mathsf{CH}
. (This will enable us afterward to deduce the general result without
\mathsf{CH}
.)

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
be a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and let
\eta
be an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
By the previous theorem,
\mathsf{CH}
implies there is a conjugacy map
\phi
from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to itself such that
\phi\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}=\eta
.
But conjugacy maps preserve the truth of all sentences in the language of dynamical systems (because they are the isomorphisms of this category). From this and the fact that
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, it follows that
\phi\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}=\eta
is an elementary embedding: for any first-order sentence
\psi
and any
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{A}
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\text{ if and only if }\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},\sigma)

\displaystyle\text{ if and only if }\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(\phi(a_{1}),\dots,\phi(a_{n}),\sigma)

\displaystyle\text{ if and only if }\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(\eta(a_{1}),\dots,\eta(a_{n}),\sigma).

Thus the corollary is true assuming
\mathsf{CH}
. To get rid of this assumption, we use the same forcing trick as in the proof of Theorem
12.1
.
Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
be a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and let
\eta
be an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
As in the proof of Theorem
12.1
, there is a forcing extension
V[G]
such that
V[G]\models\mathsf{CH}
, and such that the theory of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
or
\langle\eta[\mathbb{A}],\sigma\rangle
or
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is the same in
V
and in
V[G]
.
By the previous paragraph,
\eta
is an elementary embedding in
V[G]
. But because the theory of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\eta[\mathbb{A}],\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is the same in
V
and in
V[G]
, this implies
\eta
is an elementary embedding in
V
as well.
∎

Theorem 12.5
.

Every embedding between two elementary substructures of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary embedding.

Proof.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{B},\sigma\rangle
are elementary substructures of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, and suppose
\eta
is an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\mathbb{B},\sigma\rangle
.
Let
\psi
be any first-order sentence in the language of dynamical systems, let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{A}
, and suppose
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},\sigma)
.
Let
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle
be a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
with
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{0}
.
Then
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},\sigma)
, by elementarity.
By the previous theorem,
\eta\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}_{0}
is an elementary embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A}_{0},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Thus
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(\eta(a_{1}),\dots,\eta(a_{n}),\sigma)
.
Because
\langle\mathbb{B},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, this implies

\langle\mathbb{B},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(\eta(a_{1}),\dots,\eta(a_{n}),\sigma)
as well.
Hence
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{n},\sigma)
implies
\langle\mathbb{B},\sigma\rangle\models\psi(\eta(a_{1}),\dots,\eta(a_{n}),\sigma)
; i.e.,
\eta
is an elementary embedding.
∎

Observe that either of our uses of
\sigma
in the theorem (for the domain or the co-domain) can be replaced with
\sigma^{-1}
. Thus, with only superficial modifications to the proofs above, we obtain the following.

Theorem 12.6
.

Every embedding between two elementary substructures of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary embedding;
every embedding of an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
is an elementary embedding;
and every embedding of an elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is an elementary embedding.

The last two statements in this theorem are especially intriguing.
In the proof of Theorem
6.4
, and the analogous proof of Theorem
12.3
, we kept careful track of which substructures were elementary, using elementarity on one side of our back-and-forth construction to obtain mappings back to the other side.
Now, from Theorem
12.6
, we can see that all the substructures used these proofs were elementary substructures, on both sides of the back-and-forth argument.
But we see this only in hindsight, after Theorem
12.3
is established.
There is irony in this, something like a security system, that, when installed, acts as a deterrent and is therefore never used.

This situation suggests the possibility that our main theorem could have a simpler proof, perhaps a proof based on the model-theoretic notion of
\aleph_{1}
-saturation. An earlier draft of this paper claimed erroneously, as a corollary to Theorem
5.1
, that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is not
\aleph_{1}
-saturated. Ilijas Farah found a mistake in the proof of this corollary, and we now can show that in fact the opposite is true. (A proof will appear in forthcoming work.) However, our proof of saturation still relies on the combinatorial arguments in Sections 8 and 10. So while this proof of
\aleph_{1}
-saturation offers further insight into the structure of the shift map, it does not seem to provide an essentially easier path to proving the main theorem of this paper.

We now move on to a question that has been part of the folklore surrounding the shift map for years:
Is there any automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
that commutes with
\sigma
other than the powers of
\sigma
?

We show the answer is yes, and in fact this is a relatively straightforward consequence of Theorem
12.3
.

For the statement and proof of the following lemma, let
E
and
F
denote the following subsets of
\omega
:

E\,=\,\textstyle\bigcup\left\{[2^{k},2^{k+1})\colon k\text{ is even}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad F\,=\,\bigcup\left\{[2^{k},2^{k+1})\colon k\text{ is odd}\right\}.

Lemma 12.7
.

Suppose
\mathbb{A}
is a countable subalgebra of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
that is closed under
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
, and with
[\left\{kn\colon n\in\omega\right\}]\in\mathbb{A}
for all
k\in\omega
, and with
[E],[F]\in\mathbb{A}
.
Then there is an embedding
\eta
of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
such that
\eta\neq\sigma^{n}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbb{A}
is a countably infinite subalgebra of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
that is closed under
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
.
Let
\mathcal{S}=\left\{A\subseteq\omega\colon[A]\in\mathbb{A}\setminus[\emptyset]\right\}
.
Then
\mathcal{S}
is a countable set of infinite subsets of
\omega
.
By a standard diagonalization argument,
\mathcal{S}
is not a
splitting family
; i.e., there is some infinite
D\subseteq\omega
such that for all
A\in\mathcal{S}
either
D\cap A
is finite or
D\setminus A
is finite.

We now define a permutation
h:\omega\to\omega
by permuting some intervals associated to
D
. Specifically, let
d_{0}=0
and let
\left\{d_{i}\colon i\in\omega\right\}
be an increasing enumeration of the set
D+1
. Define
h
by setting

h(i)=\begin{cases}i+d_{2n+1}-d_{2n}&\text{ if }i\in[d_{2n},d_{2n+1})\text{ for some $n$},\\
i-d_{2n+1}+d_{2n}&\text{ if }i\in[d_{2n+1},d_{2n+2})\text{ for some $n$}.\end{cases}

In other words, if we set
I_{n}=[d_{n},d_{n+1})
for all
n
, then
h
is the map that simply swaps the intervals
I_{2n}
and
I_{2n+1}
for all
n
.

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\dots

d_{0}

d_{1}

d_{2}

d_{3}

d_{4}

d_{5}

Define
s:\omega\to\omega
by setting
s(i)=h^{-1}(h(i)+1)
for all
i\in\omega
. Checking a few cases, we see that

s(i)=\begin{cases}i+1&\text{ if }i\in[d_{2n},d_{2n+1}-1)\text{ for some $n$},\\
d_{2n}&\text{ if }i=d_{2n+2}-1\text{ for some $n$},\\
d_{2n+3}&\text{ if }i=d_{2n+1}-1\text{ for some $n$}.\end{cases}

In particular,
s(i)=i+1
for all
i\notin D
, and
s[D]=(D+1)\setminus\{d_{1}\}
.

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\bullet

\dots

As was mentioned in Section
3
, a function
f:\omega\to\omega
naturally induces a function
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, namely
f^{*}([A])=[\left\{f(n)\colon n\in A\right\}]
. Let
h^{*}
denote the function
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by
h
, i.e.,
h^{*}([A])=[\left\{h(n)\colon n\in\omega\right\}]
for all
A\subseteq\omega
. Note that
h^{*}
is an automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, because
h
is a bijection
\omega\to\omega
.

We shall finish the proof of the lemma by showing that
\eta=h^{*}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
is the desired embedding.
Because
\eta
is the restriction to
\mathbb{A}
of an automorphism
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, it is automatically an embedding of Boolean algebras
\mathbb{A}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. We must check that
\eta\circ\sigma(a)=\sigma\circ\eta(a)
for all
a\in\mathbb{A}
, and that
\eta\neq\sigma^{n}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
a\in\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and fix some
A\subseteq\omega
with
a=[A]
. Then, using the fact that
s(n)=h^{-1}(h(n)+1)
for all
n
,

\displaystyle\sigma\circ\eta(a)

\displaystyle\,=\,\sigma([\left\{h(n)\colon n\in A\right\}])\,=\,[\left\{h(n)+1\colon n\in A\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{h(s(n))\colon n\in A\right\}]\,=\,\eta([\left\{s(n)\colon n\in A\right\}]).

If
a\in\mathbb{A}
, then by our choice of
D
either
D\setminus A
is finite or
D\cap A
is finite.
If
D\cap A
is finite, then, using the fact that
s(i)=i+1
when
i\notin D
,

[\left\{s(n)\colon n\in A\right\}]=[\left\{s(n)\colon n\in A\setminus D\right\}]=[\left\{n+1\colon n\in A\setminus D\right\}]=[A+1].

On the other hand, if
D\setminus A
is finite then, using the fact that
s(i)=i+1
when
i\notin D
and the fact that
s[D]=(D+1)\setminus\{d_{1}\}
,

\displaystyle[\left\{s(n)\colon n\in A\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{s(n)\colon n\in A\setminus D\right\}\cup\left\{s(n)\colon n\in D\right\}]

\displaystyle\,=\,[\left\{n+1\colon n\in A\setminus D\right\}\cup(D+1)\setminus\{d_{1}\}]\,=\,[A+1].

In either case, if
a\in\mathbb{A}
then

\sigma\circ\eta(a)=\eta([\left\{s(n)\colon n\in A\right\}])=[A+1]=\eta\circ\sigma(a).

Finally, we must show that
\eta\neq\sigma^{n}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
N\in\omega
with
N>0
, and for each
j\in\{0,1,\dots,N-1\}
let
N\omega+j=\left\{Nk+j\colon k\in\omega\right\}
.
By hypothesis,
\mathbb{A}
contains
[N\omega]
. Furthermore, because
\mathbb{A}
is closed under
\sigma
,
\mathbb{A}
contains
[N\omega+j]=\sigma^{j}([N\omega])
for all
j\in\{0,1,\dots,N-1\}
.
For any such
j
, either
D\setminus(N\omega+j)
is finite or
D\cap(N\omega+j)
is finite.
Because the sets
N\omega+0,N\omega+1,\dots,N\omega+N-1
are disjoint, the first alternative can hold for precisely one of them: so there is some particular
j<N
such that
D\setminus(N\omega+j)
is finite.
If
n
is large enough that all members of
D\setminus(N\omega+j)
are below
d_{2n}
, then

h(i)=i\pm(d_{2n+1}-d_{2n})\equiv_{N}i
whenever
i\in[d_{2n},d_{2n+2})
,
because
d_{2n+1}-d_{2n}
is a multiple of
N
. Hence
h(i)\in N\omega+j
for all sufficiently large
i\in N\omega+j
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
n\neq 0
, then for any
N>n
, we have
N\omega\cap(N\omega+n)=\emptyset
and
\sigma^{n}([N\omega])=[N\omega+n]
. On the other hand,
\eta([N\omega])=[\left\{h(i)\colon i\in N\omega\right\}]
and
[\emptyset]\neq[\left\{h(i)\colon i\in N\omega\right\}]\leq[N\omega]
by the last sentence of the previous paragraph. Thus
\eta([N\omega])\neq[N\omega+n]=\sigma^{n}([N\omega])
.
Because
[N\omega]\in\mathbb{A}
, this means
\eta\neq\sigma^{n}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
.

For the remaining case
n=0
, we must find some
a\in\mathbb{A}
with
\eta(a)\neq a
.
This is where we use the hypothesis that
[E],[F]\in\mathbb{A}
.
Because
\mathbb{A}
is closed under
\sigma^{-1}
, we have
[E-1],[F-1]\in\mathbb{A}
.
By our choice of
D
, either
D\cap(E-1)
is finite or
D\cap(F-1)
is finite; equivalently,
either
(D+1)\cap E
is finite or
(D+1)\cap F
is finite.

Suppose
(D+1)\cap E
is finite.
Fix
m
large enough that
d_{n}\in F
for all
n\geq m
.
There are infinitely many
n\geq m
such that

[d_{n},d_{n+1}]\not\subseteq E
(i.e.,
d_{n}
is the last member of
D+1
in some interval of
E
, and the next member of
D+1
is in the next interval of
E
).
In particular, there is some odd
k
such that
[2^{k},2^{k+1})\subseteq(d_{n},d_{n+1})
.

If
n
is odd, then
h
shifts this interval downward by
\ell=d_{n}-d_{n-1}>0
, so that
h[E]
is disjoint from the interval
[2^{k}-\ell,2^{k+1}-\ell)
. Because
E
is not disjoint from any intervals of this length beginning below
2^{k}
, this means there is some
i>d_{n-1}
with
i\in E\setminus h[E]
.

If
n
is even, then
h
shifts the interval upward instead, by
\ell=d_{n+1}-d_{n}>0
, so that
h[E]
is disjoint from the interval
[2^{k}+\ell,2^{k+1}+\ell)
, but also
2^{k}+\ell\in h[E]
and
2^{k+1}+\ell\in h[E]
. In other words,
h[E]
contains a gap of length precisely
2^{k}
beginning above
2^{k}
. Because every gap in
E
beginning above
2^{k}
has length
>\!2^{k}
, this means there is some
i>d_{n}
with
i\in h[E]\setminus E
.

Either way, this shows there are arbitrarily large values of
i
in either
E\setminus h[E]
or
h[E]\setminus E
. Therefore
[E]\neq[\left\{h(i)\colon i\in E\right\}]=\eta([E])
, and this means that
\eta
is not the identity map on
\mathbb{A}
.

A similar argument, interchanging the roles of
E
and
F
, shows that if
(D+1)\cap F
is finite then
[F]\neq\eta([F])
.
∎

Theorem 12.8
.

Assuming
\mathsf{CH}
, there is an automorphism
\phi
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that
\phi\circ\sigma=\sigma\circ\phi
, but
\phi\neq\sigma^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
be a countable elementary substructure of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
such that
[E],[F]\in\mathbb{A}
.
Applying the previous lemma, let
\eta
be an embedding of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
into
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
such that
\eta\neq\sigma^{n}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By Theorem
12.3
, there is a conjugacy map
\phi
from
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
to itself such that
\phi\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}=\eta
.
Thus
\phi\circ\sigma=\sigma\circ\phi
, but
\phi\neq\sigma^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, because
\phi\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}=\eta\neq\sigma^{n}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Let us note that there are several possible ways of proving Lemma
12.7
. We have chosen the shortest we could find. But another worth mentioning is that, by constructing appropriate sequences to induce the embeddings as in Section 4, one can show that for any countable elementary substructure
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
of
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, there are
2^{\aleph_{0}}
distinct embeddings of
\langle\mathbb{A},\sigma\rangle
in
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
. A simple counting argument then implies that most of these embeddings are not equal to
\sigma^{n}\!\restriction\!\mathbb{A}
for any
n
.

Next we address another folklore question:
Can a trivial automorphism be conjugate to a nontrivial one?

We show the answer is again yes, this time as a relatively straightforward consequence of Theorem
12.3
. The following lemma was suggested by Ilijas Farah.

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
\sigma
denote the automorphism
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}\to\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by the shift map
n\mapsto n+1
on
\mathbb{Z}
. There is no trivial automorphism
\tau
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that
\tau\circ\tau=\sigma
.

Proof.

Aiming for a contradiction, suppose there is a map
t:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
that induces an automorphism
\tau
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
with
\tau\circ\tau=\sigma
, in the sense that
\tau([A])=[t(A)]
for all
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
.

There is some finite
F\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
such that
f\!\restriction\!(\mathbb{Z}\setminus F)
is injective, since otherwise
t
would not induce an automorphism in the manner described in the previous paragraph.
Similarly, there is some finite
G\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
such that
t\circ t(n)=n+1
for all
n\notin G
. (Otherwise there one can find an infinite
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
such that
t\circ t(A)\cap(A+1)=\emptyset
, which means
\tau\circ\tau([A])\wedge[A]=[\emptyset]
and in particular, because
[A]\neq[\emptyset]
,
\tau\circ\tau([A])\neq[A]
.)

Let
n>\max(F\cup G\cup t(F)\cup t^{-1}(F)\cup t^{-1}(G))
. Observe that
t(n)>\max G
, because
n>\max(t^{-1}(G))
.

We claim that
t^{2k}(n)=n+k
for all
k\geq 0
. This is proved by a simple induction on
k
: the base case is trivial, and assuming it is true for
k
, then
t^{2k}(n)=n+k>n>\max G
, and this implies that
t^{2(k+1)}(n)=t\circ t(t^{2k}(n))=t^{2k}(n)+1=n+k+1
.
By an essentially identical inductive argument, we also have
t^{2k+1}(n)=t^{2k}(t(n))=t(n)+k
for all
k\geq 0
.

If
n<t(n)
, then there is some
k>0
such that
t(n)=n+k=t^{2k}(n)
, and in particular
t(n)=t(t^{2k-1}(n))
.
If
n>t(n)
, then there is some
k>0
such that
n=t(n)+k=t^{2k+1}(n)
, and in particular,
t(t^{-1}(n))=t(t^{2k}(n))
.
However,
n,t^{-1}(n)>\max F
, and
t^{2k}(n)=n+k,t^{2k-1}(n)=t(n)+k-1>\max F
, so this contradicts our choice of
F
.
∎

Theorem 12.10
.

Suppose that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate. Then there is a nontrivial autormorphism
\zeta
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\zeta\rangle
are conjugate. In particular,
\mathsf{CH}
implies
\sigma
is conjugate to a nontrivial automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.

Proof.

Let
t
be a bijection on
\omega
with one
\mathbb{Z}
-like orbit: for concreteness, take
t(0)=1
, otherwise
t(2n)=2n-2
, and
t(2n+1)=2n+3
.
Let
\tau
be the automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by
t
. Then
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\tau\rangle
is conjugate to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
.
Observe that
\sigma^{2}
is the automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
induced by the function
n\mapsto n+2
on
\omega
. This function has two
\mathbb{N}
-like orbits.
Supposing
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{-1}\rangle
are conjugate (e.g., under
\mathsf{CH}
), it is clear that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\tau\rangle
and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma^{2}\rangle
are also conjugate.
Let
\phi
be an automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that
\phi\circ\tau=\sigma^{2}\circ\phi
.
Define
\zeta=\phi^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\phi
. Then

\displaystyle\zeta\circ\zeta

\displaystyle\,=\,\phi^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\phi\circ\phi^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\phi\,=\,\phi^{-1}\circ\sigma^{2}\circ\phi\,=\,\tau.

Because
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\tau\rangle
is conjugate to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, this implies via the previous lemma that
\zeta
is a nontrivial automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
. And
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is conjugate to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\zeta\rangle
, because
\phi\circ\zeta=\sigma\circ\phi
.
∎

These last two theorems can be summarized by saying that under
\mathsf{CH}
, either there is a nontrivial automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
that commutes with
\sigma
, or else there is a nontrivial automorphism that is conjugate to
\sigma
. We end this paper by showing that, intriguingly, at least one of these two things must be true as soon as there are any nontrivial automorphisms at all.

Theorem 12.11
.

If there is a nontrivial automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, then either there is a nontrivial automorphism that commutes with
\sigma
, or else there is a nontrivial automorphism that is conjugate to
\sigma
.

Proof.

Suppose there is a nontrivial automorphism
\phi
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and let
\psi=\phi\circ\sigma\circ\phi^{-1}
. Observe that
\psi
is an automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, and that
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\psi\rangle
is conjugate to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
, with
\phi
as the conjugacy map, because

\phi\circ\sigma\,=\,\phi\circ\sigma\circ\phi^{-1}\circ\phi\,=\,\psi\circ\phi.

If
\psi
is nontrivial then the second alternative of the theorem holds, and we are done.
So suppose
\psi
is trivial. Because
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
is incompressible and
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\psi\rangle
is conjugate to
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\sigma\rangle
by the previous paragraph,
\langle\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}},\psi\rangle
is incompressible. But, up to a bijection on
\omega
, there are only two incompressible trivial automorphisms of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
, namely
\sigma
and
\sigma^{-1}
(see
[
5
, Lemm 5.1]
). More precisely, there is a trivial automorphism
\tau
of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
such that either
\psi=\tau^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\tau
, or else
\psi=\tau^{-1}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\tau
. But then

\phi\circ\sigma\circ\phi^{-1}\,=\,\psi\,=\,\tau^{-1}\circ\sigma^{\pm 1}\circ\tau,

which implies that

\sigma\,=\,\phi^{-1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\sigma^{\pm 1}\circ\tau\circ\phi\,=\,(\tau\circ\phi)^{-1}\circ\sigma^{\pm 1}\circ(\tau\circ\phi),

i.e.,
\sigma
is conjugate to either
\sigma
or
\sigma^{-1}
via
\tau\circ\phi
.
If
\sigma
is conjugate to
\sigma^{-1}
, then Theorem
12.10
implies the second alternative of the theorem.
If not, then observe that the composition of a trivial and nontrivial automorphism is nontrivial, so
\tau\circ\phi
is a nontrivial automorphism commuting with
\sigma
.
∎

We do not know which of these two alternatives, if either, actually follows from the existence of a nontrivial automorphism of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{P}(\omega)}}{{\mathrm{fin}}}
.
